[slide1.xml] Chapter One Introduction to Ethical Hacking  (Threats, Risks & Governance Frameworks)
[slide2.xml] Security vs Usability Security is about finding the balance between security, functionality and usability and still meet today’s challenges.   As you move toward one you lessen the other two. In order to find this balance, you need to know: What the goals of the organization are,  What security is, and  How to measure the threats to security.
[slide3.xml] What are the core principles of Information Security? Confidentiality : Integrity :  Availability :
[slide4.xml] Core principles Confidentiality : Only authorized users can access system and data.   Integrity : Only authorized modifications to system and data, including accidental modification. Availability : Systems must be available when they are supposed to be.
[slide5.xml] Qualitative versus Quantitative Quantitative assessments are more precise and measurable.   They allow for mapping a specific  dollar amount  for a specific risk. Attempts to provide specific measurements and impacts with dollar figures representative of the expected loss. Qualitative assessments are about making educated guesses. They are more flexible and take into account intangible assets and estimated costs. Defines risk in terms of “High, Medium, or Low”. In many cases, these methods are combined. Each method has its advantages and disadvantages.
[slide6.xml] Quantitative ALE = (SLE (AV * EF) * ARO) AV  = Asset Value EF  = The percentage value of an asset after an incident.  Stolen laptop with unencrypted PII, the Exposure Factor is 100%: the Laptop and all the data are gone. SLE  = Single loss expectancy  Product of AV times EF  The difference between the original value and the remaining value of an asset after a single exploit. ARO  = Annualized rate of occurrence An estimate of how often a threat will successfully exploit a vulnerability with in a year. ALE  = Annualized loss expectancy Product of SLE times ARO  Tells us how much to spend on preventing the specified incidents.
[slide7.xml] Quantitative (With numbers) The annualized loss expectancy (ALE) is the product of the annual rate of occurrence (ARO) and the single loss expectancy (SLE).  It is mathematically expressed as: {ALE} = {ARO} times {SLE} Suppose an asset is valued (AV) at $100,000, and the Exposure Factor (EF) for this asset is 25%. The single loss expectancy (SLE) then, is 25% * $100,000, which is $25,000. The annualized loss expectancy is the product of the annual rate of occurrence (ARO) and the single loss expectancy. ALE = ARO * SLE What if the ARO is one, what is the ALE? What if the ARO is three, what is the ALE?
[slide8.xml] Quantitative (With numbers) The annualized loss expectancy (ALE) is the product of the annual rate of occurrence (ARO) and the single loss expectancy (SLE).  It is mathematically expressed as: {ALE} = {ARO} times {SLE} Suppose an asset is valued (AV) at $100,000, and the Exposure Factor (EF) for this asset is 25%. The single loss expectancy (SLE) then, is 25% * $100,000, which is $25,000. The annualized loss expectancy is the product of the annual rate of occurrence (ARO) and the single loss expectancy. ALE = ARO * SLE For an ARO of one, the annualized loss expectancy is 1 * $25,000, or $25,000. For an ARO of three, the equation is: ALE = 3 * $25,000. Therefore: ALE = $75,000
[slide9.xml] Qualitative Risk (Educated Guessing) Likelihood Measured by the capabilities of the threat & the presence or absence of countermeasures. Example of likelihood scale  High = 1, Medium = 0.5, low = 0.1 Determination of Impact Possible definitions:  loss of life, dollars, prestige, market share.   Example of impact scale  51 to 10 = High, 11 to 50 = Medium, 0 to 10 = low Risk is the product of likelihood & impact. If vulnerability has a likelihood of being exploited is  1 (high), and the impact is 100 (high), the risk would be 100.
[slide10.xml] Impact The likely impact of a security incident falls into one of three categories: High – indicates a serious loss of assets or business operations. High also indicates a failure of one or more essential operational processes, a serious business interruption, or could even indicate financial losses. Medium – indicates a short pause in critical operations or moderate loss of assets. Low – indicates relatively mild damage to assets, and no interruption of critical processes.
[slide11.xml] Risk Assessment (Qualitative)
[slide12.xml] Risk  The probability of the realization of a threat. There are three basic elements of risk:  Assets ,  Any item of economic value owned by an individual or corporation. Threats,  and  Any agent, condition, or circumstance that could potentially cause harm, loss, or damage, or compromise an IT asset or data asset. From a security professional’s perspective, threats can be categorized as events that can affect the confidentiality, integrity, or availability of the organization’s assets.  Page 8 provides the categories of threats. Vulnerabilities.  A weakness in the system design, implementation, software or code, or the lack of a mechanism.   Exploit  (although not one of the components of risk, it is still important). An exploit refers to a piece of software, a tool, or a technique that a threat can utilize to exploit a vulnerability that leads to access, privilege escalation, loss of integrity, or denial of service on a computer system. 
[slide13.xml] ECC Risk Management Risk identification, Risk assessment, Risk treatment, Risk tracking, Risk review
[slide14.xml] Risk Assessment (Quantitative) Step 1.  Determine the single loss expectancy (SLE):  SLE is calculated as follows: SLE = asset value × exposure factor.  The exposure factor (EF) is the subjective, potential portion of the loss to an asset. Step 2.  Evaluate the annual rate of occurrence (ARO):  Best educated guess on often an unwanted event (SLE) is likely to occur in a year. Step 3.  Calculate the annual loss expectancy (ALE):  Combine the potential loss and rate per year to determine the magnitude of the risk. This is expressed as annual loss expectancy (ALE), which is calculated as follows: ALE = SLE × ARO.
[slide15.xml] Sample Risk Assessment Question If you have data worth $500 that has an exposure factor of 50 percent due to lack of countermeasures such as antivirus, what would the SLE be?” SLE × EF = SLE, or $500 × .50 = $250 Calculate the annualized loss expectance (ALE) if you know that this type of event typically happened four times a year? 
[slide16.xml] Sample Risk Assessment Question If you have data worth $500 that has an exposure factor of 50 percent due to lack of countermeasures such as antivirus, what would the SLE be?” SLE × EF = SLE, or $500 × .50 = $250 The annualized loss expectance (ALE) if you knew that this type of event typically happened four times a year?  Yes, as this would mean the ARO is 4. Therefore: ALE = SLE × ARO or $250 × 4 = $1,000 This means that, on average (ALE), the loss is $1,000 per year
[slide17.xml] Threat Modeling It's an engineering technique you can use to help you identify  threats , attacks, vulnerabilities, and countermeasures that could affect your application. You can use  threat modeling  to shape your application's  There are five major threat modeling steps:  Defining security requirements.   Creating an application diagram.   Identifying threats.   Mitigating threats.   Validating that threats have been mitigated.  Threat modeling should be part of your routine development lifecycle, enabling you to progressively refine your threat model and further reduce risk.
[slide18.xml] EC-Council Threat Modeling Threat modelling works to identify, communicate, and understand threats and mitigations within the context of protecting something of value. ECC states that Threat Modelling consists of five phases: Identify Security Objectives, Applications overview, Decompose Application, Identify Threats, and Identify Vulnerabilities. Threat modeling is an element of risk management. 
[slide19.xml] Microsoft Threat Modeling Tool The Microsoft Threat Modeling Tool makes threat modeling easier for all developers through a standard notation for visualizing system components, data flows, and security boundaries.  It also helps threat modelers identify classes of threats they should consider based on the structure of their software design. We designed the tool with non-security experts in mind, making threat modeling easier for all developers by providing clear guidance on creating and analyzing threat models. https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/securityengineering/sdl/threatmodeling LAB 1: What do you think are the most important features of the tools?
[slide20.xml] Types of security tests  Security tests are also known by many names, including the following: Vulnerability testing Network evaluations Red-team exercises Penetration testing Host vulnerability assessment
[slide21.xml] Types of Security Assessments High-level assessments: Also called a level I assessment A top-down look at the organization’s policies, procedures, and guidelines. This type of vulnerability assessment or audit, does not include any hands-on testing. The purpose of a top-down assessment is to answer three questions: Do the applicable policies exist? Are they being followed? Is there content sufficient to guard against potential risk? Network evaluations: Also called a level II assessment , It includes the level 1 assessment and it includes hands-on activities.   These hands-on activities include information gathering, scanning, vulnerability assessment  scanning, and other hands-on activities.  Penetration tests: also referred to as level III assessments . These events usually take on an adversarial role and look to see what the outsider can access and control. Penetration tests are less concerned with policies and procedures and are more focused on finding low-hanging fruit and seeing what a hacker can accomplish on this network. 
[slide22.xml] Hacker and Cracker Descriptions Cracker Used to describe individuals who seek to compromise the security of a system without permission from an authorized party.  Ethical hacker  An individual who performs security tests and other vulnerability-assessment activities to help organizations secure their infrastructures. Sometimes ethical hackers are referred to as white hat hackers
[slide23.xml] Hacking Methodology
[slide24.xml] Types of hackers
[slide25.xml] Ethical Hacker rules
[slide26.xml] Live-Fire Exercises(LFX)  A live-fire exercise (LFX) is one in which the participants are defending real or simulated information systems against real (though friendly) attackers. Red Team A red team is a group that acts as adversaries during an exercise.  Blue Team The blue team is the group that acts as defenders during an exercise.  White Team  The white team consists of anyone who will plan, document, assess, or moderate the exercise. 
[slide27.xml] Project Management In order for a project to be successful, some basic questions need to be answered: What’s the scope of the assessment, what are the driving events,  What are the goals of the assessment,  What will it take to get approval, and what’s needed in the final report.
[slide28.xml] Business Reasons for Ethical Hacking A breach in security:  One or more events have occurred that highlight a lapse in security.  Compliance with state, federal, regulatory, or other law or mandate: Due diligence:  An investigation or evaluation so that that the business can make an informed decision by enhancing the amount and quality of information  so that all its costs, benefits, and risks can be examined.
[slide29.xml] Phases of a Pen Penetration test Three phases: Preparation, Scoping the project: Define goals and guidelines Performing the Assessment, and Lets do some ethical hacking Conclusion. Written report
[slide30.xml] Warnings Written approval is the most critical step of the testing process. Never perform any tests without written approval. If you are an independent consultant, you might also get  insurance  before starting any type of test. Umbrella policies and those that cover errors and omissions are commonly used. These types of liability policies can help protect  you should anything go wrong. Management support is critical in a security test to be successful.
[slide31.xml] Communication Throughout the entire process, you should be in close contact with management to keep them abreast of your findings. There shouldn’t be any big surprises when you submit the report.  While you might have found some serious problems, they should be discussed with management before the report is written and submitted.  The goal is to keep them in the loop and advised of  the status of the assessment.  If you find items that present a critical vulnerability, stop all tests and immediately inform management.  Your priority should always be the health and welfare of the organization.
[slide32.xml] Reporting Detail the results of what was found.  Vulnerabilities should be discussed, as should the potential risk they pose. The report should be comprehensive and self-contained. Most reports contain: Introduction Statement of work performed Results and conclusions Recommendations.  Recommendations should be ranked from highest to lowest risk. Secure the Report While in electronic storage, use encryption. Printed copy of the report should be marked Confidential , Most consultants destroy reports and all test information after a contractually obligated period of time.
[slide33.xml] Security Program It provides the big picture for how you will keep your company’s data secure. It takes a holistic approach that describes how every part of your company is involved in the program.  Your security program defines what data is covered and what is not. It assesses the risks your company faces, and how you plan to mitigate them. It indicates how often the program will be re-evaluated and updated, and when you will assess compliance with the program.  A security program is made up of Policies, Procedures, Standards, Baselines, and Guidelines.
[slide34.xml] Components of a Security Program Policies  What management expects when it comes to security. Procedures  Step by step instructions on how to achieve the what. Standards  A set of specifications for hardware or software, agreed upon by academic and industry contributors. Baselines A minimum or starting point used for comparisons. Guidelines A general rule, principle, or piece of advice.
[slide35.xml] U.S. Code Title 18: Crimes and Criminal Procedure: Part 1: Crimes: Chapter 47: Fraud and False Statements: Section 1029 and 1030. Section 1029, Fraud and related activity with access devices:  It mandates penalties for anyone who knowingly  and with intent to defraud produce, use, or traffic in one or more counterfeit access devices.   Access devices can be an application or hardware that is created specifically to generate any type of access credentials, including passwords, credit card numbers, long-distance telephone service access codes, PINs, and so on for the purpose of unauthorized access. Section 1030, Fraud and related activity in connection with computers: It mandates penalties for anyone who accesses a computer in an unauthorized  manner or exceeds one’s access rights.  This a powerful law because companies can use it to prosecute employees when they use the capability and access that companies have given them to carry out fraudulent activities.  The law covers just about any computer or device connected to a network or Internet.
[slide36.xml] Other federal laws  Electronic Communication Privacy Act:  Computer Fraud and Abuse Act of 1984:  The Cyber Security Enhancement Act of 2002:  The Uniting and Strengthening America by Providing Appropriate Tools Required to Intercept and Obstruct Terrorism (USA PATRIOT) Act of 2001:  The Federal Information Security Management Act (FISMA):  Federal Sentencing Guidelines of 1991:  Economic Espionage Act of 1996:  U.S. Child Pornography Prevention Act of 1996:
[slide37.xml] Assurance Evaluation An  assurance evaluation  examines the security-relevant parts of a system, meaning the TCB, access control mechanisms, reference monitor, kernel, and protection mechanisms.  The relationship and interaction between these components are also evaluated in order to determine the level of protection required and provided by the system.  Historically, there were different methods of evaluating and assigning assurance levels to systems.  Today, however, a framework known as the Common Criteria is the only one of global significance.
[slide38.xml] Common Criteria ISO 15408 international standard Supersedes TCSEC and ITSEC Typically applied to computer components sold to governments, not to organizations. Provides vendors a method to develop products to a common set of requirements and allows consumers to evaluate products against a predefined common baseline. Uses “protection profiles” and “security targets” to aide in the development of products and systems that have IT security functions. Protection profiles (PP) are a common set of requirements for a category of vendor products.  If PPs aren’t sufficient, then consumers may specify there own. Security targets Consumer defines a set of requirements from a menu of possible requirements into a Security Target Vendors develop products (the Target of Evaluation) and have them evaluated against that target.
[slide39.xml] Common Criteria terms Target of Evaluation (ToE):  the system or product that is being evaluated  Security Target (ST):  the documentation describing the TOE, including the security requirements and operational environment  Protection Profile (PP):  an independent set of security requirements and objectives for a specific category of products or systems, such as firewalls or intrusion detection systems  Evaluation Assurance Level (EAL):  the evaluation score of the tested product or system
[slide40.xml] Common Criteria’s Seven Evaluation Assurance Levels (EALs) for a Target of Evaluation (TOE) EAL1: Functionally Tested (Lowest) EAL2: Structurally Tested EAL3: Methodically Tested and Checked EAL4: Methodically Designed, Tested and Reviewed EAL5: Semi-formally Designed and Tested EAL6: Semi-formally Verified Design and Tested EAL7: Formally Verified Design and Tested (Highest)
[slide41.xml] Trusted Computer System Evaluation Criteria (TCSEC) TCSEC  (Superseded by Common Criteria) Very specific, very formal and rigid. Referred to as the DoD Orange Book (which is part of the Rainbow Series) TCSEC is use to evaluate, classify and select systems to be used by DoD and Intelligence agencies. Focused on confidentiality, not integrity Evaluation divisions and classes: A – Verified Protection  (Highest) A1 – Verified Design B – Mandatory Protection B3 – Security domains B2 – Structured protection B1 – Labeled security C – Discretionary protection C2 – Controlled access C1 – Discretionary protection D – Minimal security (Lowest) D1 – Minimal Protection TCSEC introduced the concept of the Trusted Computing Base (TCB) into product evaluation.  
[slide42.xml] Information Technology Security Evaluation Criteria (ITSEC) (Superseded by Common Criteria) European standard for security evaluations ITSEC addresses  confidentiality ,  integrity , and  availability , whereas TCSEC evaluated only  confidentiality Consumer defines a set of requirements from a menu of possible requirements into a Security Target Vendors develop products (the Target of Evaluation) and have them evaluated against that target.
[slide43.xml] Compliance Regulations SOX,  GLBA HIPAA,  ISO/IEC 27001:2013  Children’s Internet Protection Act (CIPA  PCI-DSS, 
[slide44.xml] Payment Card Industry Data Security Standard (PCI-DSS) PCI-DSS 12 requirements 1: Protect your system with firewalls  2: Configure passwords and settings  3: Protect stored cardholder data   4: Encrypt transmission of cardholder data across open, public networks  5: Use and regularly update anti-virus software 6: Regularly update and patch systems 7: Restrict access to cardholder data by business need-to-know 8: Assign a unique ID to each person with computer access 9: Restrict physical access to workplace and cardholder data 10: Implement logging and log management 11: Conduct vulnerability scans and penetration tests 12: Documentation and risk assessments  Addition information: https://www.securitymetrics.com/blog/what-are-12-requirements-pci-dss-compliance
[slide45.xml] NIST Cyber Security Framework (CSF) A voluntary cybersecurity framework for organizations that are part of the critical infrastructure.  The CSF is divided into three main components: The Framework Core  which contains 5 functions, 22 categories, and 98 subcategories. The Implementation Tiers  which defines the degree of rigor of your cybersecurity program  Partial (tier 1),  Risk Informed (tier 2),  Repeatable (tier 3), or  Adaptive (tier 4).   The Framework Profile  describes the state of an organization with regard to the CSF categories and subcategories. It allows decision-makers to compare the “as-is” situation to “future” possibilities. https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=33VZ7RvMkgM
[slide46.xml] NIST Cyber Security Framework (CSF)  The CSF Core organizes cybersecurity activities into five higher-level functions with which you should be familiar.  Everything we do can be aligned with one of these higher-level functions . Identify  Understand your organization’s business context, resources, and risks. Protect  Develop appropriate controls to mitigate risk in ways that make sense. Detect  Discover in a timely manner anything that threatens your security. Respond  Quickly contain the effects of anything that threatens your security. Recover Return to a secure state that enables business activities after an incident
[slide47.xml] ISO/IEC 27000 series  A family of global Information Security Management System (ISMS) standards, known as the ISO/IEC 27000 series. ISO/IEC 27007 ISMS auditing ISO/IEC 27008 Guidance for auditors ISO/IEC 27031 Business continuity ISO/IEC 27033 Network security ISO/IEC 27034 Application security ISO/IEC 27035 Incident management ISO/IEC 27037 Digital evidence collection and preservation It is common for organizations to seek an ISO/IEC 27001 certification by an accredited third party. The third party assesses the organization against the ISMS requirements laid out in ISO/IEC 27001 and attests to the organization’s compliance level.
[slide48.xml] The Control Objectives for Information and related Technology (COBIT) A framework and set of control objectives. It defines goals for the controls that should be used to properly manage IT and to ensure that IT maps to business needs. COBIT is broken down into  four domains (categories):  Plan and Organize,  Acquire and Implement,  Deliver and Support, and  Monitor and Evaluate.  Each category drills down into  subcategories . For example, the Acquire and Implement category contains the following subcategories: Acquire and Maintain Application Software Acquire and Maintain Technology Infrastructure Develop and Maintain Procedures Install and Accredit Systems Manage Changes COBIT offers  a “checklist” approach  to IT governance by providing a list of things that must be thought through and accomplished when carrying out different IT functions. It lays out a complete roadmap that can be followed to accomplish each of the 34 control objectives this model deals with.
[slide49.xml] The Sherwood Applied Business Security Architecture (SABSA) A framework and methodology for  enterprise security architecture and service management.  It is a  layered model  in which the first layer defines business requirements from a security perspective .  Each layer of the model decreases in abstraction and increases in detail  so that it builds upon the others and moves from policy to practical implementation of technology and solutions.  The idea is to provide a chain of traceability through the strategic, conceptual, design, implementation, and metric and auditing levels. It provides a lifecycle  model so that the architecture can be constantly monitored and improved upon over time.
[slide50.xml] SABSA layers Assets Motivation Process People Location Time (What) (Why) (How) (Who) (Where) (When) Contextual The Business risk Business process Business organization Business Business time business model model and relationships geography Dependencies Conceptual Business Control Security Security Security Security related attributes objectives strategies and entity model domain lifetimes and profile architectural and trust model deadlines layering framework Logical Business Security Security Entity Security Security information policies services schema and domain processing model Privilege profiles definitions cycle profiles And associations Logical Business Security rules, Security Users, Platform Control data model practices, and mechanisms applications, and network structure procedures and user interface infrastructure execution Component Detailed Security Security Identities, Processes, Security step data standards products and functions, nodes, timing and structures tools actions, and addresses, sequencing ACLs and protocols Operational Assurance Operation risk Security Application Security Security of operation management service and user of sites, operations continuity management management networks, and schedule and support and support platforms
[slide51.xml] The Open Group Architecture Framework (TOGAF) Has its origins in the  U.S. Department of Defense.    It provides an approach for  designing, implementing, and governing an enterprise information architecture  that can be used to develop the following architecture types: Business architecture Data architecture Applications architecture Technology architecture This framework can be used to create individual architectures through the use of its  Architecture Development Method (ADM).    This method is an  iterative and cyclic  process that allows requirements to be continuously reviewed and the individual architectures updated as needed.  These different architectures can allow a technology architect to understand the enterprise from four different views ( business, data, application, and technology)  so she can ensure her team develops the necessary technology to work within the environment.
[slide52.xml] The Information Technology Infrastructure Library (ITIL)  The de facto standard of  best practices for IT service management . ITIL was created because of the increased dependence on information technology to meet business needs.  The lack of a  common language  and understanding of each other’s domain (business versus IT) has caused many companies to ineffectively blend their business objectives and IT functions. ITIL is a customizable framework that provides the goals, the general activities necessary to achieve these goals, and the input and output values for each process required to meet these determined goals.  Although ITIL has a component that deals with security, its focus is more toward internal service level agreements (SLAs) between the IT department and the “customers” it serves .

[slide55.xml] Hacking Methodology Reconnaissance and footprinting :  Obtain the big picture of your TOE. 99% passive. Scanning and enumeration:  Is active reconnaissance Obtaining a more detailed look at the target. OS, applications, services, ports, shares, accounts, passwords, etc. Gaining access :  The entry point into the network, application, or system. Escalation of privilege:  Techniques used to maintain control such as escalation of privilege. Maintaining access Make sure you can come back Covering tracks:  Planting rootkits, backdoors, and clearing logs are activities normally performed at this step.
[slide56.xml] Privilege Escalation Vertical privilege escalation (privilege elevation):  An attacker attempts to gain higher privileges or access with an existing account they have compromised. For example, an attacker takes over a regular user account on a network and attempts to gain administrative privileges. Horizontal privilege escalation:  An attacker expands their privileges by taking over a privileged account and misusing the legitimate privileges granted to the user.  This might mean hijacking an account with administrator privileges or root privileges. If web applications this might mean gaining access to a user's bank account or the admin account of a SaaS app.
[slide57.xml] Ethical Hacking Process Permission : Obtaining written permission. Reconnaissance : Can be both passive and active. Scanning : Can include the use of port-scanning tools and network mappers. Gaining access : The entry point into the network, application, or system. Maintaining access : Techniques used to maintain control such as escalation of privilege. Covering tracks : Covering tracks and clearing logs are activities normally performed at this step. Reporting:  Writing the report and listing your findings.
[slide58.xml] Methodology to secure an organization Assessment : Ethical hacking, penetration testing, and hands-on security tests. Policy development : Development of policy based on the organization’s  goals and mission. The focus should be on the organization’s critical assets. Implementation:  The building of technical, operational, and managerial controls to secure key assets and data. Training:  Employees need to be trained as to how to follow policy and how to configure key security controls, such as IDS systems and firewalls. Audit:  Auditing involves periodic reviews of the controls that have been put in place to provide good security. Regulations such as Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act (HIPAA) specify that this should be done yearly.
[slide59.xml] Security Testing Methodolgies  National Institute of Standards and Technology 800-115, Technical Guide to Information Security Testing and Assessment Guide to information security testing; Operational Critical Threat, Asset, and Vulnerability Evaluation (OCTAVE); and  Open Source Security Testing Methodology Manual (OSSTMM).
[slide60.xml] The End

[slide6.xml] Penetration  Test Definition Penetration Test Also known as Pen Test or PTES Is an authorized attack on a computer(s) and/or network(s) to identify security weaknesses and exploit the weaknesses to validate the severity of vulnerabilities to assist in risk management  Pen Tests have varying objectives, goals, and rules that pertain to the approved activity.
[slide7.xml] Some  Basic Terminology Hacking (originally): A  term  used  to  describe  a  good  computer  programmer  who  is capable  of finding  workarounds   to  fix  technical problems. Hacking (today): Viewed in both a positive and negative manner. Most outside of the IA/IT community view it as   negative. Some in the community don’t like the term b/c it  is cliché. White Hat: A “hacker” who is  legally authorized to discover/exploit vulnerabilities with the intent to help   increase security. Grey Hat: A “hacker” who uses skill/talent w/o authorization and does not intend to cause harm/damage. Typically  conducts  work  without  complete  authorization, uses  non-authorized  methods,  etc. Black Hat: A “hacker” who has no regard for obtaining authorization and performs outside the law.   Typically  focuses  on  personal  gain,  maliciousness,  or  has  an  agenda  to achieve. Penetration Tester: Conducts  authorized  offensive  attacks  against  a  predesignated  target(s)  to  discover and exploit vulnerabilities. Red  Team (DoD): An  authorized team  of  personnel  certified  by  NSA
[slide8.xml] Testing Knowledge Crystal Box: Full (90%+) knowledge of target(s); includes past vulnerability scans and other information in order to decrease the amount of time needed to research and discovery Grey Box: Have partial knowledge of the testing environment Black Box: Have no knowledge of the testing environment

[slide14.xml] Factors  that  Limit  PTES Events Constraints  & Limitations Will  NOT  discover  all vulnerabilities Will  NOT  make  use  of  all exploits It  is  ONLY  a  snapshot  in time Events  have  a  limited  time  duration  (the adversary   doesn’t) The  scope  of  the event Specific  testing  times,  restricted  targets, allowable  exploits, etc. Skill  Level  of  the  penetration testers Restriction  of  certain  tool types Restriction  of  certain  methods  of access

DECK 4: Chapter Four - The OSI Model.pptx

[slide1.xml] Chapter Four The OSI Model (Ports, ARP, DHCP)
[slide2.xml] Ports (1 of 3) A port number is used to uniquely identify an application or processes running on a single computer.  A port number allows applications on your machine to communicate over the Internet.  Example,  Your machine can have many applications that need to communicate over the Internet, they can do so by assigning each application a port number.  Each of these applications with their assigned port number can then share can share one IP address.
[slide3.xml] Ports (2 of 4)
[slide4.xml] Ports (3 of 3) There are approximately 65,000 ports; they are divided into  Well-known ports (0–1023),  Registered ports (1024–49151), and  Dynamic ports (49152–65535).
[slide5.xml] Common Ports and Protocols 20-21 FTP 22 SSH 23 Telnet 25 SMTP  53 DNS TCP/UDP 67/68 DHCP UDP 69 TFTP UDP 79 Finger 80 HTTP  88/464 Kerberos TCP/UDP 110 POP3 TCP 111 RPCBind 135 MSRPC 137/138/139 NetBios  143 IMAP 161/162 SNMP UDP 389 LDAP TCP 443 SSL/TLS TCP 445 SMB TCP/UDP 993 IMAPS 995 POP3S 1701 L2TP 1723 PPTP 3306 MYSQL 3389 RDP 5900 VNC 8080 HTTP-Proxy You need to learn these port numbers and programs!!!!
[slide6.xml] Logical ports video logical ports video https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=uMwzsh_Dd9Y
[slide7.xml] Ping Specific Port using telnet The easiest way to ping a specific port is to use the telnet command followed by the IP address and the port that you want to ping. You can also specify a domain name instead of an IP address followed by the specific port to be pinged. $ telnet <ip_address> <port_number> $ telnet <domain_name> <port_number>
[slide8.xml] Ping Specific Port using telnet
[slide9.xml] Ping Specific Port using Powershell If you are running a computer in a Windows environment, you can ping specific port numbers using Powershell. In order to ping a specific port using Powershell, you have to use the “Test-NetConnection” command followed by the IP address and the port number to be pinged. $ Test-NetConnection <ip_address> -p <port_number> As an example, let’s say that we want to ping the “192.168.178.35/24” host on the port 389. To achieve that, we would run the following command $ Test-NetConnection 192.168.178.35 -p 389
[slide10.xml] Ping Specific Port using Powershell
[slide11.xml] Port lab Watch the PING AND TRACERT (traceroute) networking commands video at https://devconnected.com/how-to-ping-specific-port-number/ In the tracert portion, when tracert is sending an ICMP packet to 66.176.124.54. What is the last device before the ICMP packet reaches its destination and what is the devices IP address?
[slide12.xml] Layer # Name Primary purpose 7 Application Allows your  applications access to network services HTTP, Telnet,  SMB, SMTP 6 Presentation Data representation from one  machine to another Translation, interpretation, compression, encryption 5 Session Manage  dialogue Full-duplex, half-duplex,  simplex 4 Transport Select transport mode Guaranteed vs non-  guaranted  delivery 3 Network Manage external (Internet)  communications IP addressing, fragmentation, routing 2 Data Link Manage internal (LAN)  communications Physical  addressing, media access control (placing data on the physical wire) 1 Physical Bit-level electrical transmission between different devices Ethernet, DSL, USB, RS-232 protocols
[slide13.xml] 
[slide14.xml] OSI Terminology Segment or datagram  Refers to a data unit at Layer 4, the Transport Layer,  Packet  refers to a data unit at layer 3, the Network Layer.  Frame  Refers to a data unit at Layer 2, the Data Link Layer,  Frame  Still referred to as a frame at Layer 1, the Physical Layer,  For TCP/IP communication over Ethernet, a TCP segment is carried in one or more IP packets, which are each carried in one or more Ethernet frames.
[slide15.xml] OSI video https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HEEnLZV2wGI   18 minutes The OSI Model Animation - YouTube https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-6Uoku-M6oY https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3b_TAYtzuho The OSI and TCP IP Model - YouTube https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LX_b2M3IzN8
[slide16.xml] 
[slide17.xml] 
[slide18.xml] Encapsulation (1 of 2) Figure 4-27 IPv4 Packet Layout
[slide19.xml] IP encapsulating a TCP packet IP encapsulating a UDP packet IP encapsulating a ICMP Packert
[slide20.xml] Encapsulation http://www.firewall.cx/networking-topics/the-osi-model/179-osi-data-encapsulation.html CCNA Training CBT - Data Encapsulation https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3se8JizBmPg 4:07 minutes
[slide21.xml] TCP/UDP Transport Control Protocol TCP enables two hosts to establish a connection and exchange data reliably. To do this, TCP performs a three-step handshake before data is sent. During the data transmission process,  TCP guarantees delivery of data  by using sequence and acknowledgment numbers. At the completion of the data-transmission process.  TCP performs a four-step shutdown that gracefully concludes the session.  User Datagram Protocol UDP performs none of the handshaking processes that we see performed with TCP.   Although that makes it considerably less reliable than TCP,  it does offer the benefit of speed. It is ideally suited for data that requires fast delivery and is not sensitive to packet loss.  UDP is easier to spoof by attackers than TCP because it does not use sequence and acknowledgment numbers. 
[slide22.xml] Three way handshake
[slide23.xml] Three-way handshake The client sends the server a TCP packet with the sequence number flag ( SYN flag ) set and an initial sequence number (ISN). The server replies by sending a packet with the  SYN/ACK  flag set to the client.   The synchronize sequence number flag informs the client that it would like to communicate with it, and the acknowledgment flag informs the client that it received its ISN. The client’s ISN (now called an acknowledgment number) will be one digit higher than the client’s ISN. The server generates it’s own ISN, as well, to keep track of every byte sent to the client. When the client receives the server’s packet, it creates an  ACK  packet to acknowledge that the data has been received from the server. At this point, communication can begin.
[slide24.xml] Three way handshake SYN Sequence # 105 Three way handshake SYN/ACK Your Acknowledgement  # 106 My    Sequence # 223 ACK Your Acknowledgement # 224 My    Sequence # 106
[slide25.xml] Very Important videos
[slide26.xml] Terminating a TCP connection At the conclusion of communication, TCP terminates the session by using a four step shutdown: The client sends the server a packet with the FIN/ACK flags set. The server sends a packet ACK flag set to acknowledge the client’s packet. The server then generates another packet with the FIN/ACK flags set to inform the client that it also is ready to conclude the session. The client sends the server a packet with the ACK flag set to conclude the session.
[slide27.xml] TCP Flags The six most common flags are: ACK, Push, RST, SYN, FIN, and URG.  For example, the SYN and ACK flags are used in the three-way handshaking, and the RST and FIN flags are used to tear down a connection.  FIN is used during a normal four-step shutdown, and RST is used to signal the end of an abnormal session.  Another flag is urgent (URG). If no flags are set at all, the flags can be referred to as NULL, as none are set.
[slide28.xml] Remember
[slide29.xml] Ethernet frame  An ethernet frame is what is sent over the wire or wireless signal on your network. The sending packet will add its ethernet frame to the packet so the packet can get to the receiving machine or the gateway router if the packet is destined for the Internet.  If the packet contained within the frame is destined for the Internet, the ethernet frame is stripped off by the sending router. The receiving router than adds its ethernet frame to the packet so the packet can be sent around the receiving network until it finds the correct machine
[slide30.xml] Ethernet frame
[slide31.xml] Ethernet frame An Ethernet frame is preceded by a preamble and start frame delimiter (SFD).  Each Ethernet frame starts with an Ethernet header, which contains destination and source MAC addresses as its first two fields.  The middle section of the frame is payload data including any headers for other protocols (for example, Internet Protocol) carried in the frame.  The frame ends with a frame check sequence (FCS), which is a 32-bit cyclic redundancy check used to detect any in-transit corruption of data.
[slide32.xml] IP Header
[slide33.xml] IP Header fields(1 of 3) IP packets are composed of a header and payload. The IPv4 packet header consists of: 4 bits for the  IP Version,  IPv4 or IPv6 packet, 4 bits for the  IHeader Length , the header length. 8 bits for the  Type of Service , AKA  Quality of Service (QoS),  dictates the packet’s priority. 16 bits for the  length of the entire packet  in bytes, 16 bits that contain an  identification tag  to help reconstruct the packet from several fragments. 3 bits. The first contains a zero, followed by a flag that says whether the packet is allowed to be fragmented or not (DF: Don't fragment), and a flag to state whether more fragments of a packet follow (MF: More Fragments)
[slide34.xml] IP Header fields (2 of 3) 13 bits that contain  the fragment’s offset , a field to identify position of fragment within original packet 8 bits that contain the  Time to live (TTL),  which is the number of hops (router, computer or device along a network) the packet is allowed to pass before it dies  (for example, a packet with a TTL of 16 will be allowed to go across 16 routers to get to its destination before it is discarded), 8 bits that contain the  protocol  (TCP, UDP, ICMP, etc.) 16 bits for  the Header Checksum , a number used in error detection. 32 bits that contain the  source IP address , 32 bits that contain the  destination address .
[slide35.xml] IP Header fields (3 of 3) An  options  contains a flag of varied length, which flag depends upon what protocol is used,  A  padding field , padding is basically used to make sure that the IP packet header has a length that is a multiple of 32 bits. It is needed because of the varying length of the options field in the IP header. Then the data that the packet carries is added.  An IP packet has no trailer.  Remember, an IP packet is often carried as the payload inside an Ethernet frame, which has its own header and trailer.
[slide36.xml] IP Header: Networking & TCP/IP Tutorial. TCP/IP Explained https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UrO-9Uagn24 9:34 minutes
[slide37.xml] 
[slide38.xml] The TCP Header (segment) fields: (1 of 3) Source Port and Destination Port fields  (16 bits each) identify the end points of the connection. Sequence Number field  (32 bits) used to keep track of the conversation between two machines.  Acknowledgement Number field  (32 bits) contains the value of the next sequence number that the sender of the segment is expecting to receive Data Offset  (variable length) tells how many 32-bit words are contained in the TCP header. 
[slide39.xml] The TCP  Header (segment) fields: (2 of 3) Reserved field  (6 bits) must be zero. This is for future use. Flags field  (6 bits) contains the various flags: URG—Indicates that some urgent data has been placed. ACK—Indicates that acknowledgement number is valid. PSH—Indicates that data should be passed to the application as soon as possible. RST—Resets the connection. SYN—Synchronizes sequence numbers to initiate a connection. FIN—Means that the sender of the flag has finished sending data.
[slide40.xml] The TCP Header (segment) fields: (3 of 3) Window field  (16 bits) specifies the size of the sender's receive window (that is, buffer space available for incoming data). Checksum field  (16 bits) indicates whether the header was damaged in transit. Urgent pointer field  (16 bits) points to the first urgent data byte in the segment. Options field  (variable length) specifies various TCP options. Data field  (variable length) contains upper-layer information.
[slide41.xml] TCP Header: Networking & TCP/IP Tutorial. TCP/IP Explained https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=M-zX_6FuFKg 9:54 minutes
[slide42.xml] IP v4 Fragmentation Fragmentation normally occurs when files must be split because of maximum transmission unit (MTU) size limitations.   The MTU is how much the receiving computer can receive. If the packet is larger than the receiving MTU allows, the IP protocol will  divide the packet into smaller fragments.  Each fragment is labeled with a length, an offset, and a more bit. The length specifies the total length of the fragment,  The offset specifies the distance from the first byte of the original datagram,  The more bit is used to indicate whether the fragment has more fragments to follow or is this fragment the last in the series of fragments. 
[slide43.xml] IP Header: Networking & TCP/IP Tutorial. TCP/IP Explained https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UrO-9Uagn24 9:34 minutes I know it’s the same video you just watched. Watch it again!!!
[slide44.xml] ICMP Its purpose is to provide feedback used for information gathering and trouble shooting. The first byte of an ICMP header indicates the  type  of ICMP message. The second byte contains the  code  for each particular type of ICMP.  For example, a type 3, code 3 ICMP means that a destination error occurred and that the specific destination error is that the targeted port is unreachable.
[slide45.xml] IP version 6 Improvements
[slide46.xml] IPv6 Address format An IPv6 address is made of 128 bits divided into eight 16-bits blocks. Each block is then converted into 4-digit Hexadecimal numbers separated by colon symbols. For example, A 128 bit IPv6 address represented in binary format and divided into eight 16-bits blocks: 0010000000000001; 0000000000000000; 0011001000111000; 1101111111100001 0000000001100011; 0000000000000000; 0000000000000000; 1111111011111011 Each block is then converted into Hexadecimal and again separated by ‘:’ symbol: 2001:0000:3238:DFE1:0063:0000:0000:FEFB In IPv6, a hextet is a sixteen-bit aggregation, or four nibbles. .a hextet consists of 4 hexadecimal digits (four nibbles).  A hextet is the unofficial name for each of the 8 blocks in an IPv6 address. In IPv6 a nibble is 4 bits (half a byte), in IPv6 one nibble represents one hex character.
[slide47.xml] Remember these ICMP Types and codes Type Code Function 0/8 0 Echo response/request (ping) 3 0–15 Destination unreachable 4 0 Source quench 5 0–3 Redirect 11 0–1 Time exceeded
[slide48.xml] 48 Common ICMP code values for Type 3, Destination Unreachable Type 3 Destination Unreachable
[slide49.xml] Type 0 and Code 0
[slide50.xml] Source Routing Designed to enable individuals to specify the route that a packet should take through a network. It allows the user to bypass network problems or congestion. IP’s source routing informs routers not to use their normal routes for delivery of the packet but to send it via the router identified in the packet’s header. This can let a hacker use another system’s IP address and get packets returned to him regardless of what routes are in between him and the destination. 
[slide51.xml] Review of ARP How network devices associate a specific MAC address with an IP address. ARP is a simple protocol that consists of two message types: 1. An ARP request:  Router A asks the network, “Who has this IP address?” 2. An ARP reply:  Switch B tells computer A, “I have that IP. My MAC address is XYZ.” Remember the majority of time a switch will perform this function. ARP Poisoning A switch is poisoned by sending phony ARP requests or replies to the switch in attempt to steer traffic to the sniffing system. The bogus ARP packets will be stored by the switch in its ARP cache and  now the attacker is mapped to the spoofed device. The MAC address being spoofed is usually the router so that the attacker can capture all outbound traffic. After this setup is in place, the hacker can pull off many types of man-in-the-middle attacks. This includes passing on the packets to their true destination, scanning them for useful information, or recording the packets for a session replay later.

[slide56.xml] ARP Spoofing Tools Arpspoof:  Redirects packets from system to system by forging ARP replies. Ufasoft Snif:   Designed for capturing and analysis of the packets going through the LAN. WinARPAttacker:  Can scan, attack, detect and attack computers on LAN. Ettercap: can be used for ARP poisoning, for passive sniffing, as a protocol decoder, and as a packet grabber.  Can also be used to capture usernames and passwords by using the -C switch .  Learn Switches for Cert. Cain and Abel:  A multipurpose tool that has the capability to perform a variety of tasks, including ARP poisoning, Windows computer enumeration, sniffing, and password cracking.  WINDNSSpoof:  This tool is a simple DNS ID spoofer for Windows. 
[slide57.xml] MAC Spoofing MAC spoofing can be used to bypass port security. Many organizations implement port security so that only certain MAC addresses are authorized to connect to a specific port.  When you use port security, you are simply assigning one or more MAC addresses to a secure port. If a port is configured as a secure port and the MAC address is not valid, a security violation occurs.  The attacker can use MAC spoofing to prevent the security violation and allow unauthorized traffic.  MAC spoofing can be accomplished by changing the setting of a Windows computer from the Registry, issuing a command from shell in Linux, or using a tool such as SMAC. SMAC:  A MAC spoofing tool that allows an attacker to spoof a MAC address.  The attacker can change a MAC address to any other value or manufacturer. SMAC is available from http://www.klcconsulting.net/smac.
[slide58.xml] Collision vs Broadcast Domains A  broadcast  domain contains all devices that can reach each other at the data link layer.  A  collision  domain is a part of a network where packet collisions can occur. A collision occurs when two devices send a packet at the same time on the shared network segment. A switch has 1 broadcast domain and there is a collision domain for each port. A router has 2 broadcast domains and 1 Separate collision domains
[slide59.xml] 
[slide60.xml] 
[slide61.xml] Dynamic Hosts Control Protocol (DHCP) A protocol that automatically assigns and manages IP addresses Eliminates need to manually assign and manage addresses Reduces possibility for address conflict Allows systems to move with little complication Uses the concept of “leasing” an address Space can be reserved for “static” address needs Uses UDP port 67 for servers and UDP port 68 for clients. DHCP process goes through 4 stages while assigning an IP address to the client. These stages are often abbreviated as  DORA  for: discovery,  offer,  request, and  acknowledgement.

[slide2.xml] Log Review Logs  are records of events that happen in your computer, either by a person or by a running process. They help you track what happened and troubleshoot problems. Why are logs so cryptic? The output format could be very cryptic, and in such a format that only the developers will truly understand because they created it. They generally have no interest in generating output for the common user, nor do they have any mandate or standard that tells them to. This creates problems for security professionals or computer administrators who are trying to investigate an incident or failure that may have occurred within their infrastructure.
[slide3.xml] Security Information and Event Management (SIEM) and Log Management  SIEMs and Log Management are two examples of software tools that allow IT organizations to monitor their security posture using log files, detect and respond to Indicators of Compromise (IoC) and conduct forensic data analysis and investigations into network events and possible attacks.  The easiest way to think of a SIEM is that it is part IDS and part Log management tool. Syslog is a logging system that has been standardized so that any flavor of UNIX/Linux operating system will output the same log format. Windows operating systems support the Eventlog format, and all events output to a standardized event log format.

[slide5.xml] Reading SMTP Logs SMTP logs are going to provide: time of requestor response, address of the recipient, message size and status code, etc. Code 220: server is ready for processing. Code 250: message accepted.  You don't have to send it again.   Code 421: service is not available. Code 450: server cannot access the mailbox to deliver a message.  Code 451: server aborted due to processing error.  Code 452: server has insufficient storage space available.

Router/Firewall ACLs Review Analysts should periodically review whether permissions and denials are adequate and in compliance with security policy. They should also review the router and firewall to ensure that both are in optimum operating condition. After this review is complete, Security Analysts are able to provide recommendations for enhancing protection. Social Media Profiling Many employees are very active on social media and sometimes inadvertently share classified information. This can pose a security risk. Content shared on LinkedIn, Facebook, Twitter, and other social media can be viewed by many people, including hackers. Social media profiling enables Security Analysts to keep track of the information employee's share on their social media accounts. They often employ the methods hackers use to profile target accounts. Security Analysts need to train personnel in the judicious use of social media. Every organization should issue clear rules regarding what can or cannot be shared via social media.

[slide21.xml] What is running on this machine There are approximately 65,000 port numbers; they are divided into  Well-known ports (0–1023),  Registered ports (1024–49151), and  Dynamic ports (49152–65535). A port number is used to uniquely identify an application or processes running on a single computer.  A port number allows applications on your machine to communicate over the Internet.  Example,  Your machine can have many applications that need to communicate over the Internet, they can do so by assigning each application a port number.  Each of these applications with their assigned port number can then share can share one IP address.
[slide22.xml] Finding Open Ports and Access Points Port scanning is the process of connecting to TCP and UDP ports for the purpose of finding what services and applications are running on the target device.  After running applications, open ports, and services are discovered, the hacker can then determine the best way to attack the system. WARNING Scanning someone’s network can be considered illegal.

Connected to 192.168.178.2. Escape character is '^]'. $ telnet 192.168.178.2 389 Connected to 192.168.178.2. Escape character is '^]’. Being able to connect to your remote host simply means that your service is up and running. In order to quit the Telnet utility, you can use the “Ctrl” + “]” keystrokes to escape and execute the  “q” command to quit.

[slide28.xml] Regional Internet Registries (RIR) Domain name registration is handled by a series of Regional Internet Registries (RIR) that manage, distribute, and register public IP addresses within their respective regions.  RIR Region of Control ARIN North and South America and sub-Saharan Africa APNIC Asia and Pacific  RIPE  Europe, Middle East, and parts of Africa  LACNIC Latin America and the Caribbean AfriNIC RIR to support Africa
[slide29.xml] Querying the RIR databases  Whois is a utility that queries the RIRs and returns:  network ip range, the domain ownership, address, location, phone number, and  DNS name servers .  Whois is built into the Linux operating system. From the Linux prompt, users can type in  whois domainname.com  or  whois?  to get a list of various options.  Windows users have to use a third-party tool or website to obtain Whois information. 
[slide30.xml] Popular port-scanning techniques Scan Type Initial  Flag type Open Port Response Closed Port Response Notes Full (TCP connect) SYN SYN/ACK RST Nosiest but most reliable Half  Stealth SYN scan SYN SYN/ACK RST No completion of 3-way handshake.  Designed for stealth but my  be picked up by IDS. XMAS  FIN, URG, PSH No response RST/ACK Doesn’t work on Windows machines. FIN FIN No response RST/ACK Doesn’t work on Windows machines NULL No response RST/ACK Doesn’t work on Windows machines ACK ACK RST No response Used in firewalls filter tests
[slide31.xml] What type of scan is this?
[slide32.xml] What type of scan is this?
[slide33.xml] (Idle) Zombie Scan Every IP packet on the Internet has a fragment identification number (IPID).  Since many operating systems simply increment this number for each packet they send, probing for the IPID can tell an attacker how many packets have been sent since the last probe. An IPID probe can be accomplished by sending a SYN/ACK to the recipient and it will respond with a RST and IPID number.
[slide34.xml] (Idle) Zombie Scan Probe the zombie's IPID and record it. Forge a SYN packet from the zombie and send it to the desired port on the target.  Probe the zombie's IPPD again. The target port state is then determined by comparing this new IPID with the one recorded in step 1. An IPID increase of 1, port is closed. An IPID increase of 2, port is open.
[slide35.xml] (Idle) Zombie Scan
[slide36.xml] UDP Port Scanning UDP does not have flags, nor does UDP issue responses. It’s a fire and forget protocol! The most you can hope for is a response from ICMP. If the port is closed, ICMP attempts to send an ICMP type 3 code 3 port unreachable message to the source of the UDP scan. But, if the network is blocking ICMP, no error message is returned. Therefore, the response to the scans might simply be no response.  If you are planning on doing UDP scans, plan for unreliable results.
[slide37.xml] Network Mapper (Nmap)  Its used to map out a network, you can use it to discover a network’s topology. Available for Windows and Linux as a GUI and command-line program.  It is probably the most widely used port scanner ever developed. It can do many types of scans and OS identification.  It also enables you to control the speed of the scan from slow to insane.   It can be used to identify a machine’s OS (aka OS fingerprinting)  It can be used to identify a what applications and services (and what versions) are running on a machine.
[slide38.xml] Port Status The most common port status is either, open, closed and filtered. Open  Is when an application on the host is ready to accept connections. Closed When the port responds with a reset packet. No application available to accept connections there. Filtered Occurs when Nmap can't probe the port, but it doesn't necessarily know that it's closed. This is usually due to a firewall blocking the scan on the network or host.

[slide41.xml] Hping Hping is an open-source spoofing tool that provides a pen tester with the ability to craft their own network packets to exploit vulnerable firewalls and intrusion detection and intrusion prevention systems.   It can do things like host and port detection and firewall testing, you can do timestamping, you can use traceroute, you can do fragmentation or you can even do denial of services and use it as an attack tool. hping3 -S -p80 -c1 192.168.1.1.  This is saying send a SYN packet over port 80 with a count of one (meaning send only one SYN packet) to this IP address. This is going to be very stealthy, because I'm only sending one packet out and trying to get a response to see if you're awake. This will allow me to enumerate your network very quietly and very stealthy against a single IP address just shown here. hping3 -A -p80 -c1 192.168.1.1.    I'm sending a ACK packet instead of a SYN packet.  Timestamping This allows to determine the system's uptime, how long has that host been online?  To send this type of a packet, you would use hping3 -c2 -S -p80 - -tcp-timestamp 192.168.1.1.  So in this case, I'm sending two SYN packets over port 80 to determine the uptime of that system. Now, the reason I'm sending two packets here is it's usually going to be a little bit more effective to send two when you're doing a timestamp than just doing one. traceroute Now, traceroute is going to use TCP or UDP packets to probe DNS ports to be able to perform traces when you can't use ICMP in a given network because it's blocked.  Traceroute and ping can often be blocked by firewalls because they're not going to allow echo reply packets, which use ICMP to go out of the network. By using different packet formats like SYN packets and ACK packets to do a traceroute for you.
[slide42.xml] Hping (in the good old days) Fragmentation.  Hping can be used to fragment packet, so an attacker (in the old days) could hide his attack in by fragmenting it. Fragmentation attempts to evade detection by the IDS and IPS and firewalls by sending fragmented packets across the network for later reassembly. Most modern OSs are not vulnerable to this because packets are reassembled first and then scanned by the IDS before being allowed to traverse the network. Denial of service or DoS.  Hping can be used to perform a denial of service attack.  When doing the ping of death, a packet is crafted to be over the maximum size of 65,535 bytes. Most modern OSs are not vulnerable to a ping of death, remember packets are reassembled first and then scanned by the IDS before being allowed to traverse the network.
[slide43.xml] Zeek (Bro) and p0f Zeek A part of security onion Zeek displays information about different devices on our network.  What software and version number is being used on the network, What type of service is being used IP address that piece of software being run on. p0f A tool that utilizes an array of sophisticated, purely passive trafficked fingerprinting techniques, to be able to identify the players behind any incidental TCP/IP communications. So even with just a single SYN packet, this tool can actually detect what type of operating system or what type of browser is being used, what type of wired/wire media, etc.
[slide44.xml] Responder Responder comes as part of the Kali.  It is a command line tool that's used to poison responses to NetBIOS, LLMNR, and MDNS name resolution requests in an attempt to perform a man-in-the-middle attack. Responder is designed to intercept those messages and those requests and return the attacker's host IP as the name of record. The attacker is replacing the destination IP address with his.  The benefit of using something like Responder in penetration testing is you can get into the system and you can actually become that man-in-the-middle. This is actually really useful when you're dealing with something like Windows file sharing or Server Message Block messages over SMB, because this can allow the attacker to retrieve the password hashes and then try to crack those later on.  Now when you use Responder and for this attack to work, the victim system must be either tricked into querying a nonexistent name or prevented from using the legitimate DNS service. The way it works is If a computer on the network can't reach the real DNS service, Windows is going start asking other computers in the network for that DNS name, and Responder will then say "Hey, I have the answer for you," and give you their IP address so they are between you and the real destination computer.  The Link-Local Multicast Name Resolution (LLMNR) is a protocol based on the Domain Name System (DNS) packet format that allows both IPv4 and IPv6 hosts to perform name resolution for hosts on the same local link. The multicast DNS (mDNS) protocol resolves hostnames to IP addresses within small networks that do not include a local name server. NetBIOS is a service that allows applications on computers to communicate with one another over a local area network (LAN)
[slide45.xml] Aircrack -ng (a suite of tools)
[slide46.xml] Mitigations ( aircrack -ng) So one of your best mitigations here is using long, strong passwords, especially in a home environment. Now, in a corporate environment, it is much better for you to use RADIUS authentication.  RADIUS authentication is a completely effective mitigation against tools like  aircrack -ng, because by using RADIUS, we are no longer going to be vulnerable to those password attacks across the network, because now we're using digital certificates as our method of authentication here.
[slide47.xml] Reaver A command line tool, used to perform brute force attacks against WPS-enabled access points. WPS is the Wifi Protected Setup mechanism (button) on your wireless router. You have a new printer and you have a long, strong password for your network. Well, it's really a difficult to start typing in that long, strong password into a printer when you have to use just up and down arrow keys.  That’s what WPS is for.  You push the button on your router, and the button on your printer, and if you do both of those within 60 seconds, they would auto communicate, pass the password back and forth, and then they would reconfigure themselves.  This is great for operations, horrible for security.  The problem is WPS uses a pin and that pin can be brute forced.  With WPS, you can do brute force attempts against that pin and crack it within several hours generally.  The reason is that pin is only eight digits long. In addition to that, the way that pin is actually calculated is actually a four plus four number.  So they actually take that eight digit pin and break it up into two sets of four. So if we want to brute force that we're really only having to try 10,000 combinations and 10,000 combinations, and my computer can go through 20,000 combinations pretty darn quickly.  And so that's the idea of why this is such a weak thing to use. 
[slide48.xml] Mitigation (Reaver) You can mitigate this by enabling rate-limiting for pin authentications.  Now, what does that mean? Well, when I try to brute force the pin here, my system is actually going to connect to your access point and try pin 0000 one, then it's going to try the next pin, 0000two. Well, if you put a wait time in between there of 15 seconds, or 30 seconds, or three minutes, you can actually have that backup delay and that delay makes it so it takes me infinitely longer for me to  get that password cracked. If you have no delay, I can get through 10,000 attempts in a few minutes.    But if you put in a 30-second delay each time, it's now going to take me several hours or several days to crack that pin.  The problem is even with doing this mitigation, you're just going to frustrate the attacker.  But if they're determined and you have WPS enabled, they are going to get in eventually, because again, there's only 10,000 plus 10,000, or 20,000 options here for them to be able to guess.   So what do I recommend? If you have WPS turn it off on your networks, it is bad, bad, bad. It is a huge vulnerability. 
[slide49.xml] Hashcat A command line tool used to perform brute force and dictionary attacks against password hashes.   In the modern era attackers don’t rely on  slow CPUs, they rely on the GPUs which are designed for mathematical problems, they relay on a lot of them the more the better. If you have enough GPUs put together you can brute force almost anything. Hashcat  –m  hashcat  type –a  Attackmode  –o Output file   InputHashfile give it the hash type, for instance MD5, then -a and the attack mode, do you want to do brute force or dictionary attack, -o and you give it the output file, where do you want to save your information to, and then you have to give it an input file with all those hashes.
[slide50.xml] DNS Harvesting  DNS harvesting enables hackers to gain access to the DNS records of an organization, which can then be used to map the organization’s network.  DNS harvesting methods include exploiting Whois, unauthorized zone transfers, Windows Tracert, or the UNIX traceroute tool.  These harvesting methods transfer data from servers which have not been setup standards.  A Security Analyst can control unauthorized transfers by specifying the DNS servers where zone transfers can take place.
[slide51.xml] Practice DNS harvesting   https://digi.ninja/projects/zonetransferme.php
[slide52.xml] DNS Enumeration Is the process of locating all information about DNS.  This can include identifying internal and external DNS servers and performing lookups of DNS records for information such as usernames, computer names, and IP addresses of potential target systems and performing zone transfers. The most straightforward way is to use  Nslookup  Tools for DNS enumeration  Other tools include the following: DigDug WhereIsIP NetInspector Men and Mice Management Console
[slide53.xml] What information can they find in DNS records Type Label SRV Service Defines the host name and port number of servers providing specific services, such as Directory  Services SOA Start  of Authority Identifies the primary name server for the zone.  Contains  the name of the server responsible for all DNS records within the name space. PTR Pointer Provides an IP address to a hostname (providing for reverse DNS lookups). NS Name Server This  record defines the name servers within your namespace.  These servers are the ones that respond to your client’s requests for name resolution. MX Mail Exchange This  record identifies your email servers within your domain. CNAME Canonical  Name This record provides for domain name aliases  within your zone.  For example you may have an FTP service and Web service running on the same IP address. CNAME records could be used to list both DNS for you. A Address Maps an IP address to an  Host Name and is most often used for DNS Lookups.

[slide55.xml] DNS Attacks
[slide56.xml] Preventing MAC Spoofing, ARP and DNS poisoning Mac Filtering:  limits the number of MAC addresses that can connect to a port.  It can also limit by specific MAC addresses, as well.  If a Cisco device  it can use  Dynamic ARP Inspection (DAI) .   DAI  is a Cisco security feature that validates ARP traffic.   DAI can intercept, record, and discard ARP packets with invalid IP-to-MAC address bindings.   This capability protects the network from some man-in-the-middle attacks.  IP Source Guard   Restricts IP traffic on switches and DNS servers.  Helps prevent IP spoofing attacks when a host tries to spoof and use the IP address of another  host.  IP Source Guard can be particularly useful in guarding against DNS poisoning and DNS spoofing. DNS  spoofing can also be defeated by using  DNS Security Extensions  (DNSSEC).  It digitally signs all DNS replies to ensure their validity.

Malware developers and distributors often spread malware through spam email.  Cyber criminals frequently use bulk emails to infect target systems with ransomware or financial malware.  Using an email harvesting method enables a Security Analyst to detect whether systems on a network are compromised.
