[slide50.xml] Regional Internet Registries (RIR) Domain name registration is handled by a series of Regional Internet Registries (RIR) that manage, distribute, and register public IP addresses within their respective regions.  RIR Region of Control ARIN North and South America and sub-Saharan Africa APNIC Asia and Pacific  RIPE  Europe, Middle East, and parts of Africa  LACNIC Latin America and the Caribbean AfriNIC RIR to support Africa
[slide51.xml] Querying the RIR databases  Whois is a utility that queries the RIRs and returns:  network ip range, the domain ownership, address, location, phone number, and  DNS name servers .  Whois is built into the Linux operating system. From the Linux prompt, users can type in  whois domainname.com  or  whois?  to get a list of various options.  Windows users have to use a third-party tool or website to obtain Whois information. 
[slide52.xml] What is and Why DNS 52
[slide53.xml] 53
[slide54.xml] Namespace vs Zone A companie’s Namespace is its entire network including its web presence. A DNS system can have multiple DNS servers.  Each server is responsible for holding and managing the records for its own area, known as a zone.  So a namespace can have multiple zones. The beauty of the system is that each server only has to worry about the name records for its own portion of the namespace (zone) and to know how to contact the server "above" it. The words zone and Namespace are sometimes used interchangeable.
[slide55.xml] DNS Namespace DNS Namespace https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7fJwSLo65wo 7:40 minutes DNS Zones https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=833Qnc-7-ug 11:04 DNS - what are DNS ZONES ? (Step by Step Explained with EXAMPLES) https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JIwi6ii-rzI 13:14 minutes
[slide56.xml] DNS records Type Label SRV Service Defines the host name and port number of servers providing specific services, such as Directory  Services SOA Start  of Authority Identifies the primary name server for the zone.  Contains  the name of the server responsible for all DNS records within the name space. PTR Pointer Provides an IP address to a hostname (providing for reverse DNS lookups). NS Name Server This  record defines the name servers within your namespace.  These servers are the ones that respond to your client’s requests for name resolution. MX Mail Exchange This  record identifies your email servers within your domain. CNAME Canonical  Name This record provides for domain name aliases  within your zone.  For example you may have an FTP service and Web service running on the same IP address. CNAME records could be used to list both DNS for you. A Address Maps an IP address to an  Host Name and is most often used for DNS Lookups.
[slide57.xml] SOA record Contains the following information. Source host  -  host name of the SOA server Contact Email  - Email address of the person responsible for the zone file. Serial number  -  Revision number of the zone file (This increments each time the zone file changes.) Refresh Time  - The amount of time a secondary DNS server will wait before asking for updates Retry Time  - The amount of time a secondary server will wait to retry if the zone transfer fails. Expire Time  - The maximum amount time a secondary server will spend trying to complete zone transfer. TTL  - The minimum "time to live" for all records in the zone.  (If not updated by a zone transfer, they will perish.)
[slide58.xml] DNS records DNS record types explained https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6uEwzkfViSM 11:32 minutes
[slide59.xml] Nslookup The primary tool to query DNS servers is Nslookup.  Nslookup provides machine name and address information. Both Linux and Windows have Nslookup clients.  Nslookup is used by typing  nslookup  from the command line followed by an IP address or a machine name. Doing so causes Nslookup to return the name, all known IP addresses, and all known CNAMES for the identified machine.  Nslookup queries DNS servers for machine name and address information.
[slide60.xml] Warning A computer manager named David Ritz was successfully prosecuted in 2008 for querying a DNS server.
[slide61.xml] Using NSLookup https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8WIHluQaEbs 10:20 minutes Using NSLOOKUP to Troubleshoot DNS Issues https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ZjzG9FVEQaA 7:25 minutes NSLOOKUP and How It Works With DNS Records -  Lifewire https://www.lifewire.com/what-is-nslookup-817516 Nslookup  - Common Usage Examples - The Sysadmins https://blog.thesysadmins.co.uk/nslookup-common-usage-examples.html
[slide62.xml] Whois vs Nslookup  To gather information (again this is reconnaissance) Remember: Whois  A tool that queries the RIRs and returns the domain ownership, address, location, phone number, and DNS name servers, ip network range.   Remember: Nslookup  A tool that that queries DNS servers to obtain domain name or IP address mapping or DNS record information.
[slide63.xml] Zone Transfers DNS records are managed by the authoritative (or primary) DNS server for your namespace. It is also known as the Start of Authority (SOA). It shares information with other secondary DNS servers which make up your namespace. The process of replicating records from the authoritative DNS server to secondary name servers within your namespace is known as a zone transfer.  It's obvious administrators need to be very careful about which machines are actually allowed to perform a zone transfer.  If you allowed just any machine to ask for a zone transfer, you might as well post a network map on your website.  Because of this most administrators restrict the ability to even ask for a zone transfer to a small list of name servers inside their network.
[slide64.xml] Ed what is the Zone Transfer process? DNS zone transfers use TCP port 53 . DNS zone transfers function as follows: The secondary name server starts the process by requesting the SOA record from the primary name server. The primary then checks the list of authorized servers, and if the secondary server’s name is on that list, the SOA record is sent. The secondary must then check the SOA record to see whether there is a match against the SOA it already maintains.  If the SOA is a match, the process stops here; however,  if the primary DNS server has a serial number that is higher than the secondary , the secondary will need an update and the zone transfer begins. The serial number indicates if changes were made since the last time the secondary server synchronized with the primary server.   If an update is required, the secondary name server will send an All Zone Transfer (AXFR) request to the primary server. Upon receipt of the AXFR, the primary server sends the entire zone file to the secondary name server.
[slide65.xml] DNS Cache Refresh rate Example.com. SOA hostmaster.sbc.net (950849 21600 3600 1728000 3600) Observe the final value of 3600 on that line.  That is the  TTL  value that would inform a hacker how long DNS poisoning would last.  3,600 seconds is 60 minutes. 
[slide66.xml] Practice DNS information gathering and other Stuff  https://digi.ninja/projects/zonetransferme.php
[slide67.xml] DNS Enumeration Is the process of locating all information about DNS.  This can include identifying internal and external DNS servers and performing lookups of DNS records for information such as usernames, computer names, and IP addresses of potential target systems and performing zone transfers. The most straightforward way is to use  Nslookup  Tools for DNS enumeration  Other tools include the following: DigDug WhereIsIP NetInspector Men and Mice Management Console
[slide68.xml] An example of using DNS (the user) A person is trying to connect to your FTP server to upload some data.   The user types in ftp.anycomp.com and hits ENTER.   The DNS server closet to the user (defined in your TCP/IP properties) looks through its cache to see if it knows the address for ftp.anycomp.com.   If it's not there, the server works its way through the DNS architecture to find the authoritative server for anycomp.com, who must have the correct IP address. This response is returned to the client and ftp-ing begins happily enough.
[slide69.xml] An example of using DNS (the attacker) The attacker might change the IP address of your FTP server in your DNS’s cache to point to an IP address that points to the attackers bogus FTP server. Now when a user attempts to obtain the IP address of your FTP server they get the IP address of the attackers bogus FTP server.  Now the user, none the wiser, would connect and upload the documents directly to the attackers bogus FTP server.   This process is known as  DNS poisoning , and one simple mitigation is to restrict the amount of time records can stay in cache before they are updated.
[slide70.xml] DNS Attacks DNS hijacking  When the attacker can gains access to your local DNS server and changes the DNS setting so that the incoming requests to the web server are redirected to a malicious site. DNS amplification  A DDoS, in which attackers use publically accessible open DNS servers to flood a target system with DNS response traffic.  The idea is to turn a very small DNS query into a huge payload directed at the target network.   DNS amplification attack is a reflection-based DDoS  attack. These attacks typically target DNS servers that support open recursive relay
[slide71.xml] Implementing DNS Snooping DNS spoofing occurs when a DNS server’s records are  “spoofed”  or altered maliciously to redirect traffic to the attacker.  This redirection of traffic allows the attacker to spread malware, steal data, etc. Question, which specific DNS record is spoofed?
[slide72.xml] DNS spoofing With DNS spoofing, the DNS server is given information about a web server it thinks is legitimate when it isn’t.  This sends users to websites other than the one they wanted to go to, reroute email, or other types of redirection (like a redirect a zone transfer). Another name for DNS spoofing is DNS poisoning. 
[slide73.xml] 73
[slide74.xml] Preventing MAC Spoofing, ARP and DNS poisoning Mac Filtering:  limits the number of MAC addresses that can connect to a port.  It can also limit by specific MAC addresses, as well.  If a Cisco device  it can use  Dynamic ARP Inspection (DAI) .   DAI  is a Cisco security feature that validates ARP traffic.   DAI can intercept, record, and discard ARP packets with invalid IP-to-MAC address bindings.   This capability protects the network from some man-in-the-middle attacks.  IP Source Guard   Restricts IP traffic on switches and DNS servers.  Helps prevent IP spoofing attacks when a host tries to spoof and use the IP address of another  host.  IP Source Guard can be particularly useful in guarding against DNS poisoning and DNS spoofing. DNS  spoofing can also be defeated by using  DNS Security Extensions  (DNSSEC).  It digitally signs all DNS replies to ensure their validity.

What does DNS HIJACKING  https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dBIHIvaRYLM 9:59 DNS Hijacking tutorial by Witch3r - YouTube https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yvXQawK9DrU 4:16 DNS Hijacking (Part 1) - YouTube https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4HkRpCBcXoE 2:57 DNS Amplification Attack - YouTube https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xTKjHWkDwP0 3:00 DNS Amplification Attack - YouTube https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wjjAPv8olvk 1:22
[slide76.xml] Review an email header Received: from senderhostname [aaa.aaa.aaa.aaa] (helo=[ senderhostname]) by mailserver.senderdomain.tld with esmtpa (Exim x.xx) (envelope-from <sender@senderdomain.tld) id xxxxx-xxxxxx-xxxx for recipient@recipientdomain.tld; Tue, 12 Apr 2011 20:36:08 -0100  The message was sent from the senders computer with the IP address aaa.aaa.aaa.aaa to the mail server of the sender.  Received: from mailserver.senderdomain.tld ([bbb.bbb.bbb.bbb] helo=mailserver.senderdomain.tld) by mailexchanger.recipientdomain.tld with esmtp id xxxxxx-xxxxxx-xx The message was transferred from the senders mail server with the IP address bbb.bbb.bbb.bbb to the recipients mail-exchanger. The mail-exchanger is the mail server, which accepts incoming messages for a domain.  Received: from mailexchanger.recipientdomain.tld([ccc.ccc.ccc.ccc])  by mailserver.recipientdomain.tld running ExIM with esmtp id xxxxxx-xxxxxx-xxx; Wed, 13 Apr 2011 01:39:23 +0200 The message was finally received by the recipients mail server from the the recipients mail exchanger ccc.ccc.ccc.ccc. The message routing can contain much more steps, depending on the used mail provider. It should always be possible to see the sender computer IP address aaa.aaa.aaa.aaa and the sender mail server bbb.bbb.bbb.bbb, if the message was sent from a mail client and a client computer. If the message was sent from a webmail client, then the real IP address of the sender is not included - in this case aaa.aaa.aaa.aaa (if any) will be the IP address of the webmail.

[slide80.xml] Traceroute and Ping Traceroute (tracert) Utility used to determine the path to a target computer In Linux called traceroute, in Windows, it is known as tracert.  TTL functions as a decrementing counter.  Each hop that a datagram passes through reduces the TTL field by one.  If the TTL value reaches 0, the datagram is discarded.  The source then sends another datagram with a TTL value greater than the one it sent before.  It will continue this process until the datagram finally reaches its destination. Remember:  Linux traceroute is uses UDP, and Windows uses ICMP. The most common ICMP type in ICMP is Ping: Type 8 and Code 0 for request Type 0 and Code 0 for reply  A ping is useful to determine connectivity.
[slide81.xml] Graphical user interfaces (GUI) for Traceroute LoriotPro: is a professional and scalable SNMP manager and network monitoring solution that enables availability and performance control of your networks, systems, and smart infrastructures. The graphical display shows you the route between you and the remote site, including all intermediate nodes and their registrant information. Trout: Trout is another visual traceroute and Whois program. What’s great about this program is its speed. Unlike traditional traceroute programs, Trout performs parallel pinging. By sending packets with more than one TTL at a time, it can quickly determine the path to a targeted device. VisualRoute: VisualRoute is another graphical traceroute for Windows. VisualRoute not only shows a graphical world map that displays the path packets are taking, but also lists information for each hop, including IP address, node name, and geographic location
[slide82.xml] Identifying Active Machines One of the most basic methods of identifying active machines is to perform a ping sweep.   Ping uses ICMP and works by sending an echo request to a system and waiting for the target to send an echo reply back.  If the target device is unreachable, a request time out is returned.  Ping is a useful tool to identify active machines and to measure the speed at which packets are moved from one host to another or to get details like the TTL. Figure 3-7 shows a ping capture from a Windows computer. Programs that will perform ping sweeps include the following: Angry IP Scanner Hping WS_Ping_ProPack Network scan tools Super Scan Nmap
[slide83.xml] Finding Open Ports and Access Points Port scanning is the process of connecting to TCP and UDP ports for the purpose of finding what services and applications are running on the target device.  After running applications, open ports, and services are discovered, the hacker can then determine the best way to attack the system.
[slide84.xml] Warning Scanning someone’s network can be considered illegal.

[slide88.xml] Three-way handshake 1. The client sends the server a TCP packet with the sequence number flag ( SYN flag ) set and an initial sequence number (ISN). 2. The server replies by sending a packet with the  SYN/ACK  flag set to the client.  The synchronize sequence number flag informs the client that it would like to communicate with it, and the acknowledgment flag informs the client that it received its ISN. The client’s ISN (now called an acknowledgment number) will be one digit higher than the client’s ISN. The server generates it’s own ISN, as well, to keep track of every byte sent to the client. 3. When the client receives the server’s packet, it creates an  ACK  packet to acknowledge that the data has been received from the server. At this point, communication can begin.
[slide89.xml] Three way handshake SYN Sequence # 105 Three way handshake SYN/ACK Your Acknowledgement  # 106 My    Sequence # 223 ACK Your Acknowledgement # 224 My    Sequence # 106

Designed for stealth but my  be picked up by IDS. XMAS  FIN, URG, PSH No response RST/ACK Doesn’t work on Windows machines. FIN FIN No response RST/ACK Doesn’t work on Windows machines NULL No response RST/ACK Doesn’t work on Windows machines ACK ACK RST No response Used in firewalls filter tests

[slide96.xml] (Idle) Zombie Scan Every IP packet on the Internet has a fragment identification number (IPID).  Since many operating systems simply increment this number for each packet they send, probing for the IPID can tell an attacker how many packets have been sent since the last probe. An IPID probe can be accomplished by sending a SYN/ACK to the recipient and it will respond with a RST and IPID number.
[slide97.xml] (Idle) Zombie Scan Probe the zombie's IPID and record it. Forge a SYN packet from the zombie and send it to the desired port on the target.  Probe the zombie's IPPD again. The target port state is then determined by comparing this new IPID with the one recorded in step 1. An IPID increase of 1, port is closed. An IPID increase of 2, port is open.
[slide98.xml] (Idle) Zombie Scan
[slide99.xml] UDP Port Scanning UDP does not have flags, nor does UDP issue responses. It’s a fire and forget protocol! The most you can hope for is a response from ICMP. If the port is closed, ICMP attempts to send an ICMP type 3 code 3 port unreachable message to the source of the UDP scan. But, if the network is blocking ICMP, no error message is returned. Therefore, the response to the scans might simply be no response.  If you are planning on doing UDP scans, plan for unreliable results.
[slide100.xml] Nmap  Available for Windows and Linux as a GUI and command-line program.  It is probably the most widely used port scanner ever developed. It can do many types of scans and OS identification.  It also enables you to control the speed of the scan from slow to insane.   Nmap is considered a required tool for all ethical hackers.
[slide101.xml] Common Nmap Scan Types - sS  TCP SYN stealth port scan  - sT  TCP connect() port scan  - sU  UDP port scan - sP  ping scan (Find any reachable machines) - sF,-sX,-sN  Stealth FIN, Xmas, or Null scan  - sV  Version scan probes open ports determining service and app names/versions - O  Use TCP/IP fingerprinting to guess remote operating system - p  <range> ports to scan. Ex. range: '1-1024,1080,6666,31337' - v  Verbose. Explain as much as possible
[slide102.xml] The six port states recognized by Nmap open   An application is actively listening .   closed   A closed port is accessible (it receives and responds to Nmap probe packets), but  there is no application listening on it.  They can be helpful in showing that a host is up on an IP address (host discovery, or ping scanning), and as part of OS detection. Because closed ports are reachable, it may be worth scanning later in case some open up. Administrators may want to consider blocking such ports with a firewall.   filtered   Nmap  cannot determine whether the port is open  because packet filtering prevents its probes from reaching the port.  unfiltered   The unfiltered state means that  a port is accessible, but Nmap is unable to determine whether it is open or closed.  Only the ACK scan, which is used to map firewall rulesets, classifies ports into this state. Scanning unfiltered ports with other scan types such as Window scan, SYN scan, or FIN scan, may help resolve whether the port is open.  open|filtered   Nmap places ports in this state when it is  unable to determine whether a port is open or filtered.  This occurs for scan types in which open ports give no response. The lack of response could also mean that a packet filter dropped the probe or any response it elicited. So Nmap does not know for sure whether the port is open or being filtered.
[slide103.xml] Other scanning tools SuperScan Runs on Windows machines.  THC-Amap  Runs on Linux Scanrand Runs on Linux.  Hping Runs on Windows and Linux computers and can function as a packet builder. Hping2 and 3 can be used for firewall testing, identifying honeypots, and port scanning.  Hping3 syntax examples of note: Ping sweep: hping3 -1 IP_Address UDP scan: hping3 -2 IP_Address SYN scan: hping3 -8 IP_Address ACK scan: hping3 -A IP_Address XMAS scan: hping3 -F -P -U IP_Address
[slide104.xml] Port Knocking Port knocking is a stealth method to externally open ports blocked by a firewall. It works by sending connection attempts to a series of closed ports. When the correct sequence of port "knocks" (connection attempts) is received, the firewall opens certain port(s) to allow a connection.  The disadvantages include the fact that the technique does not harden the underlining application. Finally, anyone who has the ability to sniff the network traffic will be in possession of the appropriate knock sequence.
[slide105.xml] Port Knocking Port Knocking https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=a7VJZEJVhD0 3:17
[slide106.xml] War dialing and driving  War dialing Using a modem and software to scan for other systems with modems attached.  Modems are a tempting target for hackers because they offer them the opportunity to bypass the corporate firewall. War Driving War driving is named after war dialing as it is the process of looking for open access points. The goal is to identify open or rogue access points
[slide107.xml] Passive OS Fingerprinting  (Using IP and TCP header fields to fingerprint the OS) The IP TTL value:  Different OSs set the TTL to different values. The TCP window size: Initial Window size: OS vendors use different values for the initial window size. The IP DF option:  Not all OS vendors handle fragmentation in the same way.  The IP Type of Service (TOS) option :  Not all vendors implement this option in the same way. Other items that can be examined include IP identification number (IPID), IP options, TCP options, and even ICMP. An example of a passive fingerprinting tool is the Linux-based tool P0f. 
[slide108.xml] Active fingerprinting More powerful than passive fingerprint scanning because the hacker doesn’t have to wait for random packets.   Faster, and more accurate. The disadvantage is that active fingerprinting is not as stealthy as passive fingerprinting.
[slide109.xml] Active fingerprinting tools  Nmap Fingerprinting  the OS with Nmap is initiated with  -O  option.  Nmap needs one open and one closed port to make an accurate determination of what OS a particular system is running.  Xprobe 2 is a Linux-based Winfingerprint. A Windows-based tool  Hping
[slide110.xml] Fingerprinting Services Knowing what services are running on specific ports allows the hacker to formulate and launch application-specific attacks.  Knowing the common default ports and services and using tools such as Telnet, FTP, and Netcat are two ways to ensure success at this pre-attack stage by banner grabbing. NMAP – sV:  Version scan probes open ports determining service and app names/versions
[slide111.xml] Telnet Banner Grabbing C:\ >telnet 192.168.1.102 80 HTTP/1.1 400 Bad Request Server: Microsoft-IIS/6.0 Date: Fri, 07 Oct 2012 22:22:04 GMT Content-Type: text/html Content-Length: 87 <html><head><title>Error</title></head><body>The parameter is incorrect. </body> </html> Connection to host lost.
[slide112.xml] ACK Flag Probe Two versions of the ACK flag Probe: TTL version: If the TTL field value in the returned RST packet is less than 64, port is open. Window Size version: If the Window size field in the returned RST packet has anything other than zero, the port is open. The ACK flag Probe can also be used to check for a firewall.  If an ACK is sent and no response, this indicates the a Stateful firewall is present.  If a RST comes back there is no firewall.
[slide113.xml] Mapping the Network Attack Surface Manual Mapping Self explanitory Automated Mapping SolarWinds Network Topology Nmap  NLog  allows you to keep all of your Nmap scan logs in a database, making it possible to easily search for specific entries. CartoReso  Another network mapping option
[slide114.xml] Evading Detection Fragment packets: Tell nmap to fragment the packets so that the IDS can’t recognize them.  You need to specifically split the TCP header. IP address decoy: Obfuscate the real source of the scan by hiding it among a whole bunch of decoy source addresses.  Make is appear the decoys and the target are scanning. Using Proxies Tor (The Onion Routing) Uses other machines to send your packets and encryption.  Use an anonymizer to hide your identity

[slide3.xml] Is Scanning Necessary? If you have documentation on what is being tested, should you still conduct scanning? Why? Documentation is likely wrong Determine the actual target topology Firewalls, Routers, Servers, Clients, etc. Identify Live Systems Collect IP addresses Determine System Specifics What OS and version is running Open ports and running services Determine existing vulnerabilities And much more
[slide4.xml] Types of Scanning Network Mapping and Probing Sweeps: Find live hosts and determine IP addresses Tracing: Mapping the network topology Port Scanning Determine open, closed, filtered, etc. ports Fingerprinting Determine OS, banners, etc. based on response and behaviors (Enumeration) Version Scanning Determine version of service/protocol Vulnerability Scanning Identify misconfigurations, vulnerable services, missing patches, etc. 
[slide5.xml] Name or IP address? Scanning by the target name is problematic Why? Networks could use DNS as Load Balancers Thus, you could be scanning more than one system while you assume it is only one Scan results will not be accurate May affect the exploitation phase Creating a netcat listener Generating a specific attack on that system
[slide6.xml] Time is of the Essence In many cases, you have a large number of targets to scan Let’s do the math: 500 systems 1 second per port scan (being modest, sometimes scans must be low and slow to avoid detection) [(65,536 TCP ports * 1) * 500] = 32,768,000 sec = 1.1 years Not counting UDP 1 second per port is modest Doesn’t account for dropped packets or for low bandwidth So what do we do? Note: There are 65,536 TCP Ports and the same for UDP
[slide7.xml] Large Target Scans Conduct a Distributed Scan Can reduce the length of time to a couple of weeks Most cases we won’t have that much time Use Faster Scanning Methods Lower time-outs values (Con: could miss packet status) Some scanners (e.g. –  hping ) send packets faster Separates sending and receiving mechanisms so the sender doesn’t have to wait for time-outs  Conduct a Random/Select Sampling Find targets that are a good representation of environment (e.g. – same configs, services, etc.) Port Scan Reduction Only scan a specific set of ports requested by the customer Customer could provide firewall rules – thus reducing the number of ports scanned
[slide8.xml] Always Use Sniffers Always use a sniffer while scanning Why? You could encounter various issue: Helps identify false positives May need to review sent/received packets for issues
[slide9.xml] Sniffing During a Scan Which scanner should I use? Easy to use, configurable, fast and flexible, low cost Need to be able to quickly filter packets Wireshark, tcpdump, tshark, etc.  Should be something you are comfortable with What does it need to do?  You don’t have to save every packet Requires a lot of space, but you should May be a good idea if you are doing an incident response assessment Just need to display data on screen for reference Make sure the scanning tool is working properly Test it before the event Do you need a cheat sheet? Have it handy/readily available

[slide35.xml] Network Mapping Standard Process DNS -> Ping -> Traceroute -> Firewalking Network Mapping Goals Need to know what the network topology looks like Helps determine the best attack method Where are the openings to get a footing Lets you see what to avoid What systems are alive What is the addressing  Potential Issues: Firewalls, Tarpits, Bluecoats, Load Balancers, Honeynets/pots, … May involve OS fingerprinting, port scanning, etc. We could just “ping” IP addresses to see if systems are alive More often than not, ping is actually disabled

[slide41.xml] Network Mapping Tools hping is a command-line oriented TCP/IP packet assembler/analyzer Besides sending ICMP echo requests (like ping) It supports TCP, UDP, and RAW-IP protocols Has a traceroute mode Can send files between a covered channel More uses of hping: Firewall testing Advanced Port scanning Remote OS fingerprinting Remote uptime guessing And much more hping works on: Linux, FreeBSD, NetBSD, OpenBSD, Solaris, Mac OS X, and Windows
[slide42.xml] Network Mapping Tools Free tool at  http://hping.org/ It is a packet generating tool, so we can: Conduct network sweeps with different protocols Perform tracerouting and port scanning Supports Tool Command Language (TCL) scripting, command-line & shell scripts TCl is pronounced “tickle” or tee-see-el By default, it will send a ping to a target with no control bits set and uses TCP as the default protocol Can also use --udp, --tcp, --rawip  Example: #	hping3 --udp 92.168.10.10
[slide43.xml] Hping – Setting Control Bits By default, control bits are set to 0 Although it is easy to set control bits --syn, --ack, --fin, --rst, --urg, --push, etc.  We can also set: TTL’s, IP ID numbers, checksums, and TCP sequence numbers Nice Features: + will increment destination port by 1 on each packet (received) ++ will increase destination port in packets by 1 (sent)  During the scan, press [ctrl+z] to increase the port #
[slide44.xml] hping Option List Available options: -s  --baseport    	base source port             (default random) -p  --destport    	[+][+]<port> destination port(default 0) ctrl+z inc/dec -k  --keep        	keep still source port -w  --win         	winsize (default 64) -O  --tcpoff      	set fake tcp data offset     (instead of tcphdrlen / 4) -Q  --seqnum     	shows only tcp sequence number -b  --badcksum    	(try to) send packets with a bad IP checksum many systems will fix the 				IP checksum sending the packet so you'll get bad UDP/TCP checksum 				instead. -M  --setseq      	set TCP sequence number -L  --setack      	set TCP ack -c  --count      	packet count  -i  --interval   	wait (uX for X microseconds, for example -i u1000)   --fast       	alias for -i u10000 (10 packets for second)   --faster     	alias for -i u1000 (100 packets for second) --flood       	sent packets as fast as possible. Don't show replies. -n  --numeric    	numeric output -I  --interface  	interface name (otherwise default routing interface)
[slide45.xml] Target Options Can send packets via a selected IP address or domain name or randomly Use --rand-dest [IP Address] Must enter address with an “x” in the IP address e.g. – 192.168.10.x will send to 192.168.10.1 – 255 You must specify the interface -I [int] or --interface  Example: # hping3 --rand-dest 192.168.10.x -I eth0  hping does not support selecting a specific range of IP addresses by itself You could always write a script Be careful with this, as you could cause a DoS on stateful firewalls by overwhelming it with packets
[slide46.xml] Explain Shell Example
[slide47.xml] hping IP Ranges If you want to send a specific range of IP addresses, then you could do the following: #  for x in $(seq 100 200); do hping3 --count 1 192.168.10.”${x}”; done; This will iterate from 192.168.10.100 through 192.168.10.200 Result will provide a lot of responses; it may be better to grep through it Lets check the output for  ip = (followed by address) Also, lets discard the standard error messages which is 2>/dev/null We need to pipe it to grep searching for  ip = #  for x in $(seq 100 200); do hping3 --count 1 192.168.10.${x} 2>/dev/null | grep  ip =; done;

[slide50.xml] ExplainShell  Example
[slide51.xml] Spoofing and Speed hping can also perform packet spoofing      - Use options	-a [addr] or --spoof [addr] - Example: # hping -a 192.168.10.50 10.10.10.10 hping send packets at 1 packet per second   -i  --interval  wait (uX for X microseconds, for example -i u1000)       --fast      	    alias for -i u10000 (10 packets for second)       --faster    	    alias for -i u1000 (100 packets for second)       --flood      	    sent packets as fast as possible. Don't show replies.
[slide52.xml] It’s Alive! We can use the --beep option to monitor a connection Acts as a pulse/life-line Let’s send am ICMP Echo Request every 5 sec. #  hping3 --icmp --interval 5 --beep 192.168.10.10 Don’t want to send too many SYN packets Could result in a DoS (SYN Flood) from numerous half-open connections to the target
[slide53.xml] Activity: hping3 In this activity, we will execute hping3. Launch two terminals and type the following command: In the first terminal, run: $  ifconfig $  tcpdump  - Xn  not  arp In the second terminal, run: $ hping3 -a 192.168.10.55 10.10.10.150 What do you see in the  tcpdump  out? What is the source IP address? What is the destination IP address?
[slide54.xml] Traceroute/Tracert
[slide55.xml] Manual Mapping by TTL TTL
[slide56.xml] Traceroute Intro A computer diagnostic tool on Linux/Unix By Van Jacobson Displays a network path or route Measures delays of packets tracert – Windows companion Same basic functionality Sends sequence of Internet Control Message Protocol (ICMP) to a destination address Works by increasing the TTL (hop limit) of each packet sent When it reaches the destination, it returns an ICMP Echo Reply message Timestamp values are returned on each packet (milliseconds) 
[slide57.xml] Behavior If a certain hop doesn’t return an ICMP response Will label that hop with a “*” – no information known This means it filtered Everything thereafter will be filled with a “*” By default: Uses UDP packet (can specify TCP if wanted) Send 3 sets of packets before increasing TTL Hop is measured 3 times by default Starts with port 33434 and increment from there If the target network blocks UDP packets Use the tracert tool
[slide58.xml] traceroute Options Available options:
[slide59.xml] traceroute Intoduction Has fewer options than traceroute By default, uses ICMP Echo Request messages Each hop is measured 3 times (just like traceroute) tracert uses milliseconds vs traceroute’s seconds
[slide60.xml] Activity: tracert/traceroute In this activity, we will run the tracert/traceroute command. On your system: Windows: Open a command prompt  [Windows Key + r] Windows: >  cmd Windows: >  tracert  pgcc.edu Linux: Open a terminal Linux: $  traceroute pgcc.edu What do you see? What happened?
[slide61.xml] Discovery by Different Means What is firewalking? Developed by Mike Schiffman and David Goldsmith Is a technique for testing the vulnerability of a firewall and mapping the routers that are behind the firewalls It is a method disguising port scans Similar to tracerouting by sending TCP/UDP packets into the firewall that have a TTL set at 1 hop greater than the target If the packet makes it through the gateway, it is forwarded to the next hop where the TTL = 0 and causes a TTL “exceeded in transit” message – then the packet is discarded Using this method, access information on the firewall can be determined if the successive probe packets are sent.
[slide62.xml] Shunned :’( If you had connection replies and now you don’t… Use another source to check the target Be careful, you are possibly showing anyone who is listening on the Internet that there is an interest in this IP address This should be addresses in RoE Can be done from various websites: http://www.tracert.org/ http://traceroute.org/
[slide63.xml] LFT LFT – Fast, multi-protocol traceroute engine Performs AS number lookups via Regional Internet registries and other sources Makes use of Loose Source Routing, firewall, and load balancer detection LSR is an IP option for address translation via source routing The destination of the packet is replaced with the next router the packet is going This is equivalent to tunneling Called loose source because only part of the path is set in advance Opposite from strict source routing in which each step is delineated  LFT’s functions: Send various TCP SYN, FIN,, and UDP layer 4 probes with the TTL to elicit the ICMP Time Exceeded response from each gateway along the route LFT is now pre-built into traceroute
[slide64.xml] A Better Mapping Method Has a trecroute capability (--traceroute) Nmap works backward from the target Sends packets (with correct protocol) to the target with a TTL gathered from an earlier scan If the TTL was incorrect and you receive an ICMP Time Exceeded Message, the TTL is incremented Basically, moves up or down to acquire the right TTL The TTL is then decremented by 1 Sends the packet like in step 1 Rinse and repeat until all hops are mapped It get better because it is efficient
[slide65.xml] Smarter Mapping If you move to the next target, nmap is smart It already knows the previous router hops So it works backward from the next target Below shows the nmap progress through 3 targets 1 st  track in Green from target #1 (1-4) 2 nd  track in Red from target #2 (5-6) 3 rd  track in Purple from target #3 (7-8)
[slide66.xml] fping One of the most basic steps in mapping networks Ping is traditionally used Sending ICMP Echo (type 8) to a target in order to get An ICMP Reply (type 0) – shows the target is alive Ping is fine for small/mid-sized networks but not large Class C = 245 and B = 65,534 potential hosts This will take many days to complete We can perform a ping sweep with  fping Much faster than ping Can be accomplished in 2 ways: You can feed a series of IP addresses from standard input Read from a  filw fping  options: -a	shows only alive hosts -d 	resolves hostnames -f	helpful for scripting sweeps/read in a file -h	help (list options) Examples: fping  -a -f input.txt nmap  - sP  -PT [port #] [IP address ]
[slide67.xml] Port Scanning Here’s NMAP
[slide68.xml] Top Ports What are the top ports? 80/http 25/smtp 22/ssh 443/https 21/ftp 113/auth 23/telnet 53/domain 554/rtsp 3389/ms-term-server 
[slide69.xml] nmap Security scanner written by Gordon Lyon Pseudonym: Fyodor  Vaskovich Open Source – Available at  www.nmap.org Used to discover hosts and services on a network Thus creating a network map or “ nmap ” Runs on various OS’s Linux, Windows, Solaris, HP-UX, BSD, OS X, etc. Zenmap  is a GUI add-on to  nmap Various web-based interfaces are available Control  nmap  remotely from a web browser LOCALSCAN,  nmap -web, and  nmap -CGI Windows has  NMapWin  and  NMapW
[slide70.xml] Zenmap
[slide71.xml] Xnmap  for Mac
[slide72.xml] nmap Features Originally developed as a port scanner Has grown tremendously over the years Conducts ping sweeps OS fingerprinting Nmap Scripting Engine (NSE) Conduct Vulnerability Scanning Hollywood Movie Appearances  Matrix Reloaded Born Ultimatum  Die Hard 4 Nmap is a super powerful tool
[slide73.xml] Supports 6 Port States Open Application is accepting TCP connections, UDP datagrams, or SCTP associations on the port Closed Port is accessible, but no application is listening on it Filtered Nmap can’t determine if the port is open because of packet filtering Filtering can be from a network firewall, router rules, or host firewall May respond with ICMP (type 3 code 13)
[slide74.xml] Supports 6 Port States Unfiltered The port is accessible but nmap is unable to determine if it is open or closed Only the ACK scan (used to map firewall rules) returns this status Using other scan types (Windows scan, SYN scan, or FIN scan) may help in identification Open/Filtered Assigned when nmap can’t determine if the port is open or filtered Open ports give no response (packets are dropped) Closed/Filtered nmap can’t determine whether port is open, closed, or filtered
[slide75.xml] nmap  Output Default output is to the screen, but can also send  nmap  output to a file: oN  [filename] Save output in normal format as shown on screen oG  [filename] Store in a grep format oX  [filename] Store output in XML format oA  [filename] Creates a normal, CML, and grep format in 3 file basename.nmap ,  basename.gnmap . Basename.xml There is one more, but it is not useful oS  [filename] Script-Kiddie format
[slide76.xml] On-the-fly Interactions While nmap is running, you can run these keys: v 		Increases verboseness Shift + v		Decreases verboseness p		Initiate packet tracing Shift + p		Cut-off packet tracing d		Increase debugging mode Shift + d		Decrease debugging mode All other keys display status message: % complete, elapsed time, remaining time
[slide77.xml] Timing and Performance Techniques for optimizing scan times Default is seconds, but can use: ‘ms’, ’s’, ’m’, or ‘h’ for milliseconds, seconds, minutes, or hours --min-hostgroup <numhosts>; --max-hostgroup <numhosts> Adjust parallel scan group size --min-parallelism <numprobes>; --max-parallelism <numprobes> Adjust probe parallelization --max-retries <numtries> Specify the maximum number of port scan probe retransmissions --host-timeout <time> Give up on slow target hosts, arguments 900000ms, 900, 900s, and 15m all do the same thing -T paranoid | sneaky | polite | normal | aggressive | insane Set a timing template
[slide78.xml] Using the –T Option Invoked by syntax: #  nmap  –T [option] 0 = paranoid : 		5 minutes between packets (Serial Scan) 1 = sneaky :		 Every 15 seconds (Serial Scan) 2 = polite :		 Every 0.4 seconds (Serial Scan) 3 = normal :		 Default (Parallel Scan) 4 = aggressive :		  Max wait time 1.25s for response 5 = insane :		  Max wait time 0.3s, Max wait per host Evade IDS Serial Scan   = 	1 at a time                                                                                   But very slow… Parallel Scan = 	Multiple packets to multiple targets
[slide79.xml] Address Probing nmap probes a target before scanning Done by default UID 0 users: If on the same subnet as the scanner, it will send an ARP request If on a different subnet, it will senc ICMP Echo Request TCP SYN to 443 and ACK 80 ICMP Timestamp Request of Type 13 All other UID’s Nmap will conduct a 3-way handshake TCP SYN to 80 and 443 (No ICMP used) Using the -Pn option will not ping the target before scanning (skips above default) -P0 was the old seitch, but was confused with -PO -PO is for all IP Protocol Pings
[slide80.xml] Network Sweeps nmap can just probe for target hosts Thus launching a network sweep scan 	#  nmap –sP [option(s)] Using -sP alone uses default probes (previous slide) Other options: Based on what is allowed into the network PB : (default) ICMP Echo Request, TCP SYN 443 and ACK to 80 If UID 0, then ICMP Timestamp Request -Pn : No ping (same as P0) -PS [ports] : Use TCP SYN to specified ports in the port list  -PE : Sends ICMP Echo Request (type 8) to get targets -PP : Sends ICMP Timestamp request (type 3) to get targets -PM : Sends ICMP address maske request (type 17) to get targets -PR : Must be on the same network, ARP ping
[slide81.xml] Port Scanning Doesn’t scan all 65,535 ports by default Doesn’t scan all common ports up to 1024 Only checks the top 1,000 ports for TCP/UDP Scan top 100 ports with  -F  switch (Fast) --top-ports [n]  option allows you to enter how many ports Can use –p option to tell exactly which ports to scan -p 0-1024,2222,3133,3132,5000-65535 Use T and U to indicate TCP or UDP By default, ports are scanned in random order  -r  option removes the random order
[slide82.xml] nmap  Activity Example
[slide83.xml] 3-Way Handshake
[slide84.xml] nmap  Connect Scan nmap  has many TCP scanning options Options are based on TCP control bits Most basic TCP Connect scan is  – sT Completes the 3-way handshake Connection is then RESET Con: Slower and is more likely to be logged Less control because it uses OS connect() call Pro: It can be run as root or as a limited user
[slide85.xml] nmap  SYN Scan The SYN scan a.k.a. half-open or stealth scan Uses the  – sS option  SYN-ACK response means it is open RST response means it’s closed No response means that it is filtered Usually this is not logged since there is no connection Firewall and IDPS systems may detect it Must use root privileges to invoke the comma
[slide86.xml] More TCP Scan Options ACK Scan ( - sA ) Used to get through a filter on a router Mostly good for mapping since its not reliable for determining open/closed ports FIN Scan ( - sF ) Use the FIN Control Bit on all scan packets Null Scan ( - sN )  Set Control Bits to 0 for Null Xmas Tree Scan ( - sX ) Uses FIN, PSH, and URG Maimon  Scan ( - sM ) Uses the FIN and ACK Control Bits
[slide87.xml] Xmas Scan with  nmap RFC793 – if a closed port gets a TCP packet without the SYN, RST, or ACK flag being set, it is supposed to respond with a RST packet If the port is open, the TCP stack is supposed to just drop the packet without giving a response Not all OSs follow the RFC This allows for OS fingerprinting nmap’s  XMAS scan option: sets only the FIN, PSH, URG flags (nothing else)
[slide88.xml] Customized Control Bit Scan Adjust your scan by setting your own control bits Use syntax: --scanflags Followed by options (no spaces): SYN,ACK,RST,FIN,PSH,ECE,CWR,URG,ALL,NONE Example: 	nmap --scanflags SYNPSHURG –p 80 192.168.10.100
[slide89.xml] Sending Bad Checksums We can also create packets with a bad checksum – why? The target will reject the packet and drop it Some firewalls and IDPS don’t calculate layer 4 checksums, so Might send an ICMP Port Unreachable or a RESET packet Meaning, if a response comes back, it is from a firewall or IDPS Called “Firewall Spotting” Can also look for a different TTL from SYN-ACK response for allowed services vs. the TTL on RESET responses from blocked services May be different due to initial TTL or if the same, the RESET is decreased because it is closer
[slide90.xml] nmap UDP Scans Has less number of options available Uses -sU option Sends without a payload Recent versions of nmap send a protocol specific payload to try and get a response - 7 (echo)	- 137 (netbios-ng)		- 2049 (nfs) - 53 (domain)	- 161 (snmp) - 111 (rcpbind)	- 500 (isakmp) - 123 (ntp)	- 1645/1812 (radius) All others have a blank payload
[slide91.xml] nmap UDP Scans nmap also checks to see if the target is throttling the ICMP response rate Remember Linux systems throttle responses Linux sends 1 response per second Do the math: if you have 65,535 ports = 18+ hours for only one system nmap attempts to detect ICMP rate limiting as they decrease Throttling lengthens scan time (e.g. closed ports = ICMP port unreachable response) Important Note: If the target system was setup with UDP listening on an unusual port… This method won’t find it, but it is not common
[slide92.xml] IPv6 Support IPv6 access is often left unsecured Firewalls and IDPS may not block IPv6 It’s usually auto configured on Windows, Linux, and OS X IPv6 are 128 bits or 16 bytes 4 groups of hex digits Double :: means to fill with zeroes (only used once in address) Local Loopback is ::1 0000:0000:0000:0000:0000:0000:0000:0001 nmap can use the -6 option for IPv6 scans Use with -sP, -sT, -sV Nmap doesn’t currently support IPv6 OS detection and random targets
[slide93.xml] IPv6 Targets Find targets by using Neighbor Discovery  Use ping6 command for broadcast addresses ping6 - i  eth0 ff02::1 	  Routers  ping6 - i  eth0 ff02::2     Nodes    Now we use the command  ip  neigh  to lookup neighbors Specify specific targets by 	####::####%[interface]      Example: fe80::20c0%eth0      - Then port scan it by using: 			$  nmap  - Pn  - sV  -6 fe80::20c0%eth0 --packet-trace
[slide94.xml] THE END

[slide5.xml] Xprobe OS Fingerprinting Xprobe2 Performs fingerprinting of remote TCP/IP stacks Based on Ofir Arkin’s ICMP fingerprinting research Uses fuzzy to calculate the OS https://sourceforge.net/projects/xprobe/files/xprobe2/ https://linux.die.net/man/1/xprobe2
[slide6.xml] p0f v3 (Passive Detection) p0f v3 – by The Evil Twin https://tools.kali.org/information-gathering/p0f A versatile passive OS fingerprinting utility Can identify servers, firewalls, IDSs, and Honeypots Capabilities include: Scalable and fast identification of the OS and software on both endpoints of a vanilla TCP connection Auto-detect of connection sharing (NAT, load balancing, and app-level preferences, etc.) Detection of clients/servers that forge declarative statements (e.g. – X-Mailer, User-Agent, etc.) User-Agent: Software acting on behalf of a user (e.g. – Mail user agent) X-Mailer: A line in an email header showing the software the sender used to send it

[slide15.xml] Vulnerability Scanning Once we know the OS and version We need to know the software vulnerabilities Starting with OS, but applications can be vulnerable Can determine based on the version to narrow down Although that version may be vulnerable, other mechanisms may be blocking the exploit Observe the behavior Sometimes we can’t get the version numbers Use tools that can determine a vulnerability based on its behavior that is consistent with a specific vulnerability Configuration Check If you gain access to the system, you can check the system for misconfigurations that show weaknesses Ultimately you can try an exploit against it Could cause the system to crash, HBSS to kick off alerts or preventive measure
[slide16.xml] Scanners Are the results from vulnerability scanning tools enough? No, but it is a good start Scanning tools are limited They can help us take the services discovered and research for evidence of vulnerabilities These tools can’t have an extensive and adaptive conversation to gather deeper intel We can use tools like nmap’s NSE
[slide17.xml] Using nmap NSE nmap Scripting Engine (NSE) Functions: Allow for arbitrary messages to be sent/received by nmap to multiple targets (run scripts in parallel) Easily extendable with community developed scripts Support extended network discovery (whois, DNS, etc.) Perform more advanced version detection  Conduct vulnerability scanning Detect infected or back-doored systems Exploit discovered vulnerabilities
[slide18.xml] nmap Engine Scripts Written in Lua programming language It is fast, free, has a small interpreter that works across platforms, easily embedded inside apps, also used in games Invoke NSE by: Run all scripts in default category  	#  nmap -sC [target] -p [port #] Run an individual scropt #  nmap --script-trace[all,category,dir,script,..] [target] -p [port #] You can also add –script-trace Detailed output from the scripts
[slide19.xml] NSE Script Types and Phases NSE Supports 4 types of scripts Perule Scripts Host Scripts Service Scripts Postrule Scripts Often located in their own script subdirectory Scripts are indexed in a database stored in scripts/script.db Argument “all” will execute all scripts in the nmap script database Use cautiously because it may contain exploits, DoS attacks, etc. You can search this directory for specifics # cat script.db | grep safe  wc -l Finds scripts files (.nse) that match the “safe” category

[slide24.xml] Enumeration  An in-depth analysis of targeted computers.  Enumeration is  performed by actively connecting  to each system to identify the user accounts, system accounts, services, and other system details. 
[slide25.xml] NULL Sessions Windows Null SMB Session What is a Null SMB A session that has no (blank) user ID/password/Domain Membership SMB operates  ans  an application-layer network protocol Provides shared access to files, printers, and serial ports Server Message Block a.k.a. Common Internet File System (CIFS) Try to establish a manual Null Session net use \\[IP  addr ] “” /u: “” XP/Vista/7 Registry value = zero HKLM\System\ CurrentControlSet \Control\ Lsa \ RestrictAnonymousSAM This may be set to 0 by admin to support certain applications This is dangerous as you will see A value of 1 restricts pulling usernames Some configurations are set to value “0” by default
[slide26.xml] Dangers With a NetBIOS connection Others can get a full dump of: Usernames, groups, shares, permission, policies, services…  Names up to 16 character length This can be performed by using the following command Example: Connect to a hidden share (IPC$) @ 10.10.10.20  net use \\10.10.10.20\IPC$ “” /u:“” Use  NetView  tool for: List computers of that domain List of shares available nbtstat A windows command-line tool that can be used to display NetBIOS connections Example:  nbtstat  -A [IP  Addr ]
[slide27.xml] Windows and Linux We can get account names locally or remotely Windows Pull via Null SMB sessions Windows Remote Access Use wmic:  wmic /node: 192.168.1.125  computersystem  get username For remote machines, you will need the administrator username/password Linux List all accounts:  cat / etc /passwd  or getent  passwd Who is logged in:  who ,  w , or  finger Linux Remote Access Could try  finger @[IP  addr ] , but it is usually disabled We could also check with  ldap  search ldapsearch  opens a connection to an  ldap  server, binds, and performs a search string IAW parameters 
[slide28.xml] Enumeration Tool user2sid/sid2user Written by Evgenii  Rudnyi Command-line tools to lookup NT family SIDs from username input (vice versa) SID is the Security Identifier Variable length numeric value Used for troubleshooting issues with security audits Possible identifier authority values 0: Null	 4: Non-unique 1: World	 5: NT Authority 2: Local	 9: Resource Manager 3: Creator Prebuilt Windows Account SIDs: 500, Original Admin; 501, Guest Examples: 	user2sid \\10.10.10.20 “domain users”  	sid2user \\10.10.10.20 S-1-5-21-3623811015-3361044348-30300820-500
[slide29.xml] Examples Enumerate Users Establish a null session net use \\[IP  addr ] Determine hostname nslookup  [IP  addr ]  OR  ping –a [IP  addr ] Run user2sid against the target user2sid \\[IP  addr ] [domain] The result will give you the SID you need Determine the administrator name sid2user \\[IP  addr ] [SID] 500 Enumerate users in a command-line loop C:\>  for /L % i  in (1000,1,1020) do @sid2user \\[IP  addr ] [Enter SID]  i % >> users.txt Note: /L 		starts a loop	 % i 		is our variable (we will assign a range for the RID) (1000,1,1020) 	means start at 1000, increment by 1, until 1020 do 		starts what we want to do in the loop (our sid2user) % i 		is where our variable is inserted for RID >> users.txt 	will append each result to the users.txt file
[slide30.xml] Another Tool Enum Developed by Razor Team from  BlindView Has now been acquired by Symantec Used for SMB enumeration Can be used to: Establish NULL sessions Password brute forcing Example: enum  [switches] [target address/name] Extract Groups: enum  -G 10.10.10.20 Enum4linux By  Portullis  Security Similar utility with different options

[slide4.xml] Linux Primer What makes a good Pen Tester? To start, a great system administrator Networking Need to be proficient at the command line Understand how the OS works What else?
[slide5.xml] Linux Primer
[slide6.xml] Command Line Interface (CLI) Basic Linux Commands Add a new user / delete user useradd  /  userdel useradd  -d [ home_dr ] [login] Change password passwd Users and Groups Users identified by  user identification  (UIDs) Each UID is associated with an integer (range 0 – 4,294,967,295) Users UID=0 are given superuser privileges Users are placed in groups,  ID’ed  by group identifications (GIDs) Each GID is associated with an integer (range same as UID) Let the system assign UID to avoid duplication Use  id  to display your user and group information uid =500(bill)  gid =500(bill) groups=500(bill),3(sys),4( adm ) Use  groups  command to display the groups 	bill   sys    adm su  < accountname > 	       switch user account (will be prompted for a password)
[slide7.xml] Command Line Interface (CLI) What is the difference between the # and $ prompt? $ prompt == normal user # prompt == root user Besides id and groups, you can search for users using the getent command: getent passwd getent group
[slide8.xml] Accounts Work as a non-root user Unless necessary (most of our tools require root) Use the command  su – [login_name]  then password Kali’s default user is root, so there is not need to use  sudo However, it is good practice to use  sudo  when running commands that require it Linux VTC Virtual Terminal Control User  Alt+F1  and  Alt+F2, etc. Switches you between terminals
[slide9.xml] File System Basics
[slide10.xml] A More Detailed Look
[slide11.xml] Standard Linux Commands Standard Format    -   <command name>  <options/flags>  <arguments>    -  Example:    ls -la  /home Redirecting Output ls -la /home > out.txt             (>  writed  to a file) ls -la /home >> out.txt           (>> append to a file) Redirecting Input wc  < essay.txt Pipes ps  aux | grep  Mctray  |  wc  -l    - List processes with the aux switch    - Grep (search) for  Mctray    - Pipe the output to word count ( wc ) with -l (lines) to standard output (console)
[slide12.xml] Standard Linux Commands Common Commands pwd 			Print working directory cd < dir > 			Change directory ls	 			List files cp <from> <to> 		Copy from the ‘from’ file to the ‘to’ file mv <from> <to> 		Move/rename from the ‘from’ file to the ‘to’ file mkdir  <name> 		Make a directory named <name> rmdir  <name> 		Remove and empty directory who 			List who is currently logged into the system whoami 			Report what user you are logged on as ps  aux 			List ALL your processes running on the system echo 			Echo a string to the terminal find 			Searches a file hierarchy specified by path that match 				a given criteria grep 			Searches files for 1 or more patterns kill <PID> 			Sends a signal to a process/can only kill your own process unless you are root top 			System usage statistics man <command name> 	Manual page with information about the command
[slide13.xml] Directories The command  ls  is used for displaying directories and file details - Using  ls  by itself shows only files   - Using the  -l  shows permissions, links, etc.   - Using  -a  shows all files   - Also use color coding       - Colors are set in  / etc /DIR_COLORS - Black or white = plain files       - Blue = directories       - Green = executables       - Red = compressed files       - Note: The colors depend on the Linux distro       - In some version Blue = exec and Green =  dir - Making directories     - Need to use  mkdir command          - Example:                   >>  mkdir test_directory                   >>  ls   Will this show the directory just created?
[slide14.xml] More Commands - Copy Files   - Use the cp command     - Command: 	 cp [source file] [destination file]     - Example: 	 cp / mnt / cdrom /notes.txt / tmp /IAE410-notes.txt - Where is the file?   - Use the find command ( Ctril -Z will stop)      - Command: 	 find [directory] [search criteria]      - Example: 	 find / -name IAE410_Notes - The locate command can be used as well  - Command: 	 locate [name]      - You may have to  update the database with  updatedb - Great tool without using a lot of resources, but won’t find it if its not in the  db
[slide15.xml] File Editing/Viewing - Can use a variety of different tools for editing, such as:      -  gedit , vi,  pico ,  mcedit  …     -  gedit  is probably the easiest to use for beginners  - View your file using the cat command     - Example:  cat /home/user/draft-file.txt  - Less is more     - If the output is larger than the size of the screen, use less (allows for scrolling)  - More allows viewing of 1 screen at a time     - Example:  less notes     OR    ls / etc  | less     - Example:  more notes   OR   ls / etc  | more
[slide16.xml] $PATH - What is the difference in absolute and relative referencing?    - Example:       -  cd /home/bill/Desktop/exam.txt       -  cd bill/Desktop/exam.txt       - Which is relative and which is absolute? #  echo $PATH  will view your path   - “ echo ” means to “type the following”   -  $  means it’s a variable (not a string)   - If you run a program and it is not in your path…     - It will tell you it can’t find the program - Use the  which  command to see where your commands are being run from 
[slide17.xml] $PATH - Add a directory to your path   - PATH=$PATH:/home/410-exploit-tools   - This is temporary, permanent changes require editing your profile   - The profile is located in  ~/. bashrc and  ~/. bash_profile   -  ~/. bashrc      - This file runs every time you open a new non-login bash shell such as  xterm   -   ~/. bash_profile      - This file runs only with login shells, e.g. - when you first log in into system   -  / etc /profile      - This file is systemwide initialization file, executed for login shells.        - You can edit this file (as root) using: vi / etc /profile
[slide18.xml] Permissions Excellent Resource:  http://permissions-calculator.org/
[slide19.xml] Permissions Execute			(--x)	=> 1 Write				(-w-)	=> 2 Write/Execute			(- wx )	=> 3 Read 				(r--)	=> 4 Read/Execute 			(r-x)	=> 5 Read/Write 			( rw -)	=> 6 Read/Write/Execute		( rwx )	=> 7 What about the following?    - rwxr - xr -x  == ??? drw -r--r--   == ???
[slide20.xml] tar, rpm, make Untarring  Linux Files   - Will end with  .tar   -  Untar  by:  tar  xvf  [tar file]  (tape archive)   - If file ends with  .tar.gz  or  . tgz     - Means it is a compressed tape archive     -  Untar  by:  tar  xvfz  [file.tar.gz] RedHat Package Manager (RPM)   - Can be painful but checks for all dependencies     - Ensures your environment can support the package   - Example install:     -  rpm - i  [ package.rpm ]   - Example uninstall:     -  rpm -e [ package.rpm ]
[slide21.xml] tar, rpm, make Sometimes tools are not pre-compiled   - Some tools come with a script to configure the system	      - The make command compiles      - The make install will install all of the components      - Note: some tools  fo  not have a configure script, so you just compile it      - Example: #   ./configure           #   make           #   make install
[slide22.xml] grep grep  searches items that match a stated condition   - Example: Search for a process named  ksmd     - Command:  ps  aux | grep  ksmd   - Example: Search port 36431 for anything listening     - Command:  netstat –nap | grep 36431
[slide23.xml] What’s running? Use the  ps command to show running processes   - Important switches:  -aux     - Get all processes (even other users), find unusual       processes, and gather root (UID 0) privileges   - Shows: user, PID, CPU, memory utilization, start time, running time,       command line invocation   - Example:  ps  -aux | less Can use  top  to show the most CPU intensive processes running   - With  -p [id]  you can monitor specific processes   - Running  -q  will run with the highest priority
[slide24.xml] Job Control Use  lsof if you need more info on a process   - Shows all files and ports used by processes   - Example:  lsof  -p 1621 Using the ampersand “ & ” we can make a program run in the background   - Example:  find / -name attack-notes &   - We can run multiple jobs in the background     - Each are assigned a job number   - Pull a job to the foreground by using the  fg  command       - Example:  fg  2     - Using just  fg will pull the default (job 1)
[slide25.xml] Important Files
[slide26.xml] What are they looking for? Unusual File Detection   - Find unusually large files (larger than 10MB) Need baseline knowledge 1 st find / -size +110000k -print   - Find files named with dots and spaces find / -name “ “ -print    - Find unusual SUID root files Need to know normal SUID files in order to determine   find / - uid  0 –perm -4000 -print    - Unusual scheduled tasks Find jobs scheduled by root or UID 0 accounts   crontab -u root -l
[slide27.xml] What are they looking for? Unusual Network Usage   - Use  ip  link to look for promiscuous mode for sniffers ip  link | grep PROMISC   - Find details on processes listening on ports lsof  -l   - Find port listeners with  netstat Use -n for numerical addresses ,  -a for all non/listening ,  -p to show PID and name   Example: netstat -nap
[slide28.xml] Getting Caught Finding Unusual Accounts   - Look for UID 0 accounts Example:  egrep  ‘:0+:’ / etc /passwd Example: getent  passwd |  egrep  ‘:0+:’       -  This is with systems that use multiple authentication methods       - Pulls from an administrative database   - Find orphaned files These are remnants of a temp account that was created and then deleted Example: find / - nouser  –print   - Find new accounts Sort numerically by 3 rd  field and tab separate the passwd file Example: sort –nk3 –t: / etc /passwd | less
[slide29.xml] Backtrack Backtrack   - Predecessor to Kali    - A suite of pen testing and vulnerability assessment tools built on a Linux OS (Ubuntu-based)   - Top rated free pen testing tool   - Contains over 300+ tools   - Named after the term  Backtracking A general algorithm for finding all solutions to a problem   - Can be ran as: Bootable Live DVD Installed USB boot VMWARE, and more …  - Uses two UI Configurations Gnome desktop interface (Beginners) KDE desktop interface (Advanced users) K Desktop Environment: Windowing manager and GUI for Unix OS Uses Qt programming language (cross-platform app framework to dev app software with a GUI Uses standard C++ with a special code generator (Meta Object Compiler or MOC)  - Originated from a merge of 2 competing distro’s both based on  Knoppix  focused on pen testing  Auditor Security Collection by Max Moser WHAX by Mati  Aharoni
[slide30.xml] Kali
[slide31.xml] Kali Provided  by  Offensive Security Why is  it  named Kali? Hindu  goddess  associated  with empowerment Kali  comes  from  kala  which  means  black,  time,  and death Kali  is  the  goddess  of  Time  and Change
[slide32.xml] Kali Menu
[slide33.xml] Don’t Forget to Update apt-get    -  apt-get install  Downloads a package and all of its dependencies (install/upgrade)   -  apt-get remove Removes packages and packages that depend on it   -  apt-get update Should be run before each test event and tested Updates the list of available packages and their versions (does not install or upgrade any packages)  -  apt-get upgrade Installs newer versions of the packages you have installed After an update, the package manager knows about available updates for the software you have installed  Will not change what is installed on the  sytem , only the software versions Does not remove packages  -  apt-get  dist -upgrade Installs/Removes packages as necessary to complete the upgrade
[slide34.xml] Getting Information Finding more information about a command/program   -  man <command/program>   -  <program name>  OR  <program name> -help --help  OR  -h
[slide35.xml] Exploitation
[slide36.xml] Exploitation What does it mean?   - Making a threat become reality   - Taking a discovered vulnerability, then applying code/technique to bypass     certain security measures or causing it to do an authorized action   - Examples: Upload/download or move files/data Change configuration Install certain software Use system as a pivot point Escalation of privilege
[slide37.xml] Is it Necessary? Do we need to run exploits?   - YES, identifying a vulnerability is not enough   - This is where you prove an attacker can bypass security Provides realism    - Not all vulnerabilities are exploitable … but given enough time once can be developed    - Helps in reducing false positives You will still need to report the finding even though it didn’t work    - Exploiting a system may be the only way to get deeper into the network Exploitation Framework    - An environment where exploits can be used to attack a target    - Where new exploits can be developed easily Standardized libraries, re-usable parts, modularized/interchangeable
[slide38.xml] It’s Dangerous Out There! Is running exploits dangerous?   - Some are, while others may be less risky   - Effects that may occur: System crash or its services LSASS injection May cause the system to become unstable and may lead to a crash later Malware that is already present may take advantage of the new opening you created Legal concerns if you are in possession of PII (Credit Card numbers)   - Many exploits can be categorized in levels of risk The RoE may state not to run any “dangerous” exploits
[slide39.xml] Categories Categories fall into 3 core areas  - Local Exploitation An attacker who has limited access on a system but can escalate their privileges to an administrative or system level  - Server-side Exploits An attacker attacks a service that is listening on the network There is no user interaction required for the attack  - Client-side Exploits An attacker directs the attack to the client application that is interaction with the server/service The client app will actively pull data from a system configured to exploit it
[slide40.xml] Local Exploitation Typically used once an attacker gains access to a system with limited privileges Need to have direct access to the system Have the ability to run commands with limited privileges  How the attacker got here Sniffed a password off the network Accessed a default account Guessed a password  - The attacker will run code that: Makes their limited privilege process escalate to a higher-level process rights Cause higher privilege processes run lower privilege processes  - What’s the goal…? Windows: Administrator or Local System Linux: root or UID 0
[slide41.xml] Local Privilege Escalation There are many different LPE attacks   - Attacks focused on the kernel Un-patched kernels could allow the attacker to make calls to the kernel directly with higher privileges   - Directed attacks on higher privileged services Attacks use a limited privilege process to execute applications with high-privileges to make higher process calls on the same system Linux attacks focus on gaining UID 0 privileges via SUID root program scripts  - Race conditions Occurs when separate processes/thread of execution depend on some shared state One process action completes before the intended one
[slide42.xml] LPE Tools Linux Systems   - Enlightenment and Linux Kernel Exploit Package Provides basic framework and exploits for gaining UID 0 Windows Systems   - Metasploit has Meterpreter Priv extension for using getsstem    command Iterates through a series of escalation exploits to gain local SYSTEM privileges
[slide43.xml] Server-side Exploits  This use to be the primary target of attacks   This attack centers on a service listening on the target of a   specified port    -  Remember, in our earlier phases, we discovered the open ports,      conducted fingerprinting, and determined the version   - Now, we identify what vulnerabilities it has   - We can now direct our attack using specific packets sent to the target Filtering and other defensive measure could ultimately block this part either inbound or outbound Windows Server-side Exploits - Examples: IIS, LSASS, VNC, DCOM
[slide44.xml] Client-side Attacks In recent years, client-side exploits have risen   - These exploits wait on a client app to access an attacker      owned source that deploys the exploit   - A user will run a program that starts an outbound connection to another     resource system Now insert attacker – the attacker has injected an exploit that goes back to the user’s system to be activated Primary Client-side Attack Vectors   - Browsers, Java, etc.   - Adobe Reader / Flash Player / Media Players   - Document viewers like Word, Excel, PowerPoint There are positive and negative aspects to these

[slide47.xml] Armitage It’s  a  scriptable  red  teaming  collaboration  tool for Metasploit –	 Basically  helps  you  visualize targets Developed  by  Raphael Mudge http://www.fastandeasyhacking.com
[slide48.xml] Armitage
[slide49.xml] What Does that Mean? Exploit A  program  that  takes  advantage  of  a  specific  vulnerability  and  provides access to  the target Example MS08-067 Payload The  actual  code  that  executes  on  the  target  system  after  an  exploit   successfully  compromised  the  target  (e.g.  reverse  shell,  bind  shell, etc.) Listener A  listener  waits  for  an  incoming  connection  (exploited  target  or attacking system)  and  manages  the  connection when  its made Shell A  console-like  interface  that  provides  access  to  a  remote target Shellcode A  set  of  instructions  that  an  exploit  uses  as  a payload Module A  standalone  prepackaged  piece  of  code  that  extends  the  functionality  of msf .   Can  be  an  exploit,  auxiliary  or  post-exploitation module

[slide52.xml] MSF3 Directory Structure
[slide53.xml] File System and Libraries The MSF filesystem is laid out in an intuitive manner and is organized by directory data: 		editable files used by Metasploit documentation: 	provides documentation for the framework external: 		source code and third-party libraries lib: 		the ‘meat’ of the framework code base modules: 		the actual MSF modules plugins: 		plugins that can be loaded at run-time scripts: 		Meterpreter and other scripts tools: 		various useful command-line utilities  Libraries Rex The basic library for most tasks Handles sockets, protocols, text transformations, and others SSL, SMB, HTTP, XOR, Base65, Unicode Msf ::Core Provides the Metasploit Framework Defines the Metasploit Framework Msf ::Base Provides the ‘friendly’ API Provides simplified APIs for use in the Framework
[slide54.xml] Modules Metasploit, as presented to the user, is composed of modules Exploits Defined as modules that use payloads An exploit without a payload is an Auxiliary module Payloads, Encoders, and NOPs Payloads consist of code that runs remotely Encoders ensure that payloads make it to their destination NOPs keep the payload sizes consistent Modules Locations Primary Module Tree Located under /opt/framework/msf3/modules Provides simplified APIs for use in the Framework Located under ~/.msf3/modules/ This location is ideal for private module sets Loading Additional Trees at Runtime Pass the -m option when running  msfconsole  ( msfconsole  -m) Use the  loadpath  command within  msfconsole
[slide55.xml] Kali and Metasploit Before  Using Metasploit Metasploit Uses   PostgreSQL #	 service  postgresql start Make  sure  port  5432 is   listening Run:	 #	 ss –ant Start  the Metasploit   Service This  will  create  a  msf db called msf3 #  service  metasploit start
[slide56.xml] msfconsole Most popular interface to the MSF Its an “all-in-one” centralized console Allows efficient access to all options available in the Metasploit Framework   msfconsole  interface works on Windows with the 3.3 release Version 3.2 requires manual  installof  the Framework under Cygwin Also requires patching the Ruby installation                start it
[slide57.xml] Basic  msfconsole  Commands
[slide58.xml] What’s the Benefit?  Benefits It is the only supported way to access most of the features within Metasploit Provides a console-based interface to the Framework Contains the most features and is the most stable MSF interface Full  readline  support, tabbing, and command completion Execution of external commands in  msfconsole  is possible:
[slide59.xml] msfconsole  Launching The  msfconsole  is launched by simply running ‘ msfconsole  from the command line Msfconsole  is located in the /opt/framework/msf3 Backtrack – /opt/Metasploit/msf3
[slide60.xml] msfconsole  Help help This  command   provides  a  list  &   short description   of  all  available   commands
[slide61.xml] Make it Easy msfconsole Designed to be fast to use, hence tab completion Has a lot of modules available making it difficult to remember the exact name/path of specific modules As with most other shells, entering what you know and pressing ‘Tab’ will present you with a list of options available to you or auto-complete the string if there is only one options It depends on the Ruby  readline  extension Almost every command in the console is supported

[slide64.xml] TCP Idle Scan Allows  us  to  scan a  target stealthily We  spoof  another  host  IP  address  on  the network First,  need  to locate  an  idle  host  on  the  network Host  needs  to  use  incremental  IP ID Incremental  IP  ID  is  used  to  track  packet order Helps  us  to  predict  the  next  packet  ID number Use  msf’s framework scanner/ ip / ipidseq module  to  locate host msf  > use auxiliary/scanner/ ip / ipidseq msf  auxiliary( ipidseq ) > show options       Module options: Name Current setting Required Description ------- ------------------- ----------- -------------- GWHOST no The  gateway  IP address INTERFACE no The  name  of  the interface LHOST no The  local  IP  address RHOST yes The  target  address  range  or  CIDR identifier RPORT 80 yes The  target port SNAPLEN 65535 yes The  number  of  bytes  to capture THREADS 1 yes The  number  of  concurrent threads TIMEOUT 500 yes The  reply  read  timeout  in milliseconds
[slide65.xml] TCP Idle in Action Name Current setting Required Description ------- ------------------- ----------- -------------- GWHOST no The  gateway  IP address INTERFACE no The  name  of  the interface LHOST no The  local  IP  address RHOST yes The  target  address  range  or  CIDR identifier RPORT 80 yes The  target port SNAPLEN 65535 yes The  number  of  bytes  to capture THREADS 1 yes The  number  of  concurrent threads TIMEOUT 500 yes The  reply  read  timeout  in milliseconds Checking  the  FW  log…  shows  the  recording  of  the  IP  address  of  the  Zombie  host   (SRC=192.168.1.69)  and  not  our  IP address. Good Zombie  Choices

[slide67.xml] ipidseq  setting thread level Good Zombie  Choices Typically  should   not  set “Threads”   above  16  on   Windows  (x86) or   256  on UNIX systems running   Metasploit   (Cygwin  ~200)   Default  = 1 Depends   on    h a rd w are   spec’s * threads:  the  higher  the  number  the  higher network traffic
[slide68.xml] MAC Address Spoofing MAC s poofing  can  help  bypass  firewall  filtering by   MAC address Use  the  ---spoof-mac switch Specify  MAC from vendor: --spoof-mac DELL/Apple/3COM Make  your  own: --spoof-mac 00:01:02:25:56:AD Make  a  random:  --spoof-mac 0 Your  MAC  address  will  not  appear  in  log files msf  >  db_import scan1.xml   msf  >  hosts  –c address Hosts ==== Address -------   192 .1 6 8 .1 0 .1 192.168.10.10 192.168.10.15 192.168.10.20
